On transformation operators and Riesz basis property of root vectors system for n × n n\times n Dirac type operators. Application to the Timoshenko beam model
Anton A. Lunyov, Mark M. Malamud

Abstract
The paper is concerned with the following n × n n\times n Dirac type equation L ​ y = − i ​ B ​ ( x ) − 1 ​ ( y ′ + Q ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ) = λ ​ y , B ⁡ ( x ) = B ​ ( x ) ∗ , y = col ⁡ ( y 1 , … , y n ) , x ∈ [ 0 , ℓ ] , Ly=-iB(x)^{-1}\bigl(y^{\prime}+Q(x)y\bigr)=\lambda y,\quad B(x)=B(x)^{*},\quad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell], on a finite interval [ 0 , ℓ ] [0,\ell] . Here Q ∈ L 1 ​ ( [ 0 , ℓ ] , ℂ n × n ) Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}) is a potential matrix and B ∈ L ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , ℓ ] , ℝ n × n ) B\in L^{\infty}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}) is an invertible self-adjoint diagonal “weight” matrix. If n = 2 ​ m n=2m and B ⁡ ( x ) = diag ⁡ ( − I m , I m ) B(x)=\diag(-I_{m},I_{m}) this equation is equivalent to Dirac equation of order n n . We show the existence of triangular transformation operators for such equation under additional uniform separation conditions on the entries of the matrix function B B . Here we apply this result to study direct spectral properties of the boundary value problem (BVP) associated with the above equation subject to the general boundary conditions U ⁡ ( y ) = C ​ y ​ ( 0 ) + D ​ y ​ ( ℓ ) = 0 U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0 , rank ⁡ ( C ​ D ) = n \rank(C\ D)=n . As a first application of this result, we show that the deviation of the characteristic determinants of this BVP and the unperturbed BVP (with Q = 0 Q=0 ) is a Fourier transform of some summable function explicitly expressed via kernels of the transformation operators. In turn, this representation yields asymptotic behavior of the spectrum in the case of regular boundary conditions. Namely, λ m = λ m 0 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) \lambda_{m}=\lambda_{m}^{0}+o(1) as m → ∞ m\to\infty , where { λ m } m ∈ ℤ \{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}} and { λ m 0 } m ∈ ℤ \{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}} are sequences of eigenvalues of perturbed and unperturbed ( Q = 0 Q=0 ) BVP, respectively. Further, we prove that the system of root vectors of the above BVP constitutes a Riesz basis in a certain weighted L 2 L^{2} -space, provided that the boundary conditions are strictly regular . Along the way, we also establish completeness, uniform minimality and asymptotic behavior of root vectors. The main results are applied to establish asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, and the Riesz basis property for the dynamic generator of spatially non-homogenous damped Timoshenko beam model. We also found a new case when eigenvalues have an explicit asymptotic, which to the best of our knowledge is new even in the case of constant parameters of the model.



MSC
47E05 , 34L40 , 34L10 , 35L35

1
Introduction

In this paper we continue our investigation
[
32
]
,
[
33
]
of the spectral properties
of non-self-adjoint boundary value problems (BVP) for the following
first order system of ordinary differential equations (ODE):

\mathcal{L}(Q)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}\bigl(y^{\prime}+Q(x)y\bigr)=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(1.1)

subject to the following boundary conditions with
n\times n
matrices
C,D\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
:

U(y):=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\rank(C\ D)=n.

(1.2)

Here

B=\diag(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}),\qquad\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(1.3)

is a self-adjoint invertible diagonal summable matrix function, and

Q=(Q_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n},\qquad Q_{jk}\in L^{1}[0,\ell]:=L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}),\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(1.4)

is a summable (generally non-self-adjoint) potential matrix.

Next we associate with the BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
an operator
L_{U}(Q)
. To this end we denote by
\mathfrak{H}_{k}:=L^{2}_{|\beta_{k}|}[0,\ell]
the weighted
L^{2}
-space
with the weight
|\beta_{k}|
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, and set

\mathfrak{H}:=\mathfrak{H}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathfrak{H}_{n}.
Now the operator
L_{U}(Q)
in
\mathfrak{H}
is defined
as a restriction of the maximal operator
L_{\max}(Q)
generated in
\mathfrak{H}
by the differential expression
\mathcal{L}(Q)
to the domain

\dom(L_{U}(Q)):=\{y\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})\ :\ \mathcal{L}(Q)y\in\mathfrak{H},\ \ \ U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0\}.

(1.5)

Systems (
1.1
) are of significant interest in some
theoretical and practical questions. For instance, if
n=2m
,

B(x)=\diag(-I_{m},I_{m})
,
Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&Q_{12}\\
Q_{21}&0\end{pmatrix}
,
system (
1.1
) is equivalent to
the Dirac system (see
[
29
]
,
[
44
, Section 1.2]
). Note also that
equation (
1.1
) with arbitrary
constant not necessary self-adjoint matrix
B(x)=\diag(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

is used to integrate the
N
-waves problem arising in nonlinear
optics
[
54
, Sec.III.4]
.

The spectral problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) (the operator
L_{U}(Q)
) has first been investigated by G.D. Birkhoff and R.E. Langer
[
7
]
. Namely, they have extended some previous results of Birkhoff and Tamarkin on non-self-adjoint boundary value problem for ODE to the case of BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
). More precisely,
they introduced the concepts of
regular and strictly
regular boundary conditions
(
1.2
) and
investigated the asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and
eigenfunctions of the corresponding operator
L_{U}(Q)
.
Moreover, they proved
a pointwise convergence result
on
spectral decompositions of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
corresponding
to the BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with
regular boundary conditions.

To the best of our knowledge, the problem of the completeness of the system of root vectors

of general BVP
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
with arbitrary
constant not necessary self-adjoint matrix

B(x)=\diag(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,
was first investigated in the recent papers
[
42
,
43
]
by
one of the authors and L.L. Oridoroga. In these papers the
concept of
weakly regular
boundary conditions for the
system (
1.1
) was introduced and the completeness of
root vectors for this class of BVP was proved.
Completeness property for general
BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with non-weakly
regular and even with degenerate boundary conditions was obtained in
[
32
]
.

Emphasize that in the case of
non-weakly regular boundary conditions the completeness property
substantially depends on the values
Q(0)
and
Q(1)
.

Going over to the basis property note that
during the last decade there appeared numerous papers devoted mainly to the
Riesz basis property for
2\times 2
Dirac system subject to the

regular or strictly regular
boundary conditions
(see
[
69
,
49
,
50
,
20
,
6
,
8
,
11
,
10
,
12
,
9
,
13
]
).
The most complete result on the Riesz basis property for
2\times 2
Dirac and Dirac-type systems with
Q\in L^{1}
and strictly regular boundary conditions was obtained independently by different methods and at the same time by A.M. Savchuk and A.A. Shkalikov
[
60
]
and by the authors
[
31
,
33
]
. The case of regular boundary conditions is treated in
[
60
]
for the first time. Other proofs were obtained later in
[
57
]
,
[
58
]
(see also their recent survey
[
59
]
and references therein).

To the best of our knowledge the first result on the Riesz basis property for
BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) generated by general
n\times n
system (
1.1
)
with
B(x)=B=\diag(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\not=B^{*}

and bounded
Q\in L^{\infty}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})

was obtained by the authors in
[
32
]
. Treated boundary conditions form rather broad class that covers, in particular, periodic, antiperiodic, and regular separated (not necessarily self-adjoint) boundary conditions.
Note also that BVP for
2m\times 2m
Dirac equation (
B=\diag(-I_{m},I_{m})
) was investigated in
[
51
]
(Bari-Markus property for Dirichlet BVP with
Q\in L^{2}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2m\times 2m})
and in
[
24
,
25
]
(Bessel and Riesz basis properties on abstract level).

Note also that
periodic and antiperiodic (necessarily non-strictly regular) BVP
for
2\times 2
Dirac and Sturm-Liouville equations have also attracted certain attention during the last decade. For instance, in
[
10
, Theorem 13]
,
[
9
, Theorem 19]
and
[
13
]
, it is established a
criterion
for the system of root vectors to contain a Riesz basis for periodic (resp., antiperiodic)
2\times 2
Dirac operator in terms of the Fourier coefficients of
Q
as well as in terms of periodic (resp., antiperiodic) and Dirichlet spectra.
It is also worth mentioning that F. Gesztesy and V.A. Tkachenko
[
16
,
17
]
for
q\in L^{2}[0,\pi]
and P. Djakov and B.S. Mityagin
[
9
]
for
q\in W^{-1,2}[0,\pi]
established by different methods a
criterion
for the system of root vectors to contain a Riesz basis for Sturm-Liouville operator
-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+q(x)
on
[0,\pi]
. See also recent survey
[
14
]
by P. Djakov and B. Mityagin, surveys
[
34
,
38
]
by A.S. Makin, and the references therein.

Note in conclusion, that the Riesz basis property for abstract operators is investigated in numerous papers. Let us mention
[
21
,
46
,
47
,
1
,
62
,
4
,
5
]
, the recent survey by A.A. Shkalikov
[
63
]
, and the references therein.

Let us formulate our main results. To this end we need to impose certain conditions on the entries of the matrix function
B(\cdot)
. We assume that there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
and
n_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, such that

\displaystyle-\infty<-\theta^{-1}<\beta_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n_{-}}(x)<-\theta<0,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(1.6)

\displaystyle 0<\theta<\beta_{n_{-}+1}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n}(x)<\theta^{-1}<\infty,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(1.7)

and for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n-1}\}

\text{either}\quad\beta_{k}\equiv\beta_{k+1}\quad\text{or}\quad\beta_{k}(x)+\theta<\beta_{k+1}(x),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(1.8)

Let us recall the definition of
regular boundary conditions
(see
[
7
, p.89]
)
confining ourselves to the case of
B(x)=B(x)^{*}
. Set

S:=\diag(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}),\qquad s_{k}:=\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot)),\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(1.9)

Conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) guarantee that, in fact,
S=\diag(-I_{n_{-}},I_{n-n_{-}})
. Denote by
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
the spectral projections in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
onto “positive” and “negative” parts of the spectrum of
S=S^{*}
, respectively.
Now the concept of regularity of boundary conditions for the problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) reads as follows:

\det(CP_{+}+\ DP_{-})\neq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\det(CP_{-}+\ DP_{+})\neq 0.

(1.10)

Additionally, boundary conditions (
1.2
) are called strictly regular if eigenvalues of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
are asymptotically separated (see Definition
5.13
for details).

Our first main result establishes existence of triangular transformation operators for equation (
1.1
) with

Q\in L^{1}
and
non-constant
self-adjoint
n\times n
matrix function
B(\cdot)
, satisfying conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) (see Theorem
3.7
). Namely, assuming for simplicity that
\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}
let
A=\col(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
have non-zero entries. Then we show that the vector solution
y_{A}(\cdot,\lambda)
of the equation (
1.1
) subject to the initial condition
y_{A}(0,\lambda)=A
admits a triangular representation

y_{A}(x,\lambda)=(I+\mathcal{K}_{A})e_{A}(x,\lambda)=e_{A}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt,

(1.11)

where

e_{A}(x,\lambda)=\col(a_{1}e^{i\lambda\rho_{1}(x)},\ldots,a_{n}e^{i\lambda\rho_{n}(x)}),\qquad\rho_{k}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{k}(t)\,dt,

is the solution to the unperturbed equation
\mathcal{L}(0)f=\lambda f
subject to the same initial condition.

This result is substantial advancement comparing to the corresponding result by one of the authors in
[
41
]
, where this was established for

Q\in L^{\infty}
and
constant
self-adjoint
n\times n
matrix
B=B^{*}
, and the corresponding result by the authors in
[
33
]
, where this was established for

Q\in L^{1}
and
constant
self-adjoint
2\times 2
matrix
B=\diag(b_{1},b_{2})=B^{*}
. This result has a wide area of applications. Here we apply triangular transformation operators only to investigation
of the spectral properties of BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
(operator
L_{U}(Q)
).

As an immediate application of triangular transformation operators, we obtain formulas relating the fundamental matrix solutions
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
and
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
to equation (
1.1
) with
Q\not=0
and
Q=0
, respectively (see Proposition
4.2
and formula (
4.29
)).
In fact, it leads to a representation of the deviation
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)-\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
as a Fourier transform of linear combinations of several transformation operators kernels (see formula (
4.45
)).

In turn, starting with this Fourier representation of
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)-\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)

we establish an important identity relating characteristic determinants
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
and
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
of the operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}(0)
(see (
5.11
)–(
5.13
) for exact definitions). Namely, letting

b_{-}:=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n_{-}}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{+}:=b_{n_{-}+1}+\ldots+b_{n},\quad\text{where}\quad b_{k}:=\rho_{k}(\ell)=\int_{0}^{\ell}\beta_{k}(x)\,dx.

(1.12)

we show that the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
admits the following representation:

\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=\Delta_{0}(\lambda)+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du\qquad\text{with}\qquad g\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(1.13)

It is worth mentioning that the second key ingredient in the proof of formula (
1.13
) is an extension of the classical Liouville formula for the determinant of a fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
to the case of its
m
-th exterior powers
\bigwedge^{m}\Phi(x,\lambda)
obtained in Proposition
4.7
.

For a special case of
2\times 2
Dirac type operator (
constant

B\equiv\diag(b_{1},b_{2})=B^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
) representation (
1.13
) was earlier established in
[
31
]
,
[
33
, Lemma 4.1]
. In recent papers by A. Makin
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
this representation was applied to establish Riesz basis property of periodic BVP (regular but not strictly regular) for
2\times 2
Dirac equation under certain explicit algebraic assumptions on a potential matrix.

Formula (
1.13
) gives a bridge between the spectral theory of the operator
L_{U}(Q)

and the theory of entire functions of exponential type due to the simple fact: the spectrum
\sigma(L_{U}(Q))=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

of
L_{U}(Q)
coincides with the set of zeros (counting multiplicity) of the entire function
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
.
Assuming
boundary conditions to be regular
we
easily obtain from (
1.13
) that
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
is an entire sine-type function of
the same types
b_{\pm}
in
\mathbb{C}_{\mp}
as the determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
.
Further, following the schema of the proof of
[
33
, Proposition 4.7]
,
we extract the following asymptotic formula from representation (
1.13
)

\lambda_{m}=\lambda_{m}^{0}+o(1)\qquad\text{as}\qquad m\to\infty,

(1.14)

relating the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
and eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
. Note, that for
2\times 2
Dirac equation formula (
1.14
) was first established in
[
31
]
and
[
60
]
independently and by different methods.

Moreover,
assuming boundary conditions to be strictly regular

we complete formula (
1.14
) by establishing similar formula for the normalized eigenvectors
f_{m}(\cdot)
and
f_{m}^{0}(\cdot)
of the operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}(0)
, respectively. Namely,
using formula relating
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
and
\Phi^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
as well as a simple abstract formula for
simple eigenvectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
, we
establish the following formula for their deviation which is valid
uniformly in
x\in[0,\ell]
:

f_{m}(x)=f_{m}^{0}(x)+o(1)\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z},

(1.15)

In turn, this relationship and formula (
1.14
) are substantially involved in the proof of the Riesz basis property of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
provided that boundary conditions are
strictly regular
.
This proved to be challenging even on the algebraic level (the case of
Q=0
) and required establishing a new algebraic identity (see Proposition
8.5
) for the inner product of the eigenvectors of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
and its adjoint
L_{U}^{*}(0)
.

It is worth mentioning that in Section
5.4
we find necessary and sufficient conditions for quasi-periodic boundary conditions

y_{k}(\ell)=c_{k}y_{k}(0),\qquad c_{k}\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(1.16)

to be strictly regular. In this case conditions (
1.2
) hold with invertible

C=\diag(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
and
D=-I_{n}
. In accordance with (
1.10
), conditions (
1.16
) are always regular but not necessary strictly regular.
Morover,
antiperiodic
boundary conditions
(c_{1}=\ldots=c_{n}=-1)
are strictly regular if and only if
for some
b_{0}>0
the numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
given by (
1.12
)
can be ordered in such a way that the following representation holds,

b_{k}=2^{a_{k}}(2u_{k}+1)b_{0},\qquad a_{k},u_{k}\in\mathbb{Z},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\qquad 0\leqslant a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<a_{n}.

(1.17)

In particular, antiperiodic boundary conditions are strictly regular if

b_{k}=2^{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
.

We also obtain completeness property in the case of regular boundary conditions extending the corresponding
result from
[
43
]
to the case of non-constant matrix function
B(\cdot)\not=\const
.
In Section
9
we also establish the Riesz basis property with parentheses for the operator
L_{U}(Q)
provided that boundary conditions (
1.2
) are only regular (but not strictly regular). For the proof we use the perturbation idea which goes back to A.A. Shkalikov
[
61
]
and was applied later on to
2\times 2
Dirac systems in
[
60
,
33
]
.

Finally, we apply our main abstract results with
B(x)=B(x)^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{4\times 4}
to the Timoshenko beam model investigated
under different restrictions in numerous papers
(see
[
67
,
23
,
48
,
65
,
72
,
71
,
70
,
66
,
32
,
33
,
2
]
and the references therein). In our previous papers
[
32
,
33
]
we studied the Timoshenko beam model with relaxed smoothness assumptions on the coefficients, when the beam is fixed at one end and with the most general boundary condition at the other end. For this general model, we established completeness and Riesz basis property with parentheses, assuming certain identity for the coefficients of the model: the ratio of wave speeds
\frac{K(\cdot)}{\rho(\cdot)}
and
\frac{EI(\cdot)}{I_{\rho}(\cdot)}
is constant.
This assumption has to be added because in our previous papers
[
32
,
33
]
we treated BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with
a
constant matrix

B(x)=B
. However, the dynamic generator of the general Timoshenko beam model is similar to the operator
L_{U}(Q)
with
B(x)=(-\beta_{1}(x),\beta_{1}(x),-\beta_{2}(x),\beta_{2}(x))
and
functions
\beta_{1}(x),\beta_{2}(x)
with non-constant ratio
, and cannot be reduced to Dirac-type operator with a constant matrix
B
.
Since in this paper, we treat more general BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with arbitrary non-constant matrix
B(\cdot)
, this allows us to remove this algebraic assumption.
Moreover, we establish asymptotic behavior of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the dynamic generator
L_{\Tim}
of the Timoshenko beam model as well as the Riesz basis property (without parentheses) of the root vectors system of the operator
L_{\Tim}
, provided that the eigenvalues of
L_{\Tim}
are asymptotically separated. We also provide comprehensive list of explicit conditions that guarantee this property.

When our preprint was almost ready we became aware of the short communication
[
64
]
by A.A. Shkalikov, where results on Riesz basis property of regular BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with
Q\in L^{1}
were announced under the similar assumptions on matrix function
B(\cdot)
.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
is devoted to some preliminaries. In particular, we list
some identities of determinants of sums and products of matrices.

In Section
3
we prove our first main result, Theorem
3.7
, establishing existence of triangular transformation operators for equation (
1.1
).
The case of
non-constant
matrix function
B(\cdot)
poses significant difficulties even for
Q\in C^{1}
.

In Section
4
we apply transformation operators
to establish an important identity for the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
of the equation (
1.1
). Namely, in Proposition
4.2
we show that the deviation of the fundamental matrices of equation (
1.1
) with
Q\neq 0
and
Q=0
admits a Fourier transform representation involving the kernels of the transformation operators.
In Proposition
4.7
we generalize a classical Liouville’s formula and show that
m
-th exterior power
\bigwedge^{m}\Phi(x,\lambda)

of the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)

satisfies equation similar to (
1.1
). This result, in turn, implies similar Fourier transform representation for the minors of
\Phi(x,\lambda)
.

In Section
5
we establish some general properties of BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) and introduce concepts of regular and strictly regular boundary conditions. In particular, we apply Jacobi’s formula to establish certain important uniform estimates from below for eigenvectors of this BVP with
Q=0
, provided that boundary conditions are strictly regular (see Proposition
5.16
).

In Section
6
we establish key identity (
1.13
)
and similar identity related to eigenvectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. These identities are used to establish the asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenvectors in Theorems
6.6
and
6.9
, respectively.

In Section
7
, following
[
43
]
, we establish completeness of the root vectors system of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
(see Subsection
2.1
for exact definition), provided that
Q\in L^{1}
, matrix function
B(\cdot)
meats conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.8
), and boundary conditions (
1.2
) are regular.

In Section
8
, we study adjoint operator
L_{U}(Q)^{*}
. In Proposition
8.5
we establish an important identity
for the inner product of eigenvectors of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
and its adjoint
L_{U}^{*}(0)
, which is essential for proving uniform minimality property.

In Section
9
, we prove our main results on uniform minimality and Riesz basis property of the root vectors system of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
with strictly regular boundary conditions (see Theorems
9.4
and
9.11
).
Here we also establish Riesz basis property with parentheses provided that boundary conditions (
1.2
) are regular (see Theorem
9.20
).

In Section
10
we apply our abstract results with
B(x)=B(x)^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{4\times 4}
to the dynamic generator
L_{\Tim}
of the general model (
10.1
)–(
10.5
) of spatially non-homogenous Timoshenko beam with both boundary and locally distributed damping. By reducing this dynamic generator
L_{\Tim}
to the special
4\times 4
Dirac type operator
L_{U}(Q)
, we show that the root vectors system of
L_{\Tim}
forms a Riesz basis in the suitable energy space, when the corresponding operator
L_{U}(Q)
is equipped with the strictly regular boundary conditions (see Theorems (
10.7
) and (
10.8
)(ii)). We also apply results of Section
6
to establish the asymptotic behavior of the eigenvalues and the eigenvectors of the operator
L_{\Tim}
(see Theorems
10.6
and
10.8
(i)). In particular, we found an interesting case when eigenvalues of
L_{\Tim}
have an explicit asymptotical formula, which to the best of our knowledge is new even in the case of constant parameters of the model (see Theorem
10.6
(iii)).

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Definition of the system of root vectors

Let us also recall the notion of the system of root vectors of an operator with compact resolvent. First, we recall a few basic facts regarding the eigenvalues of a compact, linear operator
T\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{H})
in a
separable complex Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
. The
geometric
multiplicity
,
m_{g}(\mu_{0},T)
, of an eigenvalue
\mu_{0}\in\sigma_{p}(T)
of
T
is given by

m_{g}(\mu_{0},T):=\dim(\ker(T-\mu_{0})).

The
root subspace
of
T
corresponding to
\mu_{0}\in\sigma_{p}(T)
is given by

\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{0}}(T)=\big\{f\in\mathfrak{H}\,:\,(T-\mu_{0})^{k}f=0\ \ \text{for some}\ \ k\in\mathbb{N}\big\}.

(2.1)

Elements of
\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{0}}(T)
are called
root vectors
.
For
\mu_{0}\in\sigma_{p}(T)\backslash\{0\}
, the set

\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{0}}(T)
is a closed linear subspace of

\mathfrak{H}
whose dimension equals to the
algebraic
multiplicity
,
m_{a}(\mu_{0},T)
, of
\mu_{0}
,

m_{a}(\mu_{0},T):=\dim\big(\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{0}}(T)\big)<\infty.

Denote by
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
the sequence of non-zero
eigenvalues of
T
(
\mu_{j}\neq\mu_{k}
) and let
m_{j}
be the algebraic multiplicity
of
\mu_{j}
. By the
system of root vectors
of the operator

T
we mean any sequence of the form

\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}\{e_{jk}\}_{k=1}^{m_{j}},

where
\{e_{jk}\}_{k=1}^{m_{j}}
is a basis in
\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{j}}(T)
,

m_{j}=m_{a}(\mu_{j},T)<\infty
.
The system or root vectors of the operator
T
is called
normalized
if
\|e_{jk}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{m_{j}}\}
.

We are particularly interested in the case where
A
is a
densely defined, closed, linear operator in
\mathfrak{H}
whose
resolvent is compact, that is,

R_{A}(\lambda):=(A-\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{H}),\ \lambda\in\rho(A).

Via the spectral mapping theorem all eigenvalues of
A

correspond to eigenvalues of its resolvent
R_{A}(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, and vice versa. Hence, we use the same notions of
root vectors, root subspaces, geometric and algebraic
multiplicities associated with the eigenvalues of
A
, and the
system of root vectors of
A
.

2.2
Properties of Lipshitz functions

Recall that
\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
for
\mathcal{S}\subset X
in any normed space
X
is the class of functions
f
acting from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{C}
and satisfying the condition

|f(u)-f(u^{\prime})|\leqslant\alpha\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{X},\quad u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\quad\text{for some}\quad\alpha=\alpha_{f}>0.

It is well known that for any finite segment
[a,b]\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Lip_{1}[a,b]=\{f\in\AC[a,b]:f^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}[a,b]\}=W^{1,\infty}[a,b].

(2.2)

We also denote by
L_{1,\loc}(\mathcal{S})
a set of functions
f
that are Lipshitz on any compact subset of
\mathcal{S}
.

Our main target use case will be
X=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. To this end we denote by

|u-u^{\prime}|:=\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|,\qquad u=(x,t),\ u^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

a Manhattan distance between points
u
and
u^{\prime}
.
Further, a simply connected, closed bounded set
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is said to have a Lipshitz boundary if its boundary
\partial S
can be parametrized as

\partial S=\{(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t)):t\in[a,b]\},

(2.3)

for some
-\infty<a<b<\infty
where
\gamma_{j}\in\Lip_{1}[a,b]
and
\gamma_{j}(a)=\gamma_{j}(b)
,
j\in\{1,2\}
.
Throughout the paper we will denote for
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
,

(D_{1}f)(x,t):=D_{1}f(x,t):=\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}f(x,t),\qquad(D_{2}f)(x,t):=D_{2}f(x,t):=\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}f(x,t),

(2.4)

whenever corresponding partial derivatives exist.

In the sequel we will need the following simple properties of Lipshitz functions.

Lemma 2.1
.

(i)
Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
f_{m}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, be such that

\displaystyle f_{m}(x,t)\to f(x,t)\ \ \text{as}\ \ m\to\infty,\quad u=(x,t)\in\mathcal{S},

(2.5)

\displaystyle|f_{m}(x,t)-f_{m}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})|\leqslant\alpha(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|),\quad(x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S},\quad m\in\mathbb{N},

(2.6)

for some
\alpha>0
and
f:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{C}
. Then

f\in\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})\qquad\text{and}\qquad|f(x,t)-f(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})|\leqslant\alpha(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|),\quad(x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}.

Moreover,
\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
is a Banach space with the norm

\|f\|_{\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})}:=|f(x_{0},t_{0})|+\sup_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{u, u' \in\cS}{u \ne u'}}\frac{|f(x,t)-f(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})|}{|u-u^{\prime}|},\qquad u=(x,t),\ u^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

where
u_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{S}
is an arbitrary fixed point.

(ii)
Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a simply connected, closed bounded set with a Lipshitz boundary and let
f\in\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
, be such that

\mathcal{D}(f):=\max\{\|D_{1}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})},\|D_{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\}<\infty.

(2.7)

Then

|f(x,t)-f(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})|\leqslant\alpha(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|),\quad(x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S},

where constant
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
depends only on
\mathcal{D}(f)
and the set
\mathcal{S}
.

(iii)
Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a simply connected, closed bounded set with a Lipshitz boundary and let
f_{m}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, be such that

f_{m}(x,t)\to f(x,t)\ \ \text{as}\ \ m\to\infty,\quad(x,t)\in\mathcal{S},\qquad\|D_{1}f_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})},\ \|D_{2}f_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\leqslant\tau,\quad m\in\mathbb{N},

(2.8)

for some
\tau>0
and
f:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{C}
. Then
f\in\Lip_{1}(\mathcal{S})
.

Proof.

Parts (i) and (ii) are well-known and are of folklore nature. Let us only mention that the set
\mathcal{S}
as a simply connected, closed and bounded set with a Lipshitz boundary has the following important property: for any interior points
u,u^{\prime}\in\inter S
there exists a “Manhattan” curve
\Gamma_{u,u^{\prime}}
(a finite sequence of alternating vertical and horizontal segments) connecting
u
and
u^{\prime}
such the length of
\Gamma_{u,u^{\prime}}

is bounded by
C_{\mathcal{S}}|u-u^{\prime}|
for some
C_{\mathcal{S}}
that only depend on
\mathcal{S}
(more precisely it only depends on Lipshitz constants of the parametric curves
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
of the boundary
\partial S
). This property allows to utilize fundamental representation (
2.2
) for Lipshitz space on a finite segment.

Let us also comment on part (iii). Part (ii) and condition (
2.8
) imply uniform Lipshitz condition (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) with some
\alpha
that only depends on
\tau
and the set
\mathcal{S}
. Part (i) finishes the proof.
∎

2.3
The Banach spaces
X_{1}
and
X_{\infty}

Following
[
39
,
33
]
denote by
X_{1}:=X_{1}(\Omega)
and
X_{\infty}:=X_{\infty}(\Omega)
the linear spaces composed of (equivalent classes of) measurable functions defined on

\Omega:=\{(x,t):0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell\}

(2.9)

satisfying

\displaystyle\|f\|_{X_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{t\in[0,\ell]}\int_{t}^{\ell}|f(x,t)|dx<\infty,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{X_{\infty}}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{x\in[0,\ell]}\int_{0}^{x}|f(x,t)|dt<\infty,

(2.11)

respectively. It can easily be shown that the spaces
X_{1}
and

X_{\infty}
equipped with the norms (
2.10
) and
(
2.11
) form Banach spaces that are not
separable. Denote by
X_{1,0}
and
X_{\infty,0}
the subspaces
of
X_{1}
and
X_{\infty}
, respectively, obtained by taking the
closure of continuous functions
f\in C(\Omega)
. Clearly, the
set
C^{1}(\Omega)
of smooth functions is also dense in both
spaces
X_{1,0}
and
X_{\infty,0}
.

The following simple property of the class

X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
established in
[
33
]
will be essential in the sequel.

Lemma 2.2

(Lemma 2.2 in
[
33
]
)
.

For each
a\in[0,\ell]
the trace mapping

i_{a}:\ C(\Omega)\to C[0,a],\qquad(i_{a}R)(t):=R(a,t),\quad R\in C(\Omega),

(2.12)

originally defined on
C(\Omega)
admits a continuous extension (also denoted by
i_{a}
) as a mapping
from
X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
onto
L^{1}[0,a]
.

We will also need a property of intersections
X_{1}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty}(\Omega)
,
X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
established in
[
33
]
. To this end, for any measurable on
\Omega
kernel
R(\cdot,\cdot)
we define Volterra type operator
\mathcal{R}
as follows,

(\mathcal{R}f)(x)=\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)f(t)dt.

(2.13)

Denote by
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{p}:=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L^{p}[0,\ell]\to L^{p}[0,\ell]}
the
L_{p}
-norm of the operator
\mathcal{R}
, provided that it is bounded. Further, recall that a Volterra operator in a Banach space is a compact operator with zero spectrum.

Lemma 2.3

(Lemma 2.1 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
R\in X_{1}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\mathcal{R}
be a Volterra type operator given by (
2.13
). Then:

(i)
The operator
\mathcal{R}
is bounded in
L^{p}[0,\ell]
for
each
p\in[1,\infty]
and

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{p}\leqslant\|R\|_{X_{1}(\Omega)}^{1/p}\cdot\|R\|_{X_{\infty}(\Omega)}^{1-1/p}.

(2.14)

Moreover,

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1}=\|R\|_{X_{1}(\Omega)},\qquad\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty}=\|R\|_{X_{\infty}}.

(2.15)

(ii)
If
R\in X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
, then
\mathcal{R}
is a Volterra operator in
L^{p}[0,\ell]
for each
p\in[1,\infty]
.

In what follows, we will also systematically use notations
X_{1,0}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
and
X_{\infty,0}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
. In general, for any set
S
of complex-valued functions, notation
S\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
means a set of all
n\times m
matrices, where each entry of the matrix is an element of the set
S
.

2.4
Properties of adjugate matrix

Denote by
\GL(n):=\GL(n,\mathbb{C})
the set of invertible
n\times n
matrices with complex entries. For a matrix
\mathcal{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
denote by
\mathcal{A}^{a}
its adjugate matrix, i.e.

\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}^{a}=\mathcal{A}^{a}\mathcal{A}=\det(\mathcal{A})I_{n}.

(2.16)

Let us recall some properties of the adjugate matrix,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{a}=\det(\mathcal{A})\cdot\mathcal{A}^{-1},\qquad\mathcal{A}\in\GL(n),

(2.17)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{A}^{a}]^{*}=[\mathcal{A}^{*}]^{a},\qquad\mathcal{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

(2.18)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}]^{a}=\mathcal{B}^{a}\mathcal{A}^{a},\qquad\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

(2.19)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}_{2}]^{a}=\mathcal{A}_{2}^{a}\mathcal{A}^{a}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{a}=\det(\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{A}_{2})\cdot\mathcal{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{a}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{-1},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\in\GL(n,\mathbb{C}).

(2.20)

Let
\mathcal{A}(\cdot)=(\mathfrak{a}_{jk}(\cdot))_{j,k=1}^{n}
be an

n\times n
matrix function differentiable at a point
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and let

\mathcal{A}^{a}(\cdot)=:(\mathcal{A}_{jk}(\cdot))_{j,k=1}^{n}
be its adjugate matrix function.
Then in accordance with the Jacobi identity,

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\det(\mathcal{A}(\lambda))=\tr\left(\mathcal{A}^{a}(\lambda)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\lambda)\right)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{jk}(\lambda)\mathfrak{a}_{kj}^{\prime}(\lambda).

(2.21)

2.5
Determinants of matrix sums and products

If what follows we will need a few classical formulas for determinant of the sum and the product of matrices.
Further, assuming
n\in\mathbb{N}
is fixed throughout entire paper, we introduce the following set:

\mathfrak{P}_{m}:=\{\mathfrak{p}:=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}):1\leqslant p_{1}<\ldots<p_{m}\leqslant n\},\qquad m\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(2.22)

i.e.
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
is the set of all increasing sequences with exactly
m
elements from
1
to
n
. Additionally, we define
\mathfrak{P}_{0}:=\{\mathfrak{E}_{0}\}
, where
\mathfrak{E}_{0}:=()
is an empty sequence. In what follows, we also denote
\sigma(\mathfrak{p}):=p_{1}+\ldots+p_{m}
. Clearly
\sigma(\mathfrak{E}_{0})=0
.

Further, for any
n\times n
matrix
\mathcal{A}=(a_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n}
and elements
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})
of
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
,
m\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, we set

\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]:=\det(a_{q_{j}p_{k}})_{j,k=1}^{m}=\det\begin{pmatrix}a_{q_{1}p_{1}}&\ldots&a_{q_{1}p_{m}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{q_{m}p_{1}}&\ldots&a_{q_{m}p_{m}}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m},

(2.23)

i.e.
\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]
is a minor of the matrix
\mathcal{A}
generated by the rows with indexes
q_{1}<\ldots<q_{m}
and columns with indexes
p_{1}<\ldots<p_{m}
. If
m=0
then
\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]:=\det(I_{0}):=1
, where
I_{0}
is an empty matrix, where
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{E}_{0}
are empty sequences.

Further, for
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
we denote by
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{n-m}
the complement of
\mathfrak{p}
in the set
\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Namely, let
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
, i.e.
1\leqslant p_{1}<\ldots<p_{m}\leqslant n
, and let

\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\setminus\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}=:\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n-m}\},

where
1\leqslant r_{1}<\ldots<r_{n-m}\leqslant n
. Then by definition
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}:=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n-m})
.

Now we are ready to formulate a classical “folklore” formula for the determinant of the sum of matrices (see e.g.
[
45
]
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. Then

\det(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B})=\det(\mathcal{A})+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}].

(2.24)

Here we utilized all of the above notations, including a complement notation
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
. It is clear, that the summand in r.h.s of (
2.24
) for
m=n
equals to
\det(\mathcal{B})
. Indeed, if
m=n
then
\mathfrak{P}_{m}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{0}\}
, where
\mathfrak{p}_{0}:=(1,\ldots,n)
, and inner sum degenerates to
\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{p}_{0}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}_{0}}]\cdot\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{0}]
. Complement
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}_{0}}=\mathfrak{E}_{0}
is an empty sequence. Hence
\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{p}_{0}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}_{0}}]=\det(I_{0})=1
. It is also clear that
\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{0}]=\det(\mathcal{B})
.

Next, we formulate a straightforward extension of the classical Cauchy–Binet formula.

Lemma 2.5

(Subsection 1.2.6 in
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,
m\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
. Then

(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C})[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]=\sum_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}].

(2.25)

Some remarks. If
m=1
, this formula is nothing more than a definition of the matrix product. If
m=n
, then this formula turns into
\det(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C})=\det(\mathcal{B})\cdot\det(\mathcal{C})
.

Finally, combining Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
we arrive at the following formula that will be useful for studying characteristic determinant of the BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. Then

\det(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C})=\det(\mathcal{A})+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}].

(2.26)

To estimate root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
we will also need version of Lemma
2.6
for cofactors of
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}
. Let us recall the corresponding definition. To this end, let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathfrak{a}_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. Then by definition, cofactor
\mathcal{A}\{j,k\}
of the element
\mathfrak{a}_{jk}
of the matrix
\mathcal{A}
is the element at the
j
-th row and
k
-th column of the matrix
\mathcal{A}^{a}
adjugate to
\mathcal{A}
(introduced in Subsection
2.4
), i.e.
\mathcal{A}^{a}=:(\mathcal{A}\{j,k\})_{j,k=1}^{n}
. Let us express it via our notation
\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]
.
It is easily seen that

\mathcal{A}^{a}=\left(\mathcal{A}\{j,k\}\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}=\left((-1)^{j+k}\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{j}]\right)_{j,k=1}^{n},

(2.27)

where

\mathfrak{p}_{k}:=(1,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots n)=\widehat{(k)}\in\mathfrak{P}_{n-1}.

(2.28)

With this observation we can easily derive the following versions of Lemmas
2.4
and
2.6
for cofactors.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and let
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B})\{j,k\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}\{j,k\}+(-1)^{j+k}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{\fq, \fp\in\fP_m}{k \not\in\fq, \, j \not\in\fp}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}],

(2.29)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C})\{j,k\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}\{j,k\}+(-1)^{j+k}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{\fq, \fp, \fr\in\fP_m}{k \not\in\fq, \, j \not\in\fp}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}\mathcal{A}[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}].

(2.30)

3
Transformation operators

In this section we prove the existence of triangular transformation operators for the system

\mathcal{L}(Q)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}(y^{\prime}+Q(x)y)=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell],\\

(3.1)

expressing solution to the certain Cauchy problem for equation (
3.1
) via the solution to the same Cauchy problem for the simplest equation

\mathcal{L}(0)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}y^{\prime}=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(3.2)

This result extends Theorem 1.2 from
[
41
]
and Theorem 2.5 from
[
33
]
to the case of non-constant matrix
B(\cdot)
. Following the scheme of reasonings of Theorem 1.2 from
[
41
]
we first establish the similarity of certain restrictions
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
of the operators
\mathcal{L}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}(0)
, respectively.

3.1
Similarity of operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)

First we introduce the main objects, the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
.
To this end we need to change notation comparing to (
1.3
)–(
1.4
) and work with a block-matrix decomposition for matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
. Namely, let

\displaystyle B(x)

\displaystyle=\diag(B_{1}(x),\ldots,B_{r}(x))=B(x)^{*},\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(3.3)

\displaystyle B_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}(x)I_{n_{k}},\quad x\in[0,\ell],\qquad\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.4)

be a self-adjoint invertible diagonal summable matrix function, where
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{r}=n
, and

Q=:(Q_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{r},\qquad Q_{jk}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{k}}),\qquad Q_{jj}\equiv 0,\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.5)

be a summable (generally non-self-adjoint) potential matrix with zero “block diagonal” with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
. It will be shown in Lemma
6.11
that the case of arbitrary
Q
can be reduced to it.
We deliberately reused existing notation for
\beta_{k}
and
Q_{jk}
to avoid introducing new notation. But notation (
3.3
)–(
3.5
) will be used solely in this section, which should avoid any confusion.

Let us rewrite conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) on matrix function
B(\cdot)
with a new notation (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) in mind. Namely, in this section we assume that for some
\theta>0
and
r_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,r\}
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle\beta_{k},\ 1/\beta_{k}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell],\qquad\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))\equiv\const\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(x)<\ldots<\beta_{r_{-}}(x)<-\theta<0<\theta<\beta_{r_{-}+1}(x)<\ldots<\beta_{r}(x),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(3.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(x)+\theta<\beta_{k+1}(x),\quad x\in[0,\ell],\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{r-1}\}.

(3.8)

Let us also set

b_{j}:=\rho_{j}(\ell),\qquad\rho_{j}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{j}(t)dt,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.9)

Next we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
the restriction of the maximal operator
\mathcal{L}_{\max}(Q)
in
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
generated by the expression

\mathcal{L}(Q)
on the domain

\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)=\{f\in\dom\mathcal{L}_{\max}(Q):\ f(0)=0\}\subset\widetilde{W}^{1,1}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(3.10)

Here

\widetilde{W}^{1,p}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}):=\{f\in W^{1,p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}):f(0)=0\}.

Moreover, if
Q\in L^{2}\bigl([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\bigr)
, then

\dom\mathcal{L}_{\max}(Q)=W^{1,2}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)=\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(3.11)

In particular, one has

\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=B(x)^{-1}\otimes D_{0},\quad\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad\text{where}\quad D_{0}:=-i\frac{d}{dx}\upharpoonright\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}[0,\ell].

Note also that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)

is invertible and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)^{-1}=B(x)\otimes(iJ)
where
J
is the Volterra integration
operator,
J:\ f\to\int^{x}_{0}f(t)\,dt
.

To state the main result of this subsection let us recall the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
be closed densely defined operators in a Banach space
X
with domains
\dom L_{1}
and
\dom L_{2}
,
respectively. It is said that a bounded operator
T
intertwines the operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
if:

a)
T
maps
\dom L_{1}
onto
\dom L_{2}
;

b)
L_{2}Tf=TL_{1}f,\quad f\in\dom L_{1}
.

If in addition,
0\in\rho(T)
, i.e.
T
has a bounded inverse, then the operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are called similar.

To establish similarity of the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
for
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
we first establish this under additional smoothness assumptions of the potential
Q
related to Lipshitz properties (see Subsection
2.2
).

Proposition 3.2
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
3.3
)–(
3.8
).
In particular, we assume that
Q_{jj}=0
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
and
|\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{k}(x)|>\theta
for a.e.
x\in[0,\ell]
and
j\neq k
. Let also

\widetilde{Q}_{jk}:=\frac{Q_{jk}}{\beta_{j}-\beta_{k}}\in\Lip_{1}[0,\ell],\quad j\neq k,\qquad Q\in L^{\infty}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}).

(3.12)

Then the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=B(x)^{-1}\otimes D_{0}
are similar in

L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
,
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Moreover, there exists an
n\times n
matrix kernel

R\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

(3.13)

where domain
\Omega
is given by (
2.9
), such that triangular Volterra type operator
I+\mathcal{R}
,

(I+\mathcal{R})f:=f(x)+\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt,\qquad f\in L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}),

(3.14)

is bounded on
L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
, has a bounded inverse, and intertwines the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
, i.e.

\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)(I+\mathcal{R})f=(I+\mathcal{R})\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)f,\qquad f\in\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(3.15)

Proof.

The proof will be divided into multiple steps.

(i)
At this step we show that the intertwining property (
3.15
) is equivalent to a certain boundary value problem for the kernel
R(\cdot,\cdot)
in the triangle
\Omega=\{0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell\}
. It is easily seen that

\bigl(-iB\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)(I+\mathcal{R})f\bigr)(x)=\left[\frac{d}{dx}+Q\right]\bigl(f(x)+\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt\bigr)\\
=f^{\prime}(x)+Q(x)f(x)+\frac{d}{dx}\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt+Q(x)\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt\\
=f^{\prime}(x)+Q(x)f(x)+R(x,x)B(x)f(x)+\int^{x}_{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt+Q(x)\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt.

(3.16)

On the other hand, integrating by parts one derives

\bigl(-iB(I+\mathcal{R})\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)f\bigr)(x)=\bigl(B(I+\mathcal{R})(B^{-1}f^{\prime})\bigr)(x)\\
=f^{\prime}(x)+B(x)\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)\cdot B^{-1}(t)f^{\prime}(t)\,dt=f^{\prime}(x)+B(x)\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)f^{\prime}(t)\,dt\\
=f^{\prime}(x)+B(x)R(x,x)f(x)-B(x)R(x,0)f(0)-B(x)\int^{x}_{0}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R(x,t)\right)f(t)\,dt.

(3.17)

Equating right hand sides of both equations and noting that
f\in\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
is arbitrary satisfying
f(0)=0
, leads to the following boundary value problem for the matrix kernel
R(x,t):

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R(x,t)B(t)+B(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R(x,t)+Q(x)R(x,t)B(t)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.18)

\displaystyle R(x,x)B(x)-B(x)R(x,x)+Q(x)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.19)

for a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega
.
Let us write the matrix kernel
R(\cdot,\cdot)
in the block-matrix form
R(x,t)=\left(R_{jk}(x,t)\right)_{j,k=1}^{r}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
. Since
B(\cdot)
is block-diagonal it follows that the problem (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) splits into
r
independent problems on columns
R_{k}(x,t):=(R_{jk}(x,t))_{j=1}^{r}
of the matrix kernel
R(\cdot,\cdot)
. Fixing
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
, using the block-matrix representation of matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
and taking into account condition
Q_{jj}\equiv 0
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
, corresponding problem for the
k
-th column of the matrix kernel
R(\cdot,\cdot)
takes the following form for a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}(x,t)+\frac{\beta_{j}(x)}{\beta_{k}(t)}\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(x)R_{pk}(x,t),\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.20)

\displaystyle R_{jk}(x,x)=\frac{Q_{jk}(x)}{\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{k}(x)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(x),\qquad j\neq k,\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.21)

Emphasize that
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}
,
j\neq k
, is well-defined and summable on
[0,\ell]
due to conditions (
3.5
)–(
3.8
). It is clear now that to finish the proof it is sufficient to show that for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
there exists a vector kernel
R_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
that satisfies the incomplete Cauchy problem (
3.20
)–(
3.21
).

(ii)
To prepare for the next step, we need to extend functions
\beta_{j}(\cdot)
and
\rho_{j}(\cdot)
to be defined on
\mathbb{R}
and satisfy conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) (for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
). By definition,
\beta_{j}\in L^{\infty}
is a class of functions equivalent to a certain base function and conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) are valid only for a.e.
x\in[0,\ell]
. It is clear that for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
we can select an appropriate representative from this class of equivalence (and call it
\beta_{j}
for simplicity) such that the selected functions
\beta_{j}(\cdot)
are defined for each
x\in[0,\ell]
and satisfy conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) for each
x\in[0,\ell]
(and not just for a.e.
x\in[0,\ell]
). With this remark in mind, it is clear that the following extension satisfies conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\beta_{j}(x):=\begin{cases}\beta_{j}(0),&x<0,\\
\beta_{j}(x),&x\in[0,\ell],\\
\beta_{j}(\ell),&x>\ell,\end{cases},\qquad\rho_{j}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{j}(t)dt,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.22)

It is clear from (
3.6
) that
\rho_{j}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}
and
\rho_{j}(\cdot)
is a strictly monotonous function,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Hence there exists strictly monotonous inverse
\rho_{j}^{-1}
that also maps
\mathbb{R}
onto
\mathbb{R}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Summarizing this we have,

\rho_{j}(\mathbb{R})=\rho_{j}^{-1}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{j}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \rho_{j}^{-1}\quad\text{are strictly monotonous},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.23)

More importantly, condition (
3.8
) implies the same property for differences
\rho_{j}-\rho_{k}
,

(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(\mathbb{R})=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R},\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{j}-\rho_{k},\ (\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}\ \ \text{are strictly monotonous},\quad j\neq k.

(3.24)

Note also that conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) implie the following important Lipshitz property,

\rho_{j},\ \rho_{j}^{-1},\ \ \rho_{j}-\rho_{k},\ (\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad j\neq k,\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.25)

Local Lipshitz property is implied directly by conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
). Global Lipshitz property holds because functions
\rho_{j}
are linear outside of
[0,\ell]
.

We also need to extend matrix function
Q(\cdot)
to be defined on
\mathbb{R}
. For simplicity we will use the same notation for it and the same notation for
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}
,
j\neq k
, given by (
3.21
). In the future we will need to work with a certain system of integral equations without the assumption
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}[0,\ell]
. Hence, we start by extending
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}
(that outside of this Proposition might be only summable) in any way such that the following properties hold,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(x)=0,\qquad x\notin(-\delta,\ell+\delta),\qquad j\neq k,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\quad\text{whenever}\quad\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in L^{p}[0,\ell],\quad p\in[1,\infty],\qquad j\neq k,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R})\quad\text{whenever}\quad\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}[0,\ell],\qquad j\neq k,

(3.28)

with some
\delta>0
. Then we naturally define
Q_{jk}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
by formula (
3.21
) and also extend block-diagonal entries of the matrix function
Q(\cdot)
to be zero,

\displaystyle Q_{jk}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{k}(x))\cdot\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\neq k,

(3.29)

\displaystyle Q_{jj}(x)

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{Q}_{jj}(x):=0,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.30)

It is clear from the construction and conditions (
3.5
)–(
3.8
) that

Q(x)=0,\quad x\notin(-\delta,\ell+\delta),\qquad\text{and}\qquad Q\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\quad\text{whenever}\quad Q\in L^{p}[0,\ell],\quad p\in[1,\infty].

(3.31)

(iii)
At this step assuming
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
to be fixed, we consider the incomplete Cauchy problem (
3.20
)–(
3.21
) and complete it up to a special Goursat problem in a certain extended domain
\Omega_{k}\supset\Omega
assuming functions
\beta_{j}
,
\rho_{j}
and
Q_{jp}
to be extended on
\mathbb{R}
as constructed in step (ii). Along the way, we establish equivalent system of integral equations.

The corresponding characteristic system is
{\beta_{j}(x)}\,dx={\beta_{k}(t)}\,dt
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Since
\rho_{j}^{\prime}(x)=\beta_{j}(x)
for a.e.
x\in[0,\ell]
, this characteristic system defines the system of characteristic curves of the equation (
3.20
):

\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)=c=\const,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.32)

Condition (
3.23
) implies that we can present the characteristic at “level”
c\in\mathbb{R}
as

\Gamma_{jk}^{c}:=\{(x,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(x)):x\in\mathbb{R}\},\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{c}(x):=\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{j}(x)-c),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.33)

It is clear that
\gamma_{jk}^{c}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}
, and
\gamma_{jk}^{c}
is strictly monotonous, linear outside of a certain interval (that depends on
j
,
k
and
c
) and globally Lipshitz just like functions
\rho_{k}^{-1}
and
\rho_{j}
.

It can be easily seen that the incomplete Cauchy problem (
3.20
)–(
3.21
) is not characteristic. To integrate it we need to look for solution of this problem in the extended domain. To this end, let the column
R_{k}\in\Lip_{1,\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{n})
satisfy (
3.20
)–(
3.21
) for all
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let us obtain some important properties of
R_{k}
.
It follows from the formula for derivative of the inverse function of the absolutely continuous function that

\rho_{j}^{\prime}(x)=\beta_{j}(x),\qquad(\rho_{j}^{-1})^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{\beta_{j}(\rho_{j}^{-1}(x))},\qquad\text{for a.e.}\ \ x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.34)

Combining (
3.33
) and (
3.34
) we arrive at

(\gamma_{jk}^{c})^{\prime}(u)=\frac{\beta_{j}(u)}{\beta_{k}(\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))},\qquad\text{for a.e.}\ \ u\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.35)

Recall that as per (
2.4
),

D_{1}R_{jk}(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}(x,t),\qquad D_{2}R_{jk}(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}(x,t),\qquad x,t\in\mathbb{R},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.36)

With this notation in mind we can rewrite (
3.20
) as follows

D_{1}R_{jk}(u,v)+\frac{\beta_{j}(u)}{\beta_{k}(v)}D_{2}R_{jk}(u,v)=-\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,v),\qquad\text{for a.e.}\ \ u,v\in\mathbb{R},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.37)

Using standard rules for differentiating functions of two variables, and
combining (
3.35
) and (
3.37
), we obtain for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{du}\bigl[R_{jk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))\bigr]

\displaystyle=D_{1}R_{jk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))+(\gamma_{jk}^{c})^{\prime}(u)D_{2}R_{jk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))

\displaystyle=D_{1}R_{jk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))+\frac{\beta_{j}(u)}{\beta_{k}(\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))}D_{2}R_{jk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))

\displaystyle=-\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u)),\qquad\text{for}\ \ c\in\mathbb{R}\quad\text{and for a.e.}\ \ u\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.38)

It is clear that the function
R_{jk}(\cdot,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(\cdot))
is locally Lipshitz. Hence, integrating formula (
3.38
) from
a
to
x
, we arrive at

R_{jk}(x,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(x))-R_{jk}(a,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(a))=-\int_{a}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u))du,\quad x,a,c\in\mathbb{R},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.39)

Let us fix
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
and
-\infty<t\leqslant x<\infty
. Consider the characteristic curve
\Gamma_{jk}^{c}
passing through the point
(x,t)
. It is clear from definition (
3.33
) of
\gamma_{jk}^{c}(\cdot)
that

\gamma_{jk}^{c}(x)=t\qquad\text{and}\qquad c=\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t).

(3.40)

Let us also set for brevity for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
and
x,t,u\in\mathbb{R}

\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u):=\gamma_{jk}^{c}(u)=\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{j}(u)-\rho_{j}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)),\qquad x,t,u\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{\cdot,\cdot}(\cdot)\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(3.41)

where the last inclusion follows from the property (
3.25
).

First let
j\neq k
. Then there exists
a=a_{jk}(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(a)=a
. Indeed, this equation is equivalent to
\rho_{j}(a)-\rho_{j}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)=\rho_{k}(a)
and hence condition (
3.24
) implies that

a_{jk}(x,t):=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t))=\Bigl.(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(u)\Bigr|_{u=\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)},\qquad a_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

(3.42)

Setting
a=a_{jk}(x,t)
in (
3.39
) and taking into account (
3.40
), (
3.42
) and (
3.21
), we arrive at

-\int_{a}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))du=R_{jk}(x,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(x))-R_{jk}(a,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(a))\\
=R_{jk}(x,t)-R_{jk}(a,a)=R_{jk}(x,t)-\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a),\quad a=a_{jk}(x,t).

(3.43)

or

R_{jk}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))-\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))du,\qquad t\leqslant x,\quad j\neq k.

(3.44)

Now we are ready to define the domain
\Omega_{k}
on which we will set the Goursat problem. For convenience,
[u,v]
and
[v,u]
will denote the same segment of the real line,

[u,v]:=[v,u]=\{t\in\mathbb{R}:v\leqslant t\leqslant u\},\qquad v\leqslant u.

(3.45)

Let us also define the part of the characteristic curve
\Gamma_{jk}^{c}
that starts at the point
(x,t)
of the triangle
\Omega
and ends on the diagonal
\mathbf{D}:=\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:u=v\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t}:=\left\{\left(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\right):\ u\in[x,a_{jk}(x,t)]\right\},\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega,\qquad j\neq k.

(3.46)

With this notation in mind, we formally define

\Omega_{k}:=\bigcup_{j\neq k}\Omega_{jk},\qquad\Omega_{jk}:=\bigcup_{(x,t)\in\Omega}\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t},\qquad j\neq k.

(3.47)

Let us fix
j\neq k
. It is clear from the continuity and monotonicity of
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(\cdot)
and
(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}
, and general geometrical reasoning that we can only have three cases:

1.

\Omega_{jk}=\Omega
(when the characteristic curve segments
\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t}
do not “go outside” of the triangle
\Omega
);

2.

\Omega_{jk}=\{(u,v):\ u\in[a_{jk}(\ell,0),\ell],\ v\in[\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(u),u]\}
(when
\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t}
that “go outside” of the triangle
\Omega
intersect the diagonal
\mathbf{D}
at points with negative coordinate
u
);

3.

\Omega_{jk}=\Omega\cup\{(u,v):\ u\in(\ell,a_{jk}(\ell,0)],\ v\in[\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(u),u]\}
(when
\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t}
that “go outside” of
\Omega
intersect the diagonal
\mathbf{D}
at points with positive coordinate
u
).

It is clear that we can unify all three cases with a single representation of the following form,

\Omega_{jk}=\{(u,v):\ u\in[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}],\ \ v\in[\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u),u]\},

(3.48)

where numbers
a_{jk}^{-}\leqslant 0<\ell\leqslant a_{jk}^{+}
and function
\gamma_{jk}^{-}(\cdot)\leqslant 0
are defined as follows in each of the three cases:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{llll}\bullet&a_{jk}^{-}=0,&a_{jk}^{+}=\ell,&\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)=0,\quad u\in[0,\ell],\\
\bullet&a_{jk}^{-}=a_{jk}(\ell,0),&a_{jk}^{+}=\ell,&\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)=\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(u),\quad u\in[0,\ell],\\
\bullet&a_{jk}^{-}=0,&a_{jk}^{+}=a_{jk}(\ell,0),&\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)=\begin{cases}0,\quad&u\in[0,\ell],\\
\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(u),\quad&u\in(\ell,a_{jk}(\ell,0)],\\
\end{cases}\\
\end{array}

(see Remark
3.3
for more details).
It now follows from (
3.48
)–(
3.1
) that

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}=\{(u,v):\ u\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}],\ \ v\in[\gamma_{k}^{-}(u),u]\},\quad\text{where}

(3.52)

\displaystyle a_{k}^{-}:=\min\{a_{jk}^{-}:j\neq k\}\leqslant 0,\qquad a_{k}^{+}:=\max\{a_{jk}^{+}:j\neq k\}\geqslant\ell,

(3.53)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}^{-}(u):=\min\{\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u):\ j\neq k\ \ \text{such that}\ \ u\in[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]\},\qquad u\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}],

(3.54)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}^{-}:=\{(u,\gamma_{k}^{-}(u)):\ u\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}]\}\quad\text{is the ``lower boundary'' of the domain}\ \ \Omega_{k}.

(3.55)

Properties of
\gamma_{jk}^{c}(\cdot)
outlined after formula (
3.33
) and the fact, that the minimum of finite number of Lipshitz non-decreasing functions preserves these properties, imply that

\gamma_{k}^{-}\in\Lip_{1}[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}],\qquad\gamma_{jk}^{-}\in\Lip_{1}[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma_{k}^{-},\gamma_{jk}^{-}\quad\text{are non-decreasing},\quad j\neq k.

(3.56)

This in turn imply that
\Omega_{k}
has a Lipshitz boundary. It is also simply connected, closed and bounded.

In order to formulate Goursat problem, observe that the definition of
\Omega_{jk}
implies that the characteristic curve segment
\Gamma_{jk}^{x,t}
, defined in (
3.46
) and considered for points
(x,t)\in\Omega_{jk}
, does not go outside of
\Omega_{jk}
. The same is valid for the union
\Omega_{k}
(though this needs a bit more considerations involving “ordering” of characteristic curves implied by (
3.7
)).
Hence we can restrict kernels
R_{jk}(x,t)
to be only defined on
\Omega_{k}
and the system of equations (
3.44
) will still be valid,

R_{jk}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))-\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))du,\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\quad j\neq k.

(3.57)

Let us now go back to our raw equation (
3.39
) and handle the diagonal entry
R_{kk}(\cdot,\cdot)
(recall that
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
is fixed). We don’t have any initial data for it yet and are free to impose any appropriate initial condition. To this end note that the “characteristic function”
\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}
is strictly increasing for any given point
(x,t)
,

\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u)=\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{k}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)),\quad u\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}\quad\text{is increasing},\quad x,t\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.58)

Note also, that the characteristic curve
\Gamma_{kk}^{c}
never intersects the diagonal
\mathbf{D}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
if
c\neq 0
, while
\Gamma_{kk}^{0}=\mathbf{D}
. With account of these observations and noting that
\Gamma_{k}^{-}
(the “lower boundary” of
\Omega_{k}
) intersects the diagonal
\mathbf{D}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
at the point
a_{k}^{-}
,
we see that the characteristic curve
\Gamma_{kk}^{x,t}
, that passes through any point
(x,t)
of the domain
\Omega_{k}
, intersects with
\Gamma_{k}^{-}
. Namely, there exists
a_{kk}=a_{kk}(x,t)\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}]
such that

\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(a_{kk}(x,t))=\gamma_{k}^{-}(a_{kk}(x,t)),\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}.

(3.59)

Let us show that
a_{kk}\in\Lip(\Omega_{k})
. Let
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
and
j\neq k
be fixed and let
a_{kk,j}(x,t)
be the intersection of
\Gamma_{kk}^{x,t}
with the “lower boundary” of
\Omega_{jk}
, i.e.

\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(a_{kk,j}(x,t))=\gamma_{jk}^{-}(a_{kk,j}(x,t)),\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\quad j\neq k.

(3.60)

In each of the three cases in (
3.1
) we can find explicit form of
a_{kk,j}(x,t)
. For the first two cases we have,

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{llll}\bullet&\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)=0,\quad u\in[0,\ell],&a_{kk,j}(x,t)=\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)),\\
\bullet&\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)=\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(u),\quad u\in[0,\ell],&a_{kk,j}(x,t)=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(\rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)),\\
\end{array}

while in the third
a_{kk,j}(x,t)
is in a way “a union” of two cases. Here is the detailed proof for the second case for posterity,

\displaystyle\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(a)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{jk}^{\ell,0}(a)\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{k}(a)-\rho_{k}(x)+\rho_{k}(t))

\displaystyle=\rho_{k}^{-1}(\rho_{j}(a)-\rho_{j}(\ell))\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\rho_{j}(a)-\rho_{k}(a)\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a_{kk,j}(x,t)

\displaystyle=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(\rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(x)+\rho_{k}(t)).

Note that for some points
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
the found point
a_{kk,j}(x,t)
intersects the curve
\Gamma_{jk}^{-}:=\{(u,\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u)):\ u\in\mathbb{R}\}
outside of
\Omega_{jk}
. Despite that, it can be shown that

a_{kk}(x,t)=\min\{a_{kk,j}(x,t):j\neq k\},\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}.

(3.63)

Formulas (
3.1
), (
3.63
) and property (
3.25
) now easily imply that

a_{kk},a_{kk,j}\in\Lip(\Omega_{k}),\qquad j\neq k.

(3.64)

With this preparation in mind, we can impose the following initial condition on
R_{kk}(\cdot,\cdot)
,

R_{kk}(x,\gamma_{k}^{-}(x))=0,\qquad x\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}].

(3.65)

Setting
a=a_{kk}(x,t)
in (
3.39
) and taking into account (
3.65
) we get similar to (
3.43
),

R_{kk}(x,t)=-\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{kp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))du,\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}.

(3.66)

With account of convention
\widetilde{Q}_{kk}=0
(see (
3.30
)), we can combine (
3.57
) and (
3.66
) into a single formula for all
r
equations,

R_{jk}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))-\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))du,\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.67)

where, as before,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
is fixed. It is clear from considerations in formulas (
3.38
)–(
3.39
) that the system of integral equations (
3.67
) is equivalent to the following Goursat problem whenever
R_{k}\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega_{k})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}(x,t)+\frac{\beta_{j}(x)}{\beta_{k}(t)}\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(x)R_{pk}(x,t),\quad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.68)

\displaystyle R_{jk}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(x),\qquad x\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}],\qquad j\neq k,\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.69)

\displaystyle R_{kk}(x,\gamma_{k}^{-}(x))

\displaystyle=0,\qquad x\in[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}].

(3.70)

Moreover, it is obvious that any vector kernel solution
R_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
of this Goursat problem satisfies the desired incomplete Cauchy problem (
3.20
)–(
3.21
). Therefore, to finish the proof it is sufficient to show solvability of the system of integral equations (
3.67
) in
\Lip_{1}(\Omega_{k})
.

(iv)
At this step, again assuming
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
to be fixed, we apply the method of successive approximation to prove the existence of solution to the system (
3.67
) in
C(\Omega_{k})
. First, note that if the
k
-th block column
Q_{k}=(Q_{jk})_{j=1}^{r}
of the matrix
Q
is zero, then
R_{k}\equiv 0
is a valid solution of the system (
3.67
). Going forward we assume that
Q_{k}\neq 0
. To this end we set

R_{jk}^{(0)}(x,t):=0,\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.71)

Assuming functions
R_{jk}^{(m-1)}
to be defined for a given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we set for
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
,

\displaystyle R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))-\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(u)R_{pk}^{(m-1)}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))\,du,\quad j\neq k,\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.72)

\displaystyle R_{kk}^{(m)}(x,t)

\displaystyle:=-\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{kp}(u)R_{pk}^{(m)}(u,\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))\,du,

(3.73)

To explain the correctness of this definition let us mention that since
Q_{kk}(\cdot)=0
by the assumption, the entries
R_{kk}^{(m)}
of the vector function
\bigl(R_{jk}^{(m)}\bigr)_{j=1}^{r}
are expressed by means of equalities (
3.73
) via the entries
R_{jk}^{(m)}
,
j\neq k
, defined on the previous step by equalities (
3.72
). Note in particular, that

R_{jk}^{(1)}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t)),\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\qquad j\neq k,\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.74)

Note that since

\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}),\quad a_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad Q_{jp}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\gamma_{jk}^{\cdot,\cdot}(\cdot)\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad j,p\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.75)

then

R_{jk}^{(m)}\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega_{k}),\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.76)

First we show that for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
the (uniform) limit
\lim_{m\to\infty}R_{jk}^{(m)}=:R_{jk}
exists in
C(\Omega_{k})
and defines a solution to the system (
3.67
). Clearly, this convergence
is equivalent to the convergence in
C(\Omega_{k})
of the series

R_{jk}^{(0)}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\left[R_{jk}^{(m)}-R_{jk}^{(m-1)}\right]

(3.77)

Let us prove by induction on
N
that

\displaystyle\left|{R^{(N+1)}_{jk}(x,t)-R^{(N)}_{jk}(x,t)}\right|\leqslant C_{jk}\tau_{k}^{N}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{N}}{N!},\quad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},\quad N\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(3.78)

with constants
C_{1k},\ldots,C_{rk}\geqslant 0
and
\tau_{k}>0
given by

\displaystyle C_{jk}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{q}_{k}:=\max\{\|\widetilde{Q}_{pk}\|_{\infty}:p\neq k\}>0,\qquad j\neq k,\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.79)

\displaystyle C_{kk}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{q}_{k}\cdot q_{k}\cdot(a_{k}^{+}-a_{k}^{-}),\qquad q_{k}:=\sum^{r}_{p=1}\|Q_{kp}\|_{\infty}\geqslant 0,

(3.80)

\displaystyle\tau_{k}

\displaystyle:=\theta^{-1}\max\Bigl\{q_{k}\|Q_{jk}\|_{\infty}+\sum^{r}_{p=1}\|Q_{jp}\|_{\infty}:j\neq k\Bigr\}>0.

(3.81)

where
[a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}]
is the projection of
\Omega_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}
and is given by (
3.53
) and parameter
\theta
is from the condition (
3.8
). We also set for brevity,
\|f\|_{\infty}:=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{k})}
for any
f:\Omega_{k}\to\mathbb{C}
. Note that
\widetilde{q}_{k}>0
and
\tau_{k}>0
because of the current assumption that the
k
-th block column
Q_{k}=(Q_{jk})_{j=1}^{r}
of the matrix
Q
is non-zero. In addition,
C_{kk}=0=q_{k}
whenever the block row
(Q_{kp})_{p=1}^{r}
of
Q
is zero. In this case r.h.s. of equation (
3.73
) vanishes and
R_{kk}^{(m)}
is necessarily zero for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Let
N=0
and
j\neq k
. It follows from (
3.71
), (
3.74
) and (
3.79
) that

\left|{R_{jk}^{(1)}(x,t)-R_{jk}^{(0)}(x,t)}\right|=\left|{\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))}\right|\leqslant\|\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\widetilde{q}_{k}=C_{jk},\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},\qquad j\neq k,

(3.82)

for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Hence (
3.78
) is valid for
N=0
and
j\neq k
.

Assume that the estimate (
3.78
) is valid for
N=m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
j\neq k
and let us prove it for the same
N=m
and
j=k
. First observe that relation (
3.41
) implies that

\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{k}(\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))=\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t),\qquad x,t,u\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.83)

Taking into account relations (
3.83
), (
3.79
)–(
3.80
) and the fact that
Q_{kk}\equiv 0
, we subtract two equations (
3.73
) with
m
and
m+1
, respectively, and insert the estimate (
3.78
), valid by induction hypothesis for
N=m
and
p\neq k
, into this difference,

\displaystyle|R^{(m+1)}_{kk}(x,t)-R^{(m)}_{kk}(x,t)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum^{r}_{p=1}\left|{\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}|Q_{kp}(u)|\cdot\left|{R_{pk}^{(m+1)}(u,\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))-R_{pk}^{(m)}(u,\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))}\right|\,du}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau_{k}^{m}\widetilde{q}_{k}\sum^{r}_{p=1}\|Q_{kp}\|_{\infty}\left|\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{|\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{k}(\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))|^{m}}{m!}\,du\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{q}_{k}q_{k}\tau_{k}^{m}\left|\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m}}{m!}\,du\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{q}_{k}q_{k}\tau_{k}^{m}(a_{k}^{+}-a_{k}^{-})\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m}}{m!}=C_{kk}\tau_{k}^{m}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m}}{m!},

(3.84)

which yields the desired relation (
3.78
) for
N=m
and
j=k
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and assume that the estimate (
3.78
) is valid for
N=m
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Let us prove it for
N=m+1
and
j\neq k
. Observe that relation (
3.41
) implies that

\rho_{k}(\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))=\rho_{j}(u)-\rho_{j}(x)+\rho_{k}(t),\qquad x,t,u\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.85)

Let
j\neq k
and
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
be fixed. With account of identity (
3.85
) and taking difference of (
3.72
) for
m+1
and
m
, the induction hypothesis implies,

\displaystyle\left|{R^{(m+1)}_{jk}(x,t)-R^{(m)}_{jk}(x,t)}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum^{r}_{p=1}\left|{\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}|Q_{jp}(u)|\cdot\left|{R_{pk}^{(m)}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))-R_{pk}^{(m-1)}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))}\right|\,du}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau_{k}^{m}\sum^{r}_{p=1}C_{pk}\|Q_{jp}\|_{\infty}\left|{\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{\left|{\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{k}(\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))}\right|^{m}}{m!}\,du}\right|

\displaystyle=C_{0jk}\tau_{k}^{m}\left|{\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{\left|{\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{j}(u)+\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)}\right|^{m}}{m!}\,du}\right|,

(3.86)

where definitions (
3.79
)–(
3.81
) imply

C_{0jk}:=\sum^{r}_{p=1}C_{pk}\|Q_{jp}\|_{\infty}=\widetilde{q}_{k}\cdot\Bigl(q_{k}\|Q_{jk}\|_{\infty}+\sum^{r}_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{p=1}{p \ne k}}\|Q_{jp}\|_{\infty}\Bigr),\qquad C_{0jk}\theta^{-1}\leqslant C_{jk}\tau_{k}.

(3.87)

Since
j\neq k
, then relations (
3.24
)–(
3.25
) imply that the function

f(\cdot):=\rho_{k}(\cdot)-\rho_{j}(\cdot)+\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)

is strictly monotonous and Lipshitz on
\mathbb{R}
(recall, that
j,k,x,t
are fixed, hence we didn’t add them into notation of
f
). Moreover, from definition (
3.42
) of
a_{jk}(x,t)
if follows that
f(a_{jk}(x,t))=0
, while clearly
f(x)=\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)
. Hence, monotonicity of
f(\cdot)
implies that

\text{either}\quad f(u)\geqslant 0,\quad u\in[a_{jk}(x,t),x]\qquad\text{or}\qquad f(u)\leqslant 0,\quad u\in[a_{jk}(x,t),x].

(3.88)

Let
g:=f^{-1}
be the function inverse to
f
, which exists due to the above observations. It follows from the standard formula for derivate of the inverse function that

g^{\prime}(v)=\frac{1}{\beta_{k}(f(v))-\beta_{j}(f(v))},\qquad|g^{\prime}(v)|\leqslant\theta^{-1},\qquad v\in\mathbb{R},

(3.89)

where inequality is implied by conditions (
3.7
)–(
3.8
) and construction (
3.22
). Therefore, making a change of variable
u=g(v)
in the integral (
3.86
) we obtain

\left|{\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{\left|{\rho_{k}(u)-\rho_{j}(u)+\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)}\right|^{m}}{m!}\,du}\right|\\
=\left|{\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\frac{f(u)^{m}}{m!}\,du}\right|=\left|{\int_{0}^{\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)}\frac{v^{m}}{m!}\cdot g^{\prime}(v)\,dv}\right|\leqslant\theta^{-1}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}.

(3.90)

Inserting (
3.90
) into (
3.86
) and taking into account estimate (
3.87
) we arrive at

\left|{R^{(m+1)}_{jk}(x,t)-R^{(m)}_{jk}(x,t)}\right|\leqslant C_{0jk}\tau_{k}^{m}\theta^{-1}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}\leqslant C_{jk}\tau_{k}^{m+1}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m+1}}{(m+1)!},

(3.91)

which proves (
3.78
) for
N=m+1
and
j\neq k
.

It is clear now, that the crucial estimate (
3.78
) is proved. In turn, this estimate implies the
absolute and uniform convergence of the series (
3.77
) in
\Omega_{k}
which ensures
the existence in
\Omega_{k}
of the continuous solution
R_{jk}=\lim_{m\to\infty}R_{jk}^{(m)}
to the integral
system (
3.67
). Moreover, inserting estimate (
3.78
)
in (
3.77
) leads to the following estimate for the vector solution
(R_{jk})_{j=1}^{r}
:

|R_{jk}(x,t)|\leqslant C_{jk}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\tau_{k}^{m}\frac{|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|^{m}}{m!}\leqslant\|\widetilde{Q}\|_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{jk}\cdot e^{\tau_{k}\cdot|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|},\qquad(x,t)\in\Omega_{k},

(3.92)

where
\widetilde{C}_{jk}=1
for
j\neq k
,
\widetilde{C}_{kk}=q_{k}\cdot(a_{k}^{+}-a_{k}^{-})
and

\|\widetilde{Q}\|_{\infty}=\max\{\|\widetilde{Q}_{jp}\|_{\infty}:j,p\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}\}=\max\{\widetilde{q}_{p}:p\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}\}.

(v)
At this final step, assuming
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
to be fixed, we show that
R_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega)
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
.
According to Lemma
2.1
(iii) with account of inclusion (
3.76
) and the fact that
\Omega_{k}
is a simply connected, closed and bounded set with a Lipshitz boundary, to show that
R_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega_{k})
it is sufficient to show uniform boundedness of the derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
in
\Omega_{k}
.

To this end, let us obtain formulas for
D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t):=\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
by differentiating formulas (
3.72
)–(
3.73
). Inclusions (
3.75
) and (
3.76
) allow us to apply standard rules of differentiation (for a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
). Applying the operator
\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}
to equations (
3.72
)–(
3.73
) yields for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
,

D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}\widetilde{Q}_{jk}(a_{jk}(x,t))-\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}Q_{jp}(u)\left(D_{2}R_{pk}^{(m-1)}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\,du\\
+\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}a_{jk}(x,t)\right)\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp}(a_{jk}(x,t))\cdot R_{pk}^{(m-1)}\left(a_{jk}(x,t),\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(a_{jk}(x,t))\right),\quad j\neq k,

(3.93)

D_{2}R_{kk}^{(m)}(x,t)=-\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int_{a_{kk}(x,t)}^{x}Q_{kp}(u)\left(D_{2}R_{pk}^{(m)}(u,\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u))\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(u)\,du\\
+\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}a_{kk}(x,t)\right)\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{kp}(a_{kk}(x,t))\cdot R_{pk}^{(m)}\left(a_{kk}(x,t),\gamma_{kk}^{x,t}(a_{kk}(x,t))\right),

(3.94)

for a.e.
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
. Equations (
3.93
)–(
3.94
) have exact same form as (
3.72
)–(
3.73
) with only three notable differences that do not prevent the application of successive approximation procedure used in the previous step:

1.

The “initial data” (out-of-integral term in (
3.93
)) now belongs to
L^{\infty}(\Omega_{k})
, which is implied by inclusions
a_{jk},\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\circ a_{jk}\in\Lip_{1}(\Omega_{k})
and
Q_{jp}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. This only changes the smoothness of approximations
D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}
to
L^{\infty}(\Omega_{k})
, but does not affect the proof in any way. The only difference is that the uniform limit of
D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}
will be also in
L^{\infty}(\Omega_{k})
instead of
C(\Omega_{k})
;

2.

The “initial data” now includes the term
R_{pk}^{(N)}
. When we estimate the difference
D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m+1)}-D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}
this term will generate the difference
R_{jk}^{(N+1)}-R_{jk}^{(N)}
(either with
N=m-1
or
N=m
), for which we already have the key estimate (
3.78
) and hence this can be handled properly during estimation;

3.

The coefficient
Q_{kp}(u)
of
R_{pk}(\ldots)
in (
3.67
) is replaced with
Q_{jp}(u)\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)
. It now depends on
x,t,u
, but inclusions
Q_{jp}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
