\gamma_{jk}^{\cdot,\cdot}(\cdot)\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
imply that it belongs to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Hence in the estimates for the difference
D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m+1)}-D_{2}R_{jk}^{(m)}
similar to (
3.84
), (
3.86
) we can still estimate this coefficient from above as before.

There remarks prove the absolute and uniform convergence of the series

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}D_{2}\left[R_{jk}^{(m)}-R_{jk}^{(m-1)}\right]=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}\left[R_{jk}^{(m)}-R_{jk}^{(m-1)}\right]

(3.95)

in
\Omega_{k}
, which implies in particular uniform boundedness of
\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
in
\Omega_{k}
over
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Applying the operator
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}
to equations (
3.72
)–(
3.73
) yields similar formula for
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
except in the integral we still have
D_{2}R_{pk}^{(N)}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u))
term (either with
N=m-1
or
N=m
). Hence uniform boundedness of
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, is implied by uniform boundedness of
\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}R_{jk}^{(m)}(x,t)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and Lipshitz and boundedness properties of the involved functions
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}
,
Q_{jp}
,
a_{jk}
,
\gamma_{jk}^{\cdot,\cdot}(\cdot)
.
Lemma
2.1
(iii) now finishes the proof.
The proof of uniqueness is proved by applying the Grönwall’s lemma and is omitted.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Let us prove properties (
3.48
)–(
3.1
) more formally.
First note that

\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\leqslant u,\quad u\in[x,a_{jk}(x,t)],\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell.

(3.96)

This follows from the definition (
3.41
) of
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(\cdot)
, relations
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(x)=t\leqslant x
and
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(a)=a
for
a=a_{jk}(x,t)
, and monotonicity of functions
\rho_{k}
and
\rho_{k}^{-1}
.
Further, since
\rho_{j}
and
\rho_{k}
are (absolutely) continuous and monotonous it follows that
\left\{\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t):\ \ 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell\right\}
is a smallest segment of the real line
\mathbb{R}
containing points
0
,
\rho_{j}(\ell)
and
\rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(\ell)
. I.e.

\left\{\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{k}(t):\ 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell\right\}=:[\rho_{jk}^{-},\rho_{jk}^{+}],

(3.97)

where

\rho_{jk}^{-}:=\min\{0,\ \rho_{j}(\ell),\ \rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(\ell)\}\leqslant 0,\qquad\rho_{jk}^{+}:=\max\{0,\ \rho_{j}(\ell),\ \rho_{j}(\ell)-\rho_{k}(\ell)\}\geqslant 0.

(3.98)

Since
(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}
is continuous and monotonous, it follows that
the set of all values
a_{jk}(x,t)
when
(x,t)
runs through
\Omega
is also a finite segment of
\mathbb{R}
,

\{a_{jk}(x,t):\ 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell\}=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}([\rho_{jk}^{-},\rho_{jk}^{+}])=:[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]\supset[0,\ell],

(3.99)

where the last inclusion follows from formulas (
3.98
) for
\rho_{jk}^{\pm}
. Namely, relations

a_{jk}(0,0)=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(0)=0,\qquad a_{jk}(\ell,0)=(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})^{-1}(\rho_{j}(\ell)),\qquad a_{jk}(\ell,\ell)=\ell,

imply inclusion
[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]\supset[0,\ell]
.
These formulas also imply one of the formula (
3.1
) for
a_{jk}^{\pm}
. Which of the cases we will have depends on the relation of
a_{jk}(\ell,0)
to the numbers
0
and
\ell
.

Observations (
3.96
) and (
3.99
) already imply boundedness of
\Omega_{jk}
from “three sides”,

\Omega_{jk}\subset\{(u,v):u\in[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}],v\leqslant u\}.

(3.100)

Let us fix
u\in[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]
and find the intersection of
\Omega_{k}
with the vertical line
\mathbf{L}_{u}:=\{(u,v):v\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
Since
u\in[a_{jk}^{-},a_{jk}^{+}]
, then by definition of
a_{jk}^{-}
and
a_{jk}^{+}
there exists
(x,t)\in\Omega
such that
a_{jk}(x,t)=u
. Hence
\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)=u
by definition of
a_{jk}(x,t)
, which implies that
(u,u)\in\Omega_{jk}\cup\mathbf{L}_{u}
. From general continuity and monotonicity reasoning it is clear that
\Omega_{jk}\cup\mathbf{L}_{u}
is a finite segment of the form
[\gamma_{jk}^{-}(u),u]
, where
\gamma_{jk}^{-}(\cdot)
satisfy one of the cases in (
3.1
).

Now we are ready to state the main result of this subsection which, in particular, states the similarity of the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
3.3
)–(
3.8
).
In particular, we assume that
Q_{kk}=0
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
.
Then the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=B(x)^{-1}\otimes D_{0}
are similar in

L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
,
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Moreover, there exists a bounded on
L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
triangular Volterra type operator
I+\mathcal{R}
,

(I+\mathcal{R})f=f(x)+\int^{x}_{0}R(x,t)B(t)f(t)\,dt,\qquad f\in L^{p}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}),

(3.101)

with a bounded inverse that intertwines the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
, i.e.

\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)(I+\mathcal{R})f=(I+\mathcal{R})\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)f,\qquad f\in\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=W^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(3.102)

Here
R(x,t)=(R_{jk}(x,t))_{j,k=1}^{r}
is the block-matrix kernel
of the operator
\mathcal{R}
that meets the condition

R\in\left(X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

(3.103)

where the function spaces
X_{1,0}(\Omega)
,
X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
are defined in Subsection
2.3
above.

Proof.

First, let us extend matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
to be defined on
\mathbb{R}
as it was done in the step (ii) of the proof of Proposition
3.2
. Let us show that under the assumption
Q\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
(see (
3.5
) and (
3.31
)) the integral system (
3.67
) has a solution
R
satisfying inclusion (
3.103
). It can be treated as a generalized solution to the problem (
3.68
)–(
3.70
).
For simplicity we restrict ourselves to the case
Q\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and
\widetilde{Q}_{jk}\in C(\mathbb{R})
,
j\neq k
(see (
3.5
) and (
3.12
))
General case is treated similarly to that of our treatment of Theorem 2.5 from
[
33
]
.

To this end, we choose a sequence of smooth finite matrix functions
\widetilde{Q}_{m}=(\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m})^{r}_{j,k=1}\in\Lip_{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
Q_{jj,m}\equiv 0
,
j\in\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
, that approximate finite matrix function
\widetilde{Q}=(\widetilde{Q}_{jk})^{r}_{j,k=1}\in C(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n\times n_{k}})
. Following (
3.29
)–(
3.30
) we set

\displaystyle Q_{jk,m}(x)

\displaystyle:=(\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{k}(x))\cdot\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\neq k,

(3.104)

\displaystyle Q_{jj,m}(x)

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{Q}_{jj,m}(x):=0,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.105)

It follows that there exists positive constants
C_{q}
and
C_{\widetilde{q}}
such that

\|\widetilde{Q}_{m}\|_{C(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C_{\widetilde{q}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\|Q_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C_{q},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.106)

Moreover

\|Q_{jk,m}-Q_{jk}\|_{\infty}\to 0,\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.107)

Let us fix
k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. In accordance with Proposition
3.2
, the system of equations (
3.67
) with
Q_{m}
in place of
Q
, has
\Lip_{1}
-smooth matrix block solution
(R_{jk,m})_{j=1}^{r}
, i.e. for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
and
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
we have,

R_{jk,m}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m}\bigl(a_{jk}(x,t)\bigr)-\int_{a_{jk}(x,t)}^{x}\sum^{r}_{p=1}Q_{jp,m}(u)R_{pk,m}\left(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\right)\,du.

(3.108)

To evaluate the difference
R_{jk,m}-R_{jk,s}
we set

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{jk,m,s}(x,t):=R_{jk,m}(x,t)-R_{jk,s}(x,t),\qquad\widehat{Q}_{jk,m,s}:=Q_{jk,m}-Q_{jk,s},

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{m,s}:=Q_{m}-Q_{s},\quad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m,s}:=\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m}-\widetilde{Q}_{jk,s},\qquad m,s\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.109)

Taking the difference of two equations (
3.108
) with
m=m
and
m=s

and using the notations (
3.1
) one easily
rewrites it in the form

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{jk,m,s}(x,t)=\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m,s}\bigl(a_{jk}(x,t)\bigr)-\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int^{x}_{a_{jk}}\widehat{Q}_{jp,m,s}(u)R_{pk,s}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\bigr)du

\displaystyle-\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int^{x}_{a_{jk}}Q_{jp,m}(u)\widehat{R}_{pk,m,s}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)\bigr)du,

(3.110)

where for brevity we set
a_{jk}:=a_{jk}(x,t)
.
Further, first we show that the family
\{R_{jk,m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}
-norm. Indeed, one gets from
(
3.92
) with account of definitions (
3.106
) that

\|R_{jk,m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{k})}\leqslant C_{jk}\cdot\exp\left(\tau_{k}\cdot|\rho_{k}(a_{k}^{+})-\rho_{k}(a_{k}^{-})|\right)=M_{jk},\quad m\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.111)

It follows from (
3.1
) with account of estimate (
3.111
)
and notations (
3.106
) that

\displaystyle|\widehat{R}_{jk,m,s}(x,t)|=\|\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m}-\widetilde{Q}_{jk,s}\|_{L^{\infty}}+M_{jk}\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int^{x}_{a_{jk}}|Q_{jp,m}(u)-Q_{jp,s}(u)|\,du

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int^{x}_{a_{jk}}|Q_{jp,m}(u)|\cdot|\widehat{R}_{pk,m,s}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)|\,du

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\widetilde{Q}_{jk,m}-\widetilde{Q}_{jk,s}\|_{L^{\infty}}+M_{jk}r\|Q_{m}-Q_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}}+C_{q}\sum_{p=1}^{r}\int^{x}_{a_{jk}}|\widehat{R}_{pk,m,s}(u,\gamma_{jk}^{x,t}(u)|\,du.

(3.112)

Emphasize that all the constants in (
3.1
) do not depend on
m,s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Applying the method of successive approximation to system of equations (
3.1
)
and repeating the reasoning from the step (iv) of the proof of Proposition
3.2
we arrive at the estimate similar to (
3.92
):

\displaystyle|\widehat{R}_{jk,m,s}(x,t)|\leqslant\|\widetilde{Q}_{m}-\widetilde{Q}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{1}\exp(C_{2}|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|),

(3.113)

with some
C_{1},C_{2}>0
that do not depend on
m,s,x,t,j,k
. (see (
3.111
) and recall the definition of
C_{jk}
).
It follows that the sequence of solutions
R_{jk,m}
to equations (
3.108
)
is a Cauchy sequence in
C(\Omega_{k})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}.
Therefore for any pair
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}

there exists a uniform limit
R_{jk}(x,t):=\lim_{s\to\infty}R_{jk,s}(x,t)
that meets the following
uniform in
(x,t)\in\Omega_{k}
estimate

\displaystyle|R_{jk}(x,t)-R_{jk,m}(x,t)|\leqslant\|\widetilde{Q}_{m}-\widetilde{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{1}\exp(C_{2}|\rho_{k}(x)-\rho_{k}(t)|).

(3.114)

Moreover, due to this estimate and since
\|\widetilde{Q}_{m}-\widetilde{Q}\|_{\infty}\to 0
and
\|Q_{m}-Q\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
, we can pass to the limit as
m\to\infty
in equations (
3.108
) to show that
R(x,t)=\{R_{jk}(x,t)\}_{j,k=1}^{r}
is a matrix solution to
system of integral equations (
3.67
) and define the Volterra operator
\mathcal{R}
with the matrix kernel

R(x,t)
.

On the other hand, by Proposition
3.2
, since
Q_{m}\in\Lip_{1}([a_{k}^{-},a_{k}^{+}];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
, the operator
I+\mathcal{R}_{m}
intertwines
the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q_{m})
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
, i.e.
equation (
3.102
) holds
with
Q_{m}
and
\mathcal{R}_{m}
in place of
Q
and
\mathcal{R}
, respectively,

\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q_{m})(I+R_{m})f=(I+R_{m})\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)f,\qquad f\in\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)=\widetilde{W}^{1,2}_{0}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(3.115)

Taking inverses we rewrite these equations in the form

(I+R_{m})^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q_{m}))^{-1}=(\mathcal{L}_{0}(0))^{-1}(I+R_{m})^{-1}.\qquad

(3.116)

Noting that
\mathcal{R}
is a Volterra operator, we can pass here to the limit as
m\to\infty
to arrive
to the equation

(I+\mathcal{R})^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q))^{-1}=(\mathcal{L}_{0}(0))^{-1}(I+\mathcal{R})^{-1}.\qquad

(3.117)

It follows that
I+\mathcal{R}
maps
\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
onto
\dom\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
. Therefore taking the inverses
in (
3.117
)
we arrive at identity (
3.102
). Since
0\in\rho(I+\mathcal{R})
,
this proves the similarity and completes the proof.

∎

3.2
Transformation operators

In this subsection we prove the existence of triangular transformation operators for equation (
3.1
). As in
[
41
]
our proof is substantially relies on the similarity result, Theorem
3.4
.

It is well known that the commutant
\{J\}^{\prime}
of the Volterra integration operator
J(J:\ f\to\int^{x}_{0}f(t)dt)
on
L^{2}[0,\ell]
consists of convolutions with distributions (see
[
53
]
and
[
40
]
). In particular,
a convolution operator
K:f\to k*f=\int_{0}^{x}k(x-t)f(t)dt
with
k\in L^{1}[0,\ell]

belongs to
\{J\}^{\prime}
.

We complete this subsection by a simple lemma on commutant of the operator

J\mathcal{B}
on
L^{2}[0,\ell]
with
\mathcal{B}:\ f\to b(t)f(t)
being
a multiplication operator. This result is a substantial ingredient in the proof of the existence of transformation
operators in the next subsection (see Theorem
3.7
).

Surprisingly, that partial differential equations technique is highly involved in
a description of the commutant which is not so explicit as in the case of
b_{0}\equiv\const
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
b\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell]
and let
b(\cdot)
be either positive or negative for a.e.
x\in[0,\ell]
and let

\mathcal{P}:\ f\to\int^{x}_{0}P(x,t)b(t)f(t)\,dt

(3.118)

be a Volterra operator with
P\in C(\Omega)
.
Then the operator
\mathcal{P}
commutes with the operator

J\mathcal{B}:\ f\to\int^{x}_{0}b(t)f(t)dt

(3.119)

if and only if the kernel
P(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by

P(x,t)=P(\xi(x,t),0).

(3.120)

Here
\xi(x,t)
is the implicit solution to the equation

\quad\rho(\xi(x,t))-\rho(x)+\rho(t)=0,\qquad\rho(x):=\int^{x}_{0}b(s)\,ds.

(3.121)

Proof.

Changing the order of integrals one easily gets

PJBf=\int^{x}P(x,s)b(s)\,ds\int^{s}b(t)f(t)\,ds=\int^{x}\left(\int^{x}_{t}P(x,s)b(s)\,ds\right)b(t)f(t)\,dt,

(3.122)

JBPf=\int^{x}b(s)\,ds\int^{s}P(s,t)b(t)f(t)\,dt=\int^{x}\left(\int^{x}_{t}b(s)P(s,t)\,ds\right)b(t)f(t)\,dt.

(3.123)

Equating these relations one concludes that the commutation relation
[P,JB]=0
is equivalent to

\int^{x}_{t}P(x,s)b(s)\,ds=\int^{x}_{t}b(s)P(s,t)\,ds.

(3.124)

Assume first that
P\in C^{1}(\Omega)
. Then
differentiating this equation with respect to
x
yields

P(x,x)b(x)+\int^{x}_{t}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}P(x,s)b(s)\,ds=b(x)P(x,t).

(3.125)

In turn, applying the operator
\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}
to this equation leads to
the first order partial differential equation

\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}P(x,t)b(t)+b(x)D_{t}P(x,t)=0.

(3.126)

Conversely, if the kernel
P\in C^{1}(\Omega)
and satisfies equation (
3.126
) we obtain by reversing the reasonings that the operator
\mathcal{P}
of the form (
3.118
) commutes with
J\mathcal{B}
, i.e.

P\in\{J\mathcal{B}\}^{\prime}
.

Next we extend the function
b(\cdot)
to the whole line
\mathbb{R}
preserving its
L^{\infty}
-norm and the
sign. In what follows we keep the notation
b(\cdot)
for this extension.

It is easily seen that in the coordinates
\{\xi,\eta\}
the characteristic of equation (
3.126
)
passing through the point
(x,t)
, is given by

\Gamma(\xi,\eta):\quad\rho(\eta)=\rho(\xi)-\rho(x)+\rho(t),\qquad\rho(\xi)=\int^{\xi}_{0}b(s)\,ds,

(3.127)

where
\rho
is also defined on the line.
The “explicit” form
\eta=\gamma(\xi;x,t)
of the characteristic is defined to be the unique solution to the equation

\Gamma(\xi,\gamma(\xi;x,t)=\rho(\gamma(\xi;x,t)-\rho(\xi)+\rho(x)-\rho(t)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma(x;x,t)=t.

(3.128)

Since
\rho
is monotone on
\mathbb{R}
, each characteristic is well defined on
\mathbb{R}
.

\eta(\xi)=\gamma(\xi;x,t):=\rho^{-1}\circ(\rho(\xi)-\rho(x)+\rho(t)),\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

Next we denote by
M(\xi(x,t),0)
the point of interaction of the characteristic

\eta=\gamma(\xi;x,t)
with the axis
\eta=0
. Then in accordance with (
3.128
)

\xi(x,t)
satisfies the equation

0=\rho(0)=\rho(\xi(x,t))-\rho(x)+\rho(t),

(3.129)

It follows that

\frac{\partial\xi(x,t)}{\partial x}=\frac{\rho^{\prime}(x)}{\rho^{\prime}\bigl(\xi(x,t)\bigr)}=\frac{\beta(x)}{\beta\bigl(\xi(x,t)\bigr)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\partial\xi(x,t)}{\partial t}=-\frac{\beta(t)}{\beta\bigl(\xi(x,t)\bigr)}.

(3.130)

In turn, using these relations imply that alongside
P(x,t)
the function

\widetilde{P}(x,t):=P(\xi(x,t),0)

(3.131)

satisfies the equation (
3.126
). Besides,
the identity
\xi(x,0)=x
yields

\widetilde{P}(x,0)=P(\xi(x,0),0)=P(x,0)=:g(x).

(3.132)

So, we have two solutions
\widetilde{P}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
P(\cdot,\cdot)

to the Cauchy problem (
3.126
), (
3.132
)
in
\Omega
.
However, since the slope of the characteristic
\gamma(\xi;x,t)
is positive,

\kappa(\xi_{0},\eta_{0})=\frac{d\gamma(\xi;x,t)}{d\xi}\big|_{\xi=\xi_{0}}=\frac{\beta(\xi_{0})}{\beta(\eta_{0})}=\frac{\beta(\xi_{0})}{\beta(\gamma_{jk}(\xi_{0};x,t))}>0,

(3.133)

the Cauchy problem (
3.126
), (
3.132
)
is not characteristic, and hence has the unique solution in
\Omega
, i.e.

\widetilde{P}(x,t)=P(x,t)
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that as a byproduct we proved that if
P\in\{J\mathcal{B}\}^{\prime}
and the kernel of
P
is smooth,
P\in C^{1}(\Omega),
then it is a solution to equation (
3.126
). If
P\in C(\Omega),
then it
is a generalized solution to equation (
3.126
).
A complete description of the commutant
\{J\mathcal{B}\}^{\prime}
will be published elsewhere.

Now we are ready to establish our main result on transformation operators for the equation (
3.1
).

Theorem 3.7
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
3.3
)–(
3.8
).
In particular, we assume that
Q_{jj}=0
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Further, let

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}\\
\cdots\\
A_{r}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n_{\min}},\qquad A_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{\min}},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.134)

where
n_{\min}:=\min\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{r}\}
, and let all matrices
A_{j}
be of maximal rank, i.e.
\rank(A_{j})=n_{\min}
.
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
. Further, let

Y_{A}(x,\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}(x,\lambda)\\
\cdots\\
Y_{r}(x,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad e_{A}(x,\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\lambda\rho_{1}(x)}A_{1}\\
\cdots\\
e^{i\lambda\rho_{r}(x)}A_{r}\end{pmatrix}

(3.135)

are the
n\times n_{\min}
block-matrix solutions to equations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), respectively, satisfying the initial conditions

Y_{A}(0,\lambda)=e_{A}(0,\lambda)=A.

(3.136)

Then solution
Y_{A}(x,\lambda)
admits a triangular representation

Y_{A}(x,\lambda)=(I+\mathcal{K}_{A})e_{A}(x,\lambda)=e_{A}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt,

(3.137)

where the block-matrix kernel
K_{A}=(K_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{r}
in the operator
\mathcal{K}_{A}
satisfies

K_{A}\in\left(X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n_{1}}.

(3.138)

Here domain
\Omega
and function spaces
X_{1,0}(\Omega)
,
X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
are defined in Subsection
2.3
above.

Remark 3.8
.

Note that Lemma
2.2
and inclusion
K_{A}\in X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n_{1}}
ensure that the traces
K(x,\cdot)
are well-defined and summable for each
x\in[0,\ell]
. In particular, end trace
K(\ell,\cdot)
is well defined. Hence formula (
3.137
) is well-defined and valid for each
x\in[0,\ell]
. A more relaxed inclusion
K_{A}\in X_{\infty}(\Omega)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
would only yield this formula for a.e
x\in[0,\ell]
.

Sketch of the proof.

Assume for definiteness that
n_{\min}=n_{1}
.

(i)
At this step assuming the validity of representation (
3.137
) with
K_{A}\in C^{1}(\Omega)

we indicate the boundary value problem for the kernel
K_{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
Inserting representation (
3.137
)
into equation (
3.1
) we obtain

Y^{\prime}(x,\lambda)+Q(x)Y(x,\lambda)=e^{\prime}_{A}(x,\lambda)+K_{A}(x,x)B(x)e_{A}(x,\lambda)\\
+\int^{x}_{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt+Q(x)e_{A}(x,\lambda)+Q(x)\cdot\int^{x}_{0}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt\\
=e^{\prime}_{A}(x,\lambda)+\bigl(K_{A}(x,x)B(x)+Q(x)\bigr)e_{A}(x,\lambda)\\
+\int_{0}^{x}\bigl(\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}K_{A}(x,t)+Q(x)K_{A}(x,t)\bigr)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt

(3.139)

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.137
) after integrating by parts that

\displaystyle i\lambda B(x)Y(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=i\lambda B(x)e_{A}(x,\lambda)+i\lambda B(x)\int^{x}_{0}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}_{A}(x,\lambda)+B(x)\int^{x}_{0}K_{A}(x,t)e_{A}^{\prime}(t,\lambda)\,dt

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}_{A}(x,\lambda)+B(x)K_{A}(x,x)e_{A}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-B(x)K_{A}(x,0)e_{A}(0,\lambda)-B(x)\int^{x}_{0}D_{t}\bigl(K_{A}(x,t)\bigr)e_{A}(t,\lambda)\,dt.

(3.140)

Equating both sides of this identities we arrive at the following boundary value problem for the
n\times n
-matrix kernel
K_{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
:

D_{x}K_{A}(x,t)B(t)+B(x)D_{t}K_{A}(x,t)+Q(x)K_{A}(x,t)B(t)=0,

(3.141)

B(x)K_{A}(x,x)-K_{A}(x,x)B(x)=Q(x),

(3.142)

K_{A}(x,0)A=0.

(3.143)

Writing the kernel
K_{A}
in the block-matrix form
K_{A}=\left(K_{jk}\right)_{j,k=1}^{r}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
and using the block-matrix form of
Q
we rewrite the problem (
3.141
)–(
3.143
)
in the following form

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(t)D_{x}K_{jk}(x,t)+\beta_{j}(x)D_{t}K_{jk}(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\sum^{r}_{p=1}\beta_{k}(t)Q_{jp}(x)K_{pk}(x,t),\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.144)

\displaystyle K_{jk}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{jk}(x)}{\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{k}(x)},\qquad j\neq k,\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(3.145)

\displaystyle\sum^{r}_{k=1}K_{jk}(x,0)A_{k}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.146)

Emphasise that formula (
3.145
) has sense due to the conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.7
).

Conversely, reversing the reasonings one proves that any
C^{1}
-solution to the problem (
3.144
)–(
3.146
) generates representation (
3.137
).
So, to prove the result it suffices to show the solvability of the problem (
3.144
)–(
3.146
).

(ii)
At this step we prove the solvability.
To construct a solution
K_{A}
to the problem (
3.144
)–(
3.146
)
we use a solution
R(x,t)
to the problem
(
3.144
)–(
3.145
),
constructed in Theorem
3.4
. Besides, we introduce a Volterra operator

P=\bigoplus_{1}^{r}P_{j},\quad\text{where}\quad P_{j}:\ f_{j}\to\int^{x}_{0}P_{j}(x,t)B_{j}(t)f_{j}(t)\,dt,\qquad f_{j}\in L^{2}\bigl([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}\bigr).

(3.147)

Moreover, we assume that
P_{j}
has a smooth kernel
P_{j}\in C^{1}((\Omega);\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{j}})
and
satisfies

[P_{j},J\otimes B_{j}]=[P_{j},\left(J\beta_{j}(t)\right)\otimes I_{n_{j}}]=0,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

i.e.
P_{j}\in\{J\otimes B_{j}\}^{\prime}
.

Starting with the operator
I+\mathcal{R}
constructed in Theorem
3.4
, we define
the operator
I+\mathcal{K}_{A}
as the product of two operators:

I+\mathcal{K}_{A}:=(I+\mathcal{R})(I+\mathcal{P}).

(3.148)

In terms of the kernels of integral operators
\mathcal{K}_{A}
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
\mathcal{P}
, equality (
3.148
)
can be rewritten as

K_{A}(x,t)=R(x,t)+P(x,t)+\int^{x}_{t}R(x,s)B(s)P(s,t)\,ds.

(3.149)

In fact, identity (
3.148
) is equivalent to (
3.149
) after multiplying the last equality
by the factor
B(t)
from the right. This factor is canceled in (
3.149
) because the matrix
B(t)
is non-singular for every
t\in[0,\ell]
.

Since the operators
I+\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
commutes, the operator
I+\mathcal{K}_{A}
intertwines the operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(0)
alongside the operator
I+\mathcal{R}
, i.e. identity (
3.102
) holds with
I+\mathcal{K}_{A}
in place of
I+\mathcal{R}
. Therefore Theorem
3.4
(sufficiency) applies and ensures that the kernel
K_{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
is also a solution to the problem (
3.144
)–(
3.145
). To complete the proof it suffices to find a kernel
P(\cdot,\cdot)
in such a way that
K_{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
meets the condition (
3.146
). To this end we insert the right hand side of equality (
3.149
) in (
3.146
) and obtain

P_{j}(x,0)A_{j}+\sum^{r}_{k=1}\left[R_{jk}(x,0)+\int^{x}_{0}R_{jk}(x,s)B_{k}(s)P_{k}(s,0)\,ds\right]A_{k}=0,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.150)

Rewriting this equality as

(I+\mathcal{R})\begin{pmatrix}P_{1}(x,0)A_{1}\\
\ldots\ldots\\
P_{r}(x,0)A_{r}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{R}_{1}(x,0)\\
\ldots\ldots\\
\widetilde{R}_{r}(x,0)\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\widetilde{R}_{j}(x,0):=-\sum^{r}_{k=1}R_{jk}(x,0)A_{k}

(3.151)

we find the unique solution

\begin{pmatrix}P_{1}(x,0)A_{1}\\
\ldots\ldots\\
P_{r}(x,0)A_{r}\end{pmatrix}=(I+\mathcal{R})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{R}_{1}(x,0)\\
\ldots\ldots\\
\widetilde{R}_{r}(x,0)\end{pmatrix}\,=:\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{g}_{1}(x)\\
\ldots\ldots\\
\widetilde{g}_{r}(x)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.152)

In turn, due to the condition
\rank(A_{j})=n_{\min}=n_{1}
, meaning that
each matrix
A_{j}
is of the maximal rank, there exists (in general, non-unique)
matrix solution
\{g_{j}(x)\}_{j=1}^{r}:=\{P_{j}(x,0)\}_{j=1}^{r}
to the system (
3.152
),
i.e.
g_{j}A_{j}=\widetilde{g}_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}
.
A solution
\{P_{j}(x,0)\}_{j=1}^{r}
is definitely unique whenever
n_{1}=n_{2}=\ldots=n_{r}
,
and hence
\det A_{j}\neq 0
.

Further, to find a system of matrix functions
\{P_{j}(x,t)\}_{j=1}^{r}
we apply Lemma (
3.5
).
In accordance with this lemma each
P_{j}(x,t)
is a solution to the following Cauchy problem

\displaystyle D_{x}P_{j}(x,t)B_{j}(t)+B_{j}(x)D_{t}P_{j}(x,t)=0,

(3.153)

\displaystyle P_{j}(x,0)=g_{j}(x),\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(3.154)

and is given by (
3.120
) with
P_{j}(x,t)
in place of
P(x,t)
, i.e.

P_{j}(x,t)=P_{j}(\xi_{j}(x,t),0),\qquad P_{j}(\xi_{j}(x,0),0)=P_{j}(x,0).

Here
\xi_{j}(x,t)
is a solution to the equation

\quad\rho_{j}(\xi_{j}(x,t))-\rho_{j}(x)+\rho_{j}(t)=0,\qquad\rho_{j}(x):=\int^{x}_{0}b_{j}(s)\,ds.

(3.155)

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

(i)
For Dirac
2\times 2
system
(B=\diag(-1,1))
with continuous
Q
the triangular transformation operators have been constructed in
[
29
, Ch.10.3]
and
[
44
, Ch.1.2]
. For
Q\in(L^{1}[0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
it is proved in
[
3
]
by an appropriate generalization of Marchenko’s method.

(ii) Let
J:f\to\int_{0}^{x}f(t)dt
denote the Volterra integration operator on
L^{p}[0,1]
. Note that the similarity of integral Volterra operators given by (
2.13
) to the simplest Volterra operator of the form
B\otimes J
acting in the spaces
L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2})
has been investigated in
[
41
,
56
]
. The technique of investigation of integral equations for the kernels of transformation operators in the spaces
X_{\infty,1}(\Omega)
and
X_{1,1}(\Omega)
goes back to the paper
[
39
]
.

4
Fundamental matrix solution

In this section we apply results of the previous section to obtain an important representation for the fundamental matrix solution of equation (
1.1
) and its minors, involving Fourier transform of kernels from the transformation operators.

4.1
Preliminaries

For reader’s convenience, recall the main equation (
1.1
) and its version with zero potential:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(Q)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}(y^{\prime}+Q(x)y)=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell],

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}y:=\mathcal{L}(0)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}y^{\prime}=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.2)

Let us recall definitions (
1.3
)–(
1.4
) of matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)

B=\diag(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}),\qquad\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.3)

is a self-adjoint invertible diagonal summable matrix function, and

Q=(Q_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n},\qquad Q_{jk}\in L^{1}[0,\ell]:=L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}),\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.4)

is a summable (generally non-self-adjoint) potential matrix.

Throughout this section and many results in sections below we will assume conditions (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) on entries of the matrix function
B(\cdot)
. Namely, we assume that

\displaystyle\beta_{k},1/\beta_{k}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell],\qquad s_{k}:=\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))\equiv\const\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n_{-}}(x)<0<\beta_{n_{-}+1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n}(x),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(4.6)

and there exists
\theta>0
such that for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n-1}\}

\text{either}\quad\beta_{k}\equiv\beta_{k+1}\quad\text{or}\quad\beta_{k}(x)+\theta<\beta_{k+1}(x),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.7)

Here
n_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
is the number of negative functions among
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
. Let us also set
n_{+}:=n-n_{-}
. See Remark
4.1
for some discussion about these conditions.

Throughout this section we will also assume the following “zero block diagonality” condition on entries of the matrix function
Q(\cdot)
,

Q_{jk}\equiv 0\quad\text{whenever}\quad\beta_{j}\equiv\beta_{k},\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.8)

In particular
Q_{jj}\equiv 0
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
.

Remark 4.1
.

(i)
Note, that as opposed to the previous section, we work with notation (
1.3
) for
B(x)
.
To avoid confusion, we rewrite block-matrix decomposition (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) as

B=:\diag(\widetilde{\beta}_{1}I_{n_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{\beta}_{r}I_{n_{r}}),\qquad n_{1}+\ldots+n_{r}=n.

(4.9)

It is clear that

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\equiv\ldots\equiv\beta_{n_{1}}\equiv\widetilde{\beta}_{1},

(4.10)

\displaystyle\beta_{n_{1}+1}\equiv\ldots\equiv\beta_{n_{1}+n_{2}}\equiv\widetilde{\beta}_{2},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ldots

\displaystyle\beta_{n-n_{r}+1}\equiv\ldots\equiv\beta_{n}\equiv\widetilde{\beta}_{r}.

(4.12)

These relations imply that conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) on functions
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
from representation (
4.3
) are equivalent to conditions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) on functions
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}
from representation (
3.3
)–(
3.4
).

Note also that relations (
4.10
)–(
4.12
) and condition (
4.7
) imply that condition (
4.8
) on
Q
means that
Q
has zero block diagonal with respect to decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
.

(ii)
Sometimes it is useful to work with the matrix function
B(\cdot)
without ordering its entries (see e.g. (
10.12
)). Let us reformulate conditions (
4.6
)–(
4.7
) for such general “unordered” case. Namely, it is easy to verify that equivalent form of these conditions is the following,

\displaystyle\text{there exists}\ \ \theta>0\ \ \text{such that for each}\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\ \ \text{the following condition holds},

(4.13)

\displaystyle\text{either}\quad\nu_{jk}:=\beta_{j}-\beta_{k}\equiv 0,\quad\text{or}\quad\nu_{jk}(x)>\theta,\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\text{or}\quad\nu_{jk}(x)<-\theta,\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.14)

Further, we set

\rho_{k}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{k}(t)dt\quad\text{and}\quad b_{k}:=\rho_{k}(\ell)\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.15)

Going forward for
u\leqslant v
, notations
[u,v]
and
[v,u]
will mean the same segment of real line and will be used interchangeably. It follows from (
4.5
) that

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\rho_{n_{-}}(x)<0<\rho_{n_{-}+1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\rho_{n}(x),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(4.16)

\displaystyle b_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n_{-}}<0<b_{n_{-}+1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\rho_{k}\in\Lip[0,\ell],\quad\rho_{k}^{-1}\in\Lip[0,b_{k}],\quad\rho_{k},\rho_{k}^{-1}\ \ \text{are strictly monotonous},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.18)

where
\rho_{k}^{-1}
denotes the function inverse to
\rho_{k}
on the segment
[0,\ell]
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Here
\Lip[0,\alpha]
is the class of functions
f:[0,\alpha]\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|f(x)-f(y)|\leqslant C|x-y|
,
x,y\in[0,\alpha]
, for some
C>0
. As per remark above,
[0,\alpha]=[\alpha,0]
if
\alpha<0
.

Next, we introduce the fundamental matrices
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
and
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
as the solutions to the equations
\mathcal{L}(Q)\Phi=\lambda\Phi
and
\mathcal{L}(0)\Phi_{0}=\lambda\Phi_{0}
, respectively, satisfying the initial condition
\Phi(0,\lambda)=\Phi_{0}(0,\lambda)=I_{n}
. Clearly,

\displaystyle\Phi(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{1}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\Phi_{n}(x,\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Phi_{p}(x,\lambda)=\col(\varphi_{jp}(x,\lambda),\ldots,\varphi_{np}(x,\lambda)),

(4.19)

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{1}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\Phi^{0}_{n}(x,\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Phi^{0}_{k}(x,\lambda)=e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}\col\left(\delta_{1k},\ldots,\delta_{nk}\right).

(4.20)

Note in this connection, that the matrix equation
\mathcal{L}(Q)\Phi=\lambda\Phi
is equivalent to

\Phi^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=(i\lambda B(x)-Q(x))\Phi(x,\lambda),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(4.21)

where
i\lambda B(\cdot)-Q(\cdot)
is a summable function on
[0,\ell]
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. General theory of ODE implies the existence of global solution
\Phi(x,\lambda)
on segment
[0,\ell]
, such that
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
\Phi(x,\cdot)
is an entire function for each
x\in[0,\ell]
. Moreover, Liouville’s formula (see (
4.49
) below) implies that
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)^{-1}\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

4.2
Key identities for fundamental matrix

In the following proposition we relate the columns of fundamental matrices
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
and
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
) and let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then, there exist vector kernels

R_{q}^{[p]}\in\left(X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n},\qquad q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.22)

such that the following representation holds

\Phi_{p}(x,\lambda)=\Phi_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+\sum_{q=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{x}R_{q}^{[p]}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}\beta_{q}(t)\,dt,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.23)

Proof.

It is clear that matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy assumptions of Theorem
3.7
. For simplicity let’s assume that
r=n
and
n_{1}=\ldots=n_{r}=1
in block-matrix decomposition (
3.3
)–(
3.4
). Then notations (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) and (
4.3
) coincide. Let
\widetilde{A}
be some invertible matrix with non-zero entries:

\widetilde{A}=(a_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n},\qquad\det(\widetilde{A})\neq 0,\qquad a_{jk}\neq 0,\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.24)

E.g. one can set
a_{jk}:=j^{k}
to obtain invertible Vandermonde matrix with non-zero entries. Denote by
A^{[k]}
, the
k
-th column of
\widetilde{A}
:

\widetilde{A}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{[1]}&\ldots&A^{[n]}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A^{[k]}=\col(a_{1k},\ldots,a_{nk})\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.25)

It is clear, that for a given
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,
n\times 1
matrix
A=A^{[k]}
satisfy conditions of Theorem
3.7
. Hence triangular representation (
3.137
) takes place with some

K_{A^{[k]}}=:K^{[k]}=:\bigl(K_{jp}^{[k]}\bigr)_{j,p=1}^{n}\in\left(X_{1,0}(\Omega)\cap X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.26)

Further, note that due to Cauchy uniqueness theorem,

Y_{A^{[k]}}(x,\lambda)=\Phi(x,\lambda)A^{[k]},\qquad e_{A^{[k]}}(x,\lambda)=\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)A^{[k]},\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(4.27)

where
Y_{A}(x,\lambda)
and
e_{A}(x,\lambda)
for
A=A^{[k]}
are defined in (
3.135
)–(
3.136
). Inserting (
4.27
) into (
3.137
) we arrive at

\Phi(x,\lambda)A^{[k]}=\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)A^{[k]}+\int^{x}_{0}K^{[k]}(x,t)B(t)\Phi_{0}(t,\lambda)A^{[k]}\,dt,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.28)

Formulas (
4.28
) and (
4.25
) now imply

\Phi(x,\lambda)\widetilde{A}=\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)\widetilde{A}+\int^{x}_{0}\left(K^{[k]}(x,t)B(t)\Phi_{0}(t,\lambda)A^{[k]}\right)_{k=1}^{n}\,dt.

(4.29)

Let
\widetilde{A}^{-1}=(\alpha_{kp})_{k,p=1}^{n}
. Then with account of notations (
4.26
), (
4.3
), (
4.20
) and (
4.25
) we have for
0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant 1
:

\left(K^{[k]}(x,t)B(t)\Phi_{0}(t,\lambda)A^{[k]}\right)_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{A}^{-1}=\left(\sum_{q=1}^{n}K_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)\beta_{q}(t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}a_{qk}\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}\cdot(\alpha_{kp})_{k,p=1}^{n}\\
=\left(\sum_{k,q=1}^{n}K_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)\beta_{q}(t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}a_{qk}\alpha_{kp}\right)_{j,p=1}^{n}=\left(\sum_{q=1}^{n}R_{jq}^{[p]}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}\beta_{q}(t)\right)_{j,p=1}^{n},

(4.30)

where we set

R_{jq}^{[p]}(x,t):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}K_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)a_{qk}\alpha_{kp},\qquad p,j,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant 1.

(4.31)

Multiplying (
4.29
) by
\widetilde{A}^{-1}
from the right with account of (
4.30
) and (
4.31
) and taking
p
-th column in the resulting matrix equation, we arrive at the desired formula (
4.23
) by setting

R_{q}^{[p]}:=\col\bigl(R_{1q}^{[p]},\ldots,R_{nq}^{[p]}\bigr)_{j=1}^{n}.

(4.32)

Desired inclusion (
4.22
) follows from inclusion (
4.26
) and formula (
4.31
).

The general case can be treated as follows. Start with the matrix
\widetilde{A}
as above. For each column
A^{[k]}
of
\widetilde{A}
extend it be an
n\times n_{\min}
matrix
A
with blocks of maximal rank. After applying Theorem
3.7
, pick only the first column of the solutions
Y_{A}(x,\lambda)
and
e_{A}(x,\lambda)
as
Y_{A^{[k]}}(x,\lambda)
and
e_{A^{[k]}}(x,\lambda)
, respectively.
∎

To study the integrals appearing in formula (
4.23
), we need the following generalization of Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma for space
X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\beta\in L^{\infty}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R})
and
\beta
do not change sign on
[0,\ell]
.
Set
\rho(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\beta(t)dt
. Let
R\in X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
. Then for any
\delta>0
there exists
R_{\delta}>0
such that

\left|{\int_{0}^{x}R(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}\beta(t)dt}\right|<\delta\cdot\left(\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(x)}}\right|+1\right),\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\delta},\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.33)

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By the definition of the space
X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
, the inclusion
R\in X_{\infty,0}(\Omega)
ensures that there exists
R_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\|R-R_{\varepsilon}\|_{X_{\infty}}=\esssup_{x\in[0,\ell]}\int_{0}^{x}\left|{R(x,t)-R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)}\right|dt<\varepsilon.

(4.34)

In particular, we get the following uniform estimate

\left|{\int_{0}^{x}\left(R(x,t)-R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\right)e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}\beta(t)dt}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon\cdot\|\beta\|_{\infty}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,x]}\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}}\right|,\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.35)

Since
R_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\Omega)
,
\rho\in\AC[0,\ell]
and
\rho^{\prime}=\beta
,
integrating by parts we get for
x\in[0,\ell]
and
\lambda\neq 0
,

\left|{\int_{0}^{x}R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}\beta(t)\,dt}\right|=\left|{\int_{0}^{x}\frac{R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)}{i\lambda}\,d\left(e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}\right)}\right|=\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\left|{\int_{0}^{x}e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\,dt}\right|\\
\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,x]}\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}}\right|\cdot\max_{t\in[0,x]}\left|{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R_{\varepsilon}(x,t)}\right|\leqslant\frac{\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{1}(\Omega)}}{|\lambda|}\cdot\max_{t\in[0,x]}\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}}\right|.

(4.36)

Since function
\rho(\cdot)
is real-valued and strictly monotonous on
[0,\ell]
, and
\rho(0)=0
, it follows that

\max_{t\in[0,x]}\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(t)}}\right|=\max\{\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(x)}}\right|,1\}<\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho(x)}}\right|+1.

(4.37)

Setting

\varepsilon=\frac{\delta}{2\|\beta\|_{\infty}},\qquad R_{\delta}=\frac{2}{\delta}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{1}(\Omega)},

(4.38)

and combining estimates (
4.35
)–(
4.37
) we arrive at the desired estimate (
4.33
).
∎

Going forward, for
h\geqslant 0
we will denote by
\Pi_{h}
a horizontal strip of semi-width
h
symmetrical with respect to the real line:

\Pi_{h}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\Im\lambda|\leqslant h\},\qquad\Pi_{0}:=\mathbb{R}.

(4.39)

Corollary 4.4
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
h\geqslant 0
and
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then the following uniform asymptotic formula holds:

\Phi_{p}(x,\lambda)=\Phi_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+o(1),\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h}.

(4.40)

Proof.

Proposition
4.2
implies representation (
4.23
). Due to condition (
4.22
), Lemma
4.3
implies the following uniform at
x\in[0,\ell]
asymptotic behavior for integrals in (
4.23
):

\int_{0}^{x}R_{q}^{[p]}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{p}(t)}\beta_{p}(t)\,dt=o\left(\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(x)}}\right|+1\right),\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\qquad q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.41)

Since functions
\rho_{q}(\cdot)
are real-valued and bounded, these estimates imply that all integrals in (
4.23
) are
o(1)
uniformly at
x\in[0,\ell]
as
\lambda\to\infty
and
\lambda\in\Pi_{h}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

The following representation will be useful for studying characteristic determinant of the BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
). Let us set

\rho_{1}^{-}(x)=\min\{\rho_{1}(x),0\},\qquad\rho_{n}^{+}(x)=\max\{\rho_{n}(x),0\},\qquad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.42)

It follow from (
4.16
) that

\rho_{1}^{-}(x)\leqslant 0\leqslant\rho_{n}^{+}(x),\qquad[0,\rho_{q}(x)]\subset[\rho_{1}^{-}(x),\rho_{n}^{+}(x)],\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(4.43)

Here
[0,\rho_{q}(x)]
means
[\rho_{q}(x),0]
if
\rho_{q}(x)<0
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then there exists a measurable vector kernel
\widetilde{R}_{p}
defined on
\widetilde{\Omega}:=\{(x,u):x\in[0,\ell],u\in[\rho_{1}^{-}(x),\rho_{n}^{+}(x)]\}
such that for each
x\in[0,\ell]
, a trace function
\widetilde{R}_{p}(x,\cdot)
is well-defined, summable,

\sup_{x\in[0,\ell]}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}\left\|{\widetilde{R}_{p}(x,u)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\,du<\infty,

(4.44)

and the following representation holds

\Phi_{p}(x,\lambda)=\Phi_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}\widetilde{R}_{p}(x,u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.45)

Proof.

Proposition
4.2
implies representation (
4.23
). Let
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed and consider the integral in (
4.23
) that contains
R_{q}^{[p]}(x,t)
. Condition (
4.18
) allows us to make a change of variable
u=\rho_{q}(t)
in this integral, which with account of (
4.43
) yields

\int_{0}^{x}R_{q}^{[p]}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}\beta_{q}(t)\,dt=\int_{0}^{\rho_{q}(x)}R_{q}^{[p]}(x,\rho_{q}^{-1}(u))e^{i\lambda u}\,du=\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}\widetilde{R}_{q}^{[p]}(x,u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du,

(4.46)

where

\widetilde{R}_{q}^{[p]}(x,u)=\begin{cases}R_{q}^{[p]}(x,\rho_{q}^{-1}(u)),&u\in[0,\rho_{q}(x)],\\
0,&u\in[\rho_{1}^{-}(x),\rho_{n}^{+}(x)]\setminus[0,\rho_{q}(x)].\end{cases}

(4.47)

Inserting (
4.46
) into (
4.23
) we arrive at the desired formula (
4.45
) with
{\widetilde{R}_{p}:=\widetilde{R}_{1}^{[p]}+\ldots+\widetilde{R}_{n}^{[p]}}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Using Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma, one can prove that for each
x\in[0,\ell]
, the integral in the representation (
4.45
) tends to zero as
\lambda\to\infty
,
\lambda\in\Pi_{h}
(without using property (
4.44
)). Note, however, that to prove this convergence
uniformly
at
x\in[0,\ell]
, property (
4.44
) of the vector kernel
\widetilde{R}_{p}
from representation (
4.45
) is not sufficient by itself. Which is why in Corollary
4.4
we used more suitable representation (
4.23
) where vector kernels
R_{q}^{[p]}
known to be approximated by functions from
C^{1}(\Omega)
.

4.3
Generalization of Liouville’s formula

A classical Liouville’s formula applied to the fundamental matrix solution
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
of equation (
4.1
) yields

\frac{d}{dx}\det\Phi(x,\lambda)=\tr(i\lambda B(x)-Q(x))\cdot\det\Phi(x,\lambda),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(4.48)

which in turn implies

\det\Phi(x,\lambda)=\exp\left(i\lambda\int_{0}^{x}\tr B(t)\,dt-\int_{0}^{x}\tr Q(t)\,dt\right),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.49)

If matrix function
Q(\cdot)
satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
), then formula (
4.49
) simplifies,

\det\Phi(x,\lambda)=\exp\left(i\lambda\int_{0}^{x}\tr B(t)\,dt\right)=\exp(i\lambda(\rho_{1}(x)+\ldots+\rho_{n}(x))),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.50)

Our goal is to obtain similar relation for minors of the fundamental matrix
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
. It appears, that the set of minors of a given size with a fixed set of columns considered as a vector function in certain
\mathbb{C}^{N}
satisfies equation similar to (
4.1
). After that, applying previous considerations allows us to obtain integral representation for these minors, which will be crucial to effectively study characteristic determinant of BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).

Throughout this subsection we extensively use notation (
2.22
) for the set
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
and notation (
2.23
) for a minor
\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}]
. Additionally we set for
x\in[0,\ell]
and
m\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\beta_{q_{1}}(x)+\ldots+\beta_{q_{m}}(x),\qquad\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\in\mathfrak{P}_{m},\quad 1\leqslant q_{1}<\ldots<q_{m}\leqslant n,

(4.51)

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{q_{1}}(x)+\ldots+\rho_{q_{m}}(x),\qquad\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(4.52)

\displaystyle\tau_{m}^{-}(x)

\displaystyle:=\min\{\rho_{1}(x)+\ldots+\rho_{m}(x),0\}=\min\left(\{\rho_{\mathfrak{q}}(x):\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}\}\cup\{0\}\right),

(4.53)

\displaystyle\tau_{m}^{+}(x)

\displaystyle:=\max\{\rho_{n-m+1}(x)+\ldots+\rho_{n}(x),0\}=\max\left(\{\rho_{\mathfrak{q}}(x):\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}\}\cup\{0\}\right).

(4.54)

Proposition 4.7
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
m\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
. Then there exists a measurable scalar kernel
R_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}
defined on
\Omega_{m}:=\{(x,u):x\in[0,\ell],u\in[\tau_{m}^{-}(x),\tau_{m}^{+}(x)]\}
such that for each
x\in[0,\ell]
, a trace function
R_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}(x,\cdot)
is well-defined, summable,

\sup_{x\in[0,\ell]}\int_{\tau_{m}^{-}(x)}^{\tau_{m}^{+}(x)}\left|{R_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}(x,u)}\right|\,du<\infty,

(4.55)

and the following representation holds

\Phi(x,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]=\delta_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}\exp\left(i\lambda\rho_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\right)+\int_{\tau_{m}^{-}(x)}^{\tau_{m}^{+}(x)}R_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}(x,u)e^{i\lambda u}du,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(4.56)

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
be fixed for the entire proof. This allows us to set for brevity

f_{\mathfrak{q}}(x):=\Phi(x,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}],\qquad q\in\mathfrak{P}_{m},\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.57)

Further, set
N:=N_{m}:=\card\mathfrak{P}_{m}=\binom{n}{m}
and order all elements of
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
in some way,
\mathfrak{P}_{m}=\{\mathfrak{q}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{q}_{N}\}
. Consider

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\cdot,\lambda):=\col\bigl(\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]\bigr)_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}:=\col\bigl(f_{\mathfrak{q}_{1}}(\cdot),\ldots,f_{\mathfrak{q}_{N}}(\cdot)\bigr)

(4.58)

as a vector function in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. First, note that

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(0,\lambda):=\col\bigl(I_{n}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]\bigr)_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}=\col\bigl(\delta_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}}\bigr)_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}},

(4.59)

since
\bigl(I_{n}[\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}]=1
and
\bigl(I_{n}[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]=0
if
\mathfrak{q}\neq\mathfrak{p}
.

Let us show that vector function
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\cdot,\lambda)
satisfy the following first order system of ODE:

\mathfrak{F}_{p}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=i\lambda\mathfrak{B}(x)\mathfrak{F}_{p}(x,\lambda)-\mathfrak{Q}(x)\mathfrak{F}_{p}(x,\lambda),\qquad\mathfrak{B}(x):=\diag\bigl(\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\bigr)_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}},\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(4.60)

where
\mathfrak{Q}(x)=\bigl(\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}}(x)\bigr)_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}=\bigl(\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathfrak{q}_{j},\mathfrak{q}_{k}}(x)\bigr)_{j,k=1}^{N}
is summable
N\times N
matrix function with zero block diagonal with respect to natural block decomposition of the matrix
\mathfrak{B}(x)
. Namely,

\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}}\equiv 0\quad\text{whenever}\quad\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}\equiv\beta_{\mathfrak{r}}\quad\text{for}\quad\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(4.61)

Emphasize, that each entry of the matrix function
\mathfrak{B}(\cdot)
is a sum of some subset of the entries of the original matrix
B(x)

with different indexes
.

To prove (
4.60
)–(
4.61
), note that the matrix equation (
4.21
) has the following scalar form

\varphi_{q,p}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=i\lambda\beta_{q}(x)\varphi_{q,p}(x,\lambda)-\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{q,s}(x)\varphi_{s,p}(x,\lambda),\qquad q,p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.62)

Let
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
be fixed,
1\leqslant q_{1}<\ldots<q_{m}\leqslant n
. Using standard formula for the derivative of the determinant we get

\displaystyle f_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dx}\det(\varphi_{q_{j},\,p_{k}}(x,\lambda))_{j,k=1}^{m}=\frac{d}{dx}\det\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{q_{1},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{1},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\varphi_{q_{m},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{m},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\det\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{q_{1},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{1},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\vdots&\ldots&\vdots\\
\varphi_{q_{j-1},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{j-1},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\varphi_{q_{j},\,p_{1}}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{j},\,p_{m}}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)\\
\varphi_{q_{j+1},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{j+1},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\vdots&\ldots&\vdots\\
\varphi_{q_{m},\,p_{1}}(x,\lambda)&\ldots&\varphi_{q_{m},\,p_{m}}(x,\lambda)\\
\end{pmatrix}

(4.63)

Formula (
4.62
) implies the following relations connecting rows of the minor
\Phi(x,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]
,

\bigl(\varphi_{q_{j},\,p_{k}}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)\bigr)_{k=1}^{m}=i\lambda\beta_{q_{j}}(x)\cdot\bigl(\varphi_{q_{j},\,p_{k}}(x,\lambda)\bigr)_{k=1}^{m}-\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{q_{j},\,s}(x)\cdot\bigl(\varphi_{s,\,p_{k}}(x,\lambda)\bigr)_{k=1}^{m},\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.64)

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
denote by
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
a sequence one obtains from
\mathfrak{q}
by replacing
j
-th element
q_{j}
with
s
, i.e.

\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s):=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{j-1},s,q_{j+1},\ldots,q_{m}).

(4.65)

Not that
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
is not necessarily an element of
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
, but notation
A[\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s),\mathfrak{p}]
is still valid. Note also that it is possible for
s
to be equal to one of
q_{k}
,
k\neq j
. In this case minor
A[\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s),\mathfrak{p}]
has duplicate rows and is necessarily zero.

With account of notation (
2.23
) for
A[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]
and notation (
4.65
) for
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
, inserting (
4.64
) into (
4.63
) we arrive at

f_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}i\lambda\beta_{q_{j}}(x)f_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{q_{j},s}(x)\cdot\Phi(x,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s),\mathfrak{p}],\qquad x\in[0,\ell].

(4.66)

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed in addition to
\mathfrak{q}
we fixed above. It is clear that

\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s),\mathfrak{p}]\equiv 0\quad\text{whenever}\quad s=q_{k}\quad\text{for some}\quad k\neq j.

(4.67)

And if it is not the case, ordering elements of
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
, we arrive at a sequence
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
. Hence, in this case

\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s),\mathfrak{p}]=\sigma(\mathfrak{q},j,s)f_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)}(\cdot),

(4.68)

where
\sigma(\mathfrak{q},j,s)=\pm 1
is a signature of the permutation behind the ordering of the sequence
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
.

Further, note that
Q_{j,k}\equiv 0
whenever
\beta_{j}\equiv\beta_{k}
. Hence
Q_{q_{j},q_{j}}\equiv 0
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\}
, and so
s=q_{j}
can be excluded from the summation in (
4.66
). With account of this observation, definition (
4.51
) of
\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}(\cdot)
and relations (
4.67
)–(
4.68
), we can rewrite (
4.66
) as

f_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}(x)=i\lambda\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)f_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{s=1}{s \notin\fq}}^{n}\sigma(\mathfrak{q},j,s)Q_{q_{j},s}(x)f_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)}(x),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m},

(4.69)

which coincides with (
4.60
) if we set

\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}}(\cdot)=\begin{cases}\sigma(\mathfrak{q},j,s)Q_{q_{j},s}(\cdot),&\text{if}\ \ \mathfrak{r}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)\ \ \text{for some}\ \ j\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ s\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\setminus\mathfrak{q},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(4.70)

Here and in (
4.69
), for simplicity, we identified sequence
\mathfrak{q}
with the corresponding set
\{q_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.

Let us verify condition (
4.61
). It is clear, from (
4.70
) that we only need to consider the case when
\mathfrak{r}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)
for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,{m}\}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\setminus\mathfrak{q}
. By definition of
\mathfrak{q}(q_{j}\to s)
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(q_{j}\to s)
it is clear that

\beta_{\mathfrak{r}}=\beta_{q_{1}}+\ldots+\beta_{q_{j-1}}+s+\beta_{q_{j+1}}+\beta_{q_{n}}=\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}-\beta_{q_{j}}+\beta_{s}.

(4.71)

Hence if
\beta_{\mathfrak{r}}\equiv\beta_{\mathfrak{q}}
, then
\beta_{q_{j}}\equiv\beta_{s}
, which implies that
Q_{q_{j},\,s}\equiv 0
by the corresponding condition (
4.8
) on
Q
. Therefore, formula (
4.70
) implies that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}}\equiv 0
.

In conclusion, vector function
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\cdot,\lambda)
is a solution of the first order system of ODE (
4.60
) that satisfies initial condition (
4.59
). Moreover, potential matrix function
\mathfrak{Q}(\cdot)
in (
4.60
) is summable and satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.61
), while entries of the diagonal matrix function
\mathfrak{B}(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) if we rewrite them appropriately. Hence, all previous considerations of Sections
3
and
4
apply to solutions of system (
4.60
). In particular, Corollary
4.5
immediately yields formula (
4.56
) if we compare definition (
4.42
) of
\rho_{1}^{-}(\cdot)
,
\rho_{n}^{+}(\cdot)
and definition (
4.52
)–(
4.54
) of
\tau_{m}^{\pm}(\cdot)
. The proof is now complete.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

If
m=n
, then
\mathfrak{P}_{m}=\mathfrak{P}_{n}
has exactly one element
\mathfrak{p}_{0}:=(1,\ldots,n)
. Moreover,
\Phi(x,\lambda)[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]=\det\Phi(x,\lambda)
for
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}_{0}
. Hence system (
4.60
) turns into (
4.48
). This shows that Proposition
4.7
contains Liouville’s formula as a partial case.

5
Regular and strictly regular boundary conditions

Results of the previous sections about solutions of the equation (
1.1
) allow us to obtain many spectral properties of the corresponding BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).

5.1
General properties of BVP

For the reader’s convenience let us recall BVP (
1.1
)–(
1.2
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(Q)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}(y^{\prime}+Q(x)y)=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell],

(5.1)

\displaystyle U(y):=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\rank(C\ D)=n.

(5.2)

Note that the condition
\rank(C\ D)=n
is equivalent to
\ker(CC^{*}+DD^{*})=\{0\}
.

Emphasize that a pair of matrices
\{C,D\}
in boundary conditions (
5.2
) is not unique. Indeed, two pairs
\{C,D\}
and
\{\widehat{C},\widehat{D}\}
determine the same boundary conditions if and only if
\{\widehat{C},\widehat{D}\}=\{XC,XD\}
with some nonsingular
X\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. In Lemma
5.11
we present “canonical” form for matrices in boundary conditions (
5.2
) which is important in applications.

Let us introduce the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
as follows,

\mathfrak{H}:=\oplus_{k=1}^{n}\mathfrak{H}_{k},\qquad\mathfrak{H}_{k}:=L^{2}_{|\beta_{k}|}[0,\ell],\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(5.3)

i.e. for
f=\col(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
and
g=\col(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
we have

\displaystyle(f,g)_{\mathfrak{H}}:=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{|B(x)|f(x),g(x)}\right\rangle dx=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(f_{k},g_{k})_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}},\quad\text{where}\quad\left\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\right\rangle:=\left\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\quad\text{and}

(5.4)

\displaystyle(f_{k},g_{k})_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}}:=\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{k}(x)\overline{g_{k}(x)}|\beta_{k}(x)|dx,\qquad f\in\mathfrak{H}_{k}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \int_{0}^{\ell}|f_{k}(x)|^{2}|\beta_{k}(x)|dx<\infty.

(5.5)

With BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) one naturally associates Dirac-type operator
L_{U}(Q)
in the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
as follows,

\displaystyle L_{U}(Q)y=\mathcal{L}(Q)y=-iB^{-1}(y^{\prime}+Qy),\qquad y\in\dom(L_{U}(Q)),\quad\text{where}

(5.6)

\displaystyle\dom(L_{U}(Q)):=\{y\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})\ :\ \mathcal{L}(Q)y\in\mathfrak{H},\ \ \ Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0\}.

(5.7)

Alongside equation (
5.1
) we consider the same equation but with
Q=0
,

\mathcal{L}_{0}y:=\mathcal{L}(0)y:=-iB(x)^{-1}y^{\prime}=\lambda y,\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell],\\

(5.8)

and with same boundary conditions (
5.2
). Similarly to
L_{U}(Q)
, we associate the unperturbed Dirac-type operator
L_{0,U}:=L_{U}(0)
in
\mathfrak{H}
with BVP (
5.8
), (
5.2
).

Recall that
b_{k}
and
\rho_{k}(\cdot)
are defined in (
4.15
) via functions
\beta_{k}(\cdot)
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. For most of the results in this section we will only assume the following relaxed conditions on functions
\beta_{k}(\cdot)
,

\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell],\mathbb{R}),\qquad s_{k}:=\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))=\const\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(5.9)

which implies the following condition on functions
\rho_{k}(\cdot)
,

\rho_{k}\in\AC[0,\ell]\quad\text{and is strictly monotonous},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.10)

Since both matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
are summable, one can define fundamental matrix solutions
\Phi_{Q}(\cdot,\lambda):=\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
and
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
of the equations (
5.1
) and (
5.8
) via formulas (
4.19
) and (
4.20
), respectively.

Next we set

\displaystyle A_{Q}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=A(\lambda):=C+D\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)=:(a_{jk}(\lambda))_{j,k=1}^{n},\quad\text{where}\quad a_{jk}(\lambda)=c_{jk}+\sum_{p=1}^{n}d_{jp}\varphi_{pk}(\lambda),

(5.11)

\displaystyle A_{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=:(a_{jk}^{0}(\lambda))_{j,k=1}^{n},\quad\text{where}\quad a_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)=c_{jk}+d_{jk}e^{i\lambda b_{k}},

(5.12)

where
\varphi_{pk}(\lambda):=\varphi_{pk}(\ell,\lambda)
is the corresponding entry of the matrix
\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)
. Finally, we introduce the characteristic determinants of the problems (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) and (
5.8
), (
5.2
) by setting

\Delta(\lambda):=\Delta_{Q}(\lambda):=\det(A_{Q}(\lambda)),\qquad\Delta_{0}(\lambda):=\det(A_{0}(\lambda)),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(5.13)

respectively. The role of the characteristic determinant
\Delta(\cdot)
in the spectral theory of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) becomes clear
from the following simple statement. To state it we denote by

\displaystyle A^{a}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=A^{a}_{Q}(\lambda)=:(A_{jk}(\lambda))_{j,k=1}^{n}\ \ \ \text{the matrix adjugate to}\ \ \ A_{Q}(\lambda),

(5.14)

\displaystyle A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=:(A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda))_{j,k=1}^{n}\ \ \ \text{the matrix adjugate to}\ \ \ A_{0}(\lambda).

(5.15)

With account of notations (
2.27
)–(
2.28
) we see that for
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,

\displaystyle A_{jk}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=A(\lambda)\{j,k\}=(-1)^{j+k}A(\lambda)[\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{j}],

(5.16)

\displaystyle A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=A^{0}(\lambda)\{j,k\}=(-1)^{j+k}A^{0}(\lambda)[\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{j}].

(5.17)

Lemma 5.1
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.4
), i.e.
B,Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and
B(x)
is invertible for almost all
x
. Number
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
given by (
5.7
) if and only if
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=0
. Moreover, the algebraic multiplicity
m_{a}(\lambda)
of
\lambda
coincides with the multiplicity of
\lambda
as a root of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
. In particular,
\dim\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}(L_{U}(Q))=1
if and only if
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=0
and
\Delta_{Q}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0
. Moreover, in the later case,
\rank(A^{a}_{Q}(\lambda))=1
and there exists
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
such that

y(x,\lambda):=Y_{p}(x,\lambda):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)\neq 0,

(5.18)

is the (non-trivial) eigenvector of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda
.

In addition, if
Q=0
, then the following explicit formula holds,

y_{0}(x,\lambda):=Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)=\col\left(A_{1p}^{0}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda\rho_{1}(x)},\ldots,A_{np}^{0}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda\rho_{n}(x)}\right)\neq 0.

(5.19)

Proof.

The proof of general statement is similar to the proof of
[
43
, Theorem 1.2, step (i)]
. Namely, one can show that if
\lambda
is an
m
-multiple zero of the function
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
, then the system of functions

\left\{\left.\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\mu^{k}}Y_{p}(x,\mu)\right|_{\mu=\lambda}:\ \ k\in\{0,1,\ldots,m-1\},\ \ p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\right\}

(5.20)

spans the root subspace
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}(L_{U}(Q))
of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. Relation
\dim\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}(L_{U}(Q))=m
can be proved similarly to how it was done for ordinary differential operators in
[
52
]
.

It remains to consider the case
\dim\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}(L_{U}(Q))=1
. Then
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is the eigenvalue of the problem (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) of geometric and algebraic multiplicity one, hence
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=0
and
\Delta_{Q}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0
. Jacobi’s formula (
2.21
) implies that

\tr\left(A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)A_{Q}^{\prime}(\lambda)\right)=\Delta_{Q}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0,

(5.21)

which in turn implies that
A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)\neq 0
. Therefore, it follows from the identity

A_{Q}(\lambda)A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)=A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)A_{Q}(\lambda)=\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)I_{n}=0,

(5.22)

that for a certain
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, vector

\alpha:=\col(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}):=\col(A_{1j}(\lambda),\ldots,A_{nj}(\lambda))

(5.23)

is non-zero and satisfies
A_{Q}(\lambda)\alpha=0
. The corresponding eigenvector of BVP (
5.8
), (
5.2
) is given by

y(x,\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{k}\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)\not\equiv 0,\qquad y(0,\lambda)=\alpha\neq 0.

(5.24)

Indeed, in accordance with definition (
5.8
),
\mathcal{L}_{0}y(x,\lambda)=\lambda y(x,\lambda)
. Besides,

Cy(0,\lambda)+Dy(\ell,\lambda)=(C\Phi_{0}(0,\lambda)+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda))\alpha=A_{Q}(\lambda)\alpha=0.

(5.25)

This proves (
5.18
). Since
\lambda
is a simple eigenvalue of
L_{U}(Q)
, then all eigenvectors are proportional to each other. Formulas (
5.23
)–(
5.24
) for eigenvectors of
L_{U}(Q)
imply that all columns of the matrix
A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)
are proportional, which means that
\rank(A_{Q}(\lambda))=1
and finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

Note that each non-trivial
k
th column of the adjugate matrix (
5.14
) generates an eigenvector of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
by formula (
5.18
) with
p
replaced by
k
. Since
\rank(A_{a}(\lambda))=1
, the eigenvectors
Y_{p}(x,\lambda)
and
Y_{k}(x,\lambda)
are proportional. Therefore, we write
y(x,\lambda)
in (
5.18
) instead of
y_{p}(x,\lambda)
omitting the index
p
.

The following observation trivially follows from Lemma
5.1
and will be useful in the future.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.4
) and let
\lambda
be an algebraically simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
and let
f
be any eigenvector of
L_{U}(Q)
in
\mathfrak{H}
corresponding to
\lambda
. Then, there exists
p=p_{\lambda}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
\gamma_{p}\in\mathbb{C}
, such that

f(\cdot)=\gamma_{p}Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)=\gamma_{p}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\lambda),\qquad|\gamma_{p}|=\|f\|_{\mathfrak{H}}/\|Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(5.26)

Morever, this is valid for any
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, for which
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)\not\equiv 0
.

The following trivial properties of
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
,
A_{0}(\lambda)
,
A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)
,
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
and
Y_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
will be useful in the future. Recall, that
\Pi_{h}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\Im\lambda|\leqslant h\}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
h\geqslant 0
and let
\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R})
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. There there exists a constant
M_{h}>0
that only depends on
h
, matrices
C
and
D
, and values
\|\beta_{k}\|_{1}:=\|\beta_{k}\|_{L^{1}[0,\ell]}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, such that the following uniform inequalities hold

\displaystyle\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}}\right|\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\left\|{\frac{d}{d\lambda}Y_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(5.27)

\displaystyle|a_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|+|(a_{jk}^{0})^{\prime}(\lambda)|+|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|+|\Delta_{0}(\lambda)|\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.28)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\|Y_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.29)

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\Pi_{h}
be fixed for the entire proof. Since
\beta_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R})
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, it follows that

|\rho_{k}(x)|=\left|{\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{k}(t)dt}\right|\leqslant b_{0},\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(5.30)

where
b_{0}:=\max\{\|\beta_{1}\|_{1},\ldots,\|\beta_{n}\|_{1}\}
. Since
\rho_{k}(\cdot)
are real-valued functions, then

\left|{e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}}\right|=e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot\rho_{k}(x)}\leqslant e^{b_{0}h},\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.31)

Similarly, for
\lambda\in\Pi_{h}
,
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, we have

|a_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|\leqslant|c_{jk}|+|d_{jk}|e^{b_{0}h}\leqslant c_{0}+d_{0}e^{b_{0}h}=:\gamma_{h},\qquad|(a_{jk}^{0})^{\prime}(\lambda)|\leqslant|b_{k}d_{jk}|e^{b_{0}h}\leqslant b_{0}d_{0}e^{b_{0}h},

(5.32)

where

c_{0}:=\max\{|c_{jk}|:j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\},\qquad d_{0}:=\max\{|d_{jk}|:j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\}.

(5.33)

From the definition of the adjugate matrix it follows that
(-1)^{j+k}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)
is the determinant of some
(n-1)\times(n-1)
submatrix of
A_{0}(\lambda)
. Moreover,
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\det A_{0}(\lambda)
. Hence (
5.32
) implies that

|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|\leqslant(n-1)!\gamma_{h}^{n-1},\qquad|\Delta_{0}(\lambda)|\leqslant n!\gamma_{h}^{n},\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.34)

With account of (
4.20
) and (
4.15
) we have after making a change of variable
u=\rho_{j}(x)
,

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left|{\exp\left(i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)\right)}\right|^{2}|\beta_{j}(x)|dx\leqslant\max_{x\in[0,\ell]}\left|{\exp\left(i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)\right)}\right|^{2}\|\beta_{j}\|_{1}\leqslant b_{0}e^{2b_{0}h}.

(5.35)

Combining formula (
5.18
) with estimates (
5.34
) and (
5.35
) we arrive at

\|Y_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\left\|{\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)\Phi_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|{A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)}\right|\cdot\|\Phi_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\\
\leqslant n!\gamma_{h}^{n-1}b_{0}^{1/2}e^{b_{0}h},\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.36)

Derivative
\frac{d}{d\lambda}Y_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)
can be estimated similarly. Since

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Phi_{j}^{0}(x,\lambda)=i\rho_{j}(x)e^{i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)}\col(\delta_{1j},\ldots,\delta_{nj}),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

it is clear, that each entry of the vector function
\frac{d}{d\lambda}Y_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)
is a polynomial in
e^{ib_{j}\lambda},e^{i\rho_{j}(x)\lambda},b_{j},\rho_{j}(x)
,
c_{jp},d_{jp}
. This observation and estimates (
5.30
)–(
5.31
) imply that

\left\|{\frac{d}{d\lambda}Y_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\leqslant\widetilde{M}_{h}\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

for some
\widetilde{M}_{h}>0
. Thus, setting

M_{h}:=\max\{e^{b_{0}h},\ \ \gamma_{h}+b_{0}d_{0}e^{b_{0}h}+(n-1)!\gamma_{h}^{n-1}+n!\gamma_{h}^{n},\ \ n!\gamma_{h}^{n-1}b_{0}^{1/2}e^{b_{0}h},\ \ \widetilde{M}_{h}\},

(5.37)

and combining all the estimates established above we arrive at (
5.27
)–(
5.29
).
∎

5.2
Regular boundary conditions

Considerations of this and the next subsection are performed only in terms of number
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
given by (
4.15
) without their connection to original functions
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
. Hence, the only condition we need to impose in this and the next subsection is condition (
4.17
), i.e. that numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
are ordered and non-zero. Let us also set

b_{-}:=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n_{-}}\leqslant 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{+}:=b_{n_{-}+1}+\ldots+b_{n}\geqslant 0.

(5.38)

Note that if
n_{-}=0
then
b_{-}=0
and if
n_{-}=n
then
b_{+}=0
.

Let us recall the definition of regular boundary conditions from the introduction. Note that considerations below are valid without canonical ordering (
4.17
). To this end, let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
be the set of diagonal idempotent
n\times n
matrices:

\mathcal{P}_{n}:=\{P=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}):\ p_{k}\in\{0,1\},\ k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\}.

(5.39)

For any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we put

J_{P}:=J_{P}(C,D):=\det(T_{P}(C,D)),\qquad T_{P}(C,D):=C(I_{n}-P)+DP.

(5.40)

Finally, we set

P_{\pm}:=\diag(p_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,p_{n}^{\pm}).\qquad p_{k}^{+}=\begin{cases}1,&b_{k}>0,\\
0,&b_{k}<0,\end{cases},\qquad p_{k}^{-}=\begin{cases}0,&b_{k}>0,\\
1,&b_{k}<0,\end{cases},\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.41)

Clearly
P_{+}+P_{-}=I_{n}
and
P_{+}
(resp.
P_{-}
) is the projector onto the positive (resp. negative) part of the spectrum of the signature matrix
S=\sign(B(\cdot))\equiv\const
.

Definition 5.5
.

Boundary conditions (
5.2
) for equation (
5.1
) are called
regular
if

J_{P_{+}}(C,D)=\det(CP_{-}+DP_{+})\neq 0\quad\text{and}\quad J_{P_{-}}(C,D)=\det(CP_{+}+DP_{-})\neq 0.

(5.42)

Let us obtain some general properties of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

With account of the notations
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
J_{P}(C,D)
, and
b_{k}:=\rho_{k}(\ell)=\int_{0}^{\ell}\beta_{k}(x)dx
, the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}
admits a representation

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}J_{P}(C,D)e^{i\lambda b_{P}},\qquad\quad b_{P}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}b_{k}.

(5.43)

Proof.

Denoting by
c_{k}
and
d_{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, the columns of the matrices
C
and
D
, respectively, we write them in the form
C=(c_{1}\ \ldots\ c_{n})
and
D=(d_{1}\ \ldots\ d_{n})
.
In accordance with (
4.20
),
\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag(e^{i\lambda b_{1}},\ldots,e^{i\lambda b_{n}})
. Hence

A_{0}(\lambda)=C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\left(c_{1}+e^{i\lambda b_{1}}d_{1}\ \ \ldots\ \ c_{n}+e^{i\lambda b_{n}}d_{n}\right).

Formula (
5.43
) easily follows from this representation and the general formula for the determinant of the sum of two matrices as the sum of determinants of all
2^{n}
matrices, where for each such matrix we choose either
c_{k}
or
e^{i\lambda b_{k}}d_{k}
as the
k
-th column.
∎

To obtain further properties of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
, we need to recall some definitions.

Definition 5.7

(
[
33
,
22
]
)
.

The sequence
\mathfrak{M}
is called
incompressible
if for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
every rectangle
[t-1,t+1]\times\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
contains at most
d
entries of the sequence, i.e.

\card\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:|\Re\mu_{m}-t|\leqslant 1\}\leqslant d,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.44)

To emphasize parameter
d
we will sometimes call
\mathfrak{M}
an incompressible
sequence of density
d
.

Definition 5.8

(
[
27
]
)
.

An entire function
F(\cdot)
of exponential type is said to be of
sine-type
if

(i)
all zeros of
F(\cdot)
lie in the strip
\Pi_{h}

for some
h\geqslant 0
, and

(ii)
there exists
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
h_{0}>h
such
that

0<C_{1}\leqslant|F(x+ih_{0})|\leqslant C_{2}<\infty,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.45)

This definition is borrowed from
[
27
]
(see
also
[
22
]
). It differs from that contained
in
[
28
]
. Namely, it is assumed in
[
28
]
that the
sequence of zeros of
F(\cdot)
is
separated
and the
indicator function
h_{F}(\cdot)
of
F(\cdot)
,

h_{F}(\varphi):=\varlimsup_{r\to+\infty}\frac{\ln\left|F\left(re^{i\varphi}\right)\right|}{r},\quad\varphi\in(-\pi,\pi],

(5.46)

satisfies the condition
h_{F}(\pi/2)=h_{F}(-\pi/2)
. The latter
is imposed for convenience and can easily be achieved with
multiplying
F(\cdot)
by a function
e^{i\gamma z}
with
an appropriate
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let boundary conditions be regular (
5.2
). Then the following statements hold:

(i)
The characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is a sine-type function with
h_{\Delta_{0}}(\pi/2)=-b_{-}
and
h_{\Delta_{0}}(-\pi/2)=b_{+}
. In particular,
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
has infinitely many zeros

\Lambda_{0}:=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

(5.47)

counting multiplicity and
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Pi_{h}
for some
h>0
.

(ii)
The sequence
\Lambda_{0}
is incompressible.

(iii)
For any
\varepsilon>0
the determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
admits the following estimate from below

|\Delta_{0}(\lambda)|>C_{\varepsilon}(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}})>C_{\varepsilon},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{m}^{0}),

(5.48)

with some
C_{\varepsilon}>0
, where numbers
b_{\pm}
are given by (
5.38
).

(iv)
The sequence
\Lambda_{0}
can be ordered in such a way that the following asymptotical formula holds

\lambda_{m}^{0}=\frac{2\pi m}{b_{+}-b_{-}}(1+o(1))\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty.

(5.49)

Proof.

(i-iii)
It follows from (
5.43
) that

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\gamma_{k}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{k}},\qquad\gamma_{k}:=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{P \in\cP_n}{b_P = \sigma_k}}J_{P}(C,D),\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\},

(5.50)

where
\sigma_{1}<\ldots<\sigma_{N}
,
N\leqslant 2^{n}
, are all distinct values in the set

\{b_{P}:P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\}=\left\{\sum_{k\in S}b_{k}:S\subset\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\right\}=:\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N}\},

with
b_{P}
defined by (
5.43
) for
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Taking into account definition (
5.41
) of
P_{-}
, definition (
5.43
) of
b_{P}
and definition (
5.38
) of
b_{-}
, we have

\sigma_{1}=\min_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}b_{P}=\sum_{b_{k}<0}b_{k}=b_{P-},\quad\text{hence}\quad\sigma_{1}=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n_{-}}=b_{-}.

(5.51)

It is also clear that
b_{P}>b_{P_{-}}
whenever
P\neq P_{-}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Hence
\gamma_{1}=J_{P_{-}}(C,D)\neq 0
, since boundary conditions are regular (see (
5.42
)). Similarly

\sigma_{N}=\max_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}b_{P}=\sum_{b_{k}>0}b_{k}=b_{P+}=b_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{N}=J_{P_{+}}(C,D)\neq 0.

(5.52)

Thus, formula (
5.50
) for
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
turns into,

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=J_{P_{-}}(C,D)e^{i\lambda b_{-}}+J_{P_{+}}(C,D)e^{i\lambda b_{+}}+\sum_{k=2}^{N-1}\gamma_{k}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{k}},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(5.53)

This immediately implies that
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)\not\equiv 0
. Moreover, if
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
has no zeros, then canonical factorization for entire functions of exponential type implies that
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=e^{\alpha+\beta\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
. This contradicts representation (
5.53
). Hence
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
has zeros. In turn, since
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)\not\equiv 0
, has zeros and bounded on the real line, the canonical factorization of entire functions of exponential type implies that the set of zeros of
\Delta_{0}
is countable.
It is clear from (
5.53
) that function
f(\lambda):=\exp(-i\lambda\frac{b_{-}+b_{+}}{2})\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
satisfy the following uniform estimate for some
h\geqslant 0
,

\tau_{h}^{-1}e^{\sigma|\Im\lambda|}\leqslant|f(\lambda)|\leqslant\tau_{h}e^{\sigma|\Im\lambda|}>0,\qquad|\Im\lambda|>h,

(5.54)

with some
\tau_{h}>1
that does not depend on
\lambda
. Here
\sigma:=\frac{b_{+}-b_{-}}{2}>0
. It is clear from estimate (
5.54
)
that
f(\cdot)
is the sine-type function of exponential type
\sigma
, with
h_{f}(\pm\pi/2)=\sigma
. The desired properties of zeros as well as estimate (
5.48
) are now immediate from
[
22
, Lemmas 3 and 4]
.

(iv)
The proof is the same as in
[
33
, Proposition 4.6(iv)]
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

(i)
Lemma
5.9
remains valid if characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is not identically zero and has zeros. Indeed, it follows that at least two coefficients in (
5.50
) are non-zero, i.e.

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\gamma_{k}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{k}},\quad\text{where}\quad\gamma_{N_{1}}\gamma_{N_{2}}\neq 0\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leqslant N_{1}<N_{2}\leqslant N,

(5.55)

which implies that it is a sine-type function with all the properties listed in Lemma
5.9
if we replace
b_{-}
with
\sigma_{N_{1}}
and
b_{+}
with
\sigma_{N_{2}}
. In particular,
h_{\Delta_{0}}(\pi/2)=-\sigma_{N_{1}}
and
h_{\Delta_{0}}(-\pi/2)=\sigma_{N_{2}}
.

(ii)
Note, that since
\sigma_{1}=b_{-}\leqslant 0
and
\sigma_{N}=b_{+}\geqslant 0
, identity (
5.50
) implies that the indicator diagram of the entire function
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is always contained in the vertical segment
[-ib_{+},-ib_{-}]
and coincides with it if and only if boundary conditions are regular. In other words, boundary conditions are regular if and only if the determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is of maximal possible growth in both half-planes
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
.

(iii)
Let us clarify the previous remark when either
b_{-}=0
or
b_{+}=0
, where
b_{\pm}
is given by (
5.51
)–(
5.52
). In other words, entries of the matrix
B(x)
are either all positive or all negative. In this case, regularity condition (
5.42
) turns into
\det(CD)\neq 0
, since either
P_{-}=0
or, respectively,
P_{+}=0
, where
P_{\pm}
is given by (
5.41
). Since
b_{-}+b_{+}=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n}
, it is clear that in both cases
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is the entire function of exponential type
|b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n}|
, growing in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, resp.
\mathbb{C}_{-}
, and bounded from above and below in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
, resp.
\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

Finally, we reduce regular boundary conditions (
5.2
) to a certain equivalent canonical form which is much simpler and convenient to work with.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular and assume that equations in (
5.8
) and boundary conditions (
5.2
) are reordered to make sure canonical ordering (
4.17
), i.e. for some
n_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
,

b_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n_{-}}<0<b_{n_{-}+1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n}.

(5.56)

Then a pair of matrices
\{C,D\}
determined by the linear form
Uy=0
in (
5.2
) can be chosen to admit the following triangular block-matrix representation with respect to the orthogonal decomposition

\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}
, where
n_{+}=n-n_{-}
:

C=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&C_{12}\\
\mathbb{O}&C_{22}\end{pmatrix},\qquad D=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}&\mathbb{O}\\
D_{21}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix},

(5.57)

for some
matrices
C_{12},C_{22},D_{11},D_{21}
.
Here, in the case
n_{-}=0
,
n_{+}=n
the canonical form is
\{C=I_{n},D\}
with any arbitrary invertible
D
, while in the case
n_{-}=n
,
n_{+}=0
the canonical form is
\{C,D=I_{n}\}
with arbitrary invertible
C
.

Proof.

Definition (
5.41
) and relation (
5.56
) imply that matrices
P_{\pm}
admit the following block-matrix representation with respect to the orthogonal decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}
:

P_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&\mathbb{O}\\
