\mathbb{O}&\mathbb{O}\end{pmatrix},\qquad P_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{O}&\mathbb{O}\\
\mathbb{O}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.58)

Assume that boundary conditions in (
5.2
) are given by a pair
\{\widehat{C},\widehat{D}\}
, i.e.

Uy=\widehat{C}y(0)+\widehat{D}y(\ell)=0
. Consider their block-matrix representation with respect to the orthogonal decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}:

\widehat{C}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{C}_{11}&\widehat{C}_{12}\\
\widehat{C}_{21}&\widehat{C}_{22}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widehat{D}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{D}_{11}&\widehat{D}_{12}\\
\widehat{D}_{21}&\widehat{D}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.59)

Definition
5.5
of regularity (see (
5.42
)) implies thatt
J_{P_{+}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D})\cdot J_{P_{-}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D})\neq 0
. In particular, one has

T_{P_{+}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D})=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{C}_{11}&\widehat{D}_{12}\\
\widehat{C}_{21}&\widehat{D}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\widehat{C}P_{-}+\widehat{D}P_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\det(T_{P_{+}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D}))=J_{P_{+}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D})\neq 0.

(5.60)

Hence, multiplying the equation
\widehat{C}y(0)+\widehat{D}y(\ell)=0
by
T_{P_{+}}(\widehat{C},\widehat{D})^{-1}
from the left we arrive at the equivalent equation with new matrices (
5.57
) instead of (
5.59
).
∎

Remark 5.12
.

The proof remains valid for non-regular boundary conditions provided that
J_{P}(C,D)\neq 0
for some
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, after a proper reordering of equations in (
5.8
) and boundary conditions (
5.2
).

5.3
Strictly regular boundary conditions

Let us introduce a notion of strictly regular boundary conditions.

Definition 5.13
.

(i)
A sequence
\mathfrak{M}:=\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of complex numbers is called
separated
if for some
\delta>0
,

|\mu_{j}-\mu_{k}|>2\delta\quad\text{whenever}\quad j\neq k.

(5.61)

In particular, all entries of a separated sequence are distinct.

(ii)
The sequence
\mathfrak{M}
is called
asymptotically separated
if for some
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
the subsequence
\mathfrak{M}({m_{0}}):=\{\mu_{m}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}
is separated.

(iii)
Boundary conditions (
5.2
) are called
strictly regular
, if they are regular and the sequence of zeros
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is asymptotically separated.
In particular, there is
m_{0}
such that zeros
\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}

are algebraically (hence geometrically) simple.

See the next subsection for concrete examples of strictly regular boundary conditions.
In this subsection we obtain certain estimates from below involving
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\cdot)
and the corresponding eigenvectors assuming boundary conditions to be strictly regular.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be strictly regular and let
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequence of eigenvalues of the operator
L_{0,U}
, counting multiplicity. Then there exist
\delta,C_{0}>0
, not dependent on
m
, and such that with
m_{0}
from Definition
5.13
(iii) the following estimate from below holds

|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)|\geqslant C_{0},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}(\lambda_{m}^{0}),\quad|m|>m_{0}.

(5.62)

Proof.

Since
\Delta(\cdot)
is a sine-type function with asymptotically separated zeros, then in accordance with
[
22
, Lemmas 5]
and
[
28
, Lecture 22]

|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\geqslant C_{0}^{\prime},\quad|m|>m_{0},

for some
C_{0}^{\prime}>0
. Lemma
5.9
implies inclusion
\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from (
5.43
) (see also Lemma
5.4
) that for some
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}>0

|\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)|\leqslant C_{0}^{\prime\prime},\qquad|\Im\lambda|\leqslant h+1.\qquad

Hence Taylor expansion and inclusion
\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
yield

|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)|\geqslant|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|-\int_{\lambda_{m}^{0}}^{\lambda}|\Delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)||dz|\geqslant C_{0}^{\prime}-|\lambda-\lambda_{m}^{0}|C_{0}^{\prime\prime},\qquad|\lambda-\lambda_{m}^{0}|<1,\quad|m|>m_{0}.

(5.63)

Setting
\delta=\min\{1,\frac{C_{0}^{\prime}}{2C_{0}^{\prime\prime}}\}
and
C_{0}=C_{0}^{\prime}-\delta C_{0}^{\prime\prime}\geqslant C_{0}^{\prime}/2
one derives that
for
|\lambda-\lambda_{m}^{0}|<\delta
inequality (
5.63
) implies (
5.62
).
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be strictly regular. Then there exist
\delta>0
and
C_{1}>0
such that with
m_{0}
from Definition
5.13
(iii) the following estimate holds

\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|\geqslant C_{1},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}(\lambda_{m}^{0}),\quad|m|>m_{0}.

(5.64)

In particular, for any
m
satisfying
|m|>m_{0}
, there exist
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
that depend on
m
and such that

|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|\geqslant C_{2}(=C_{1}/n^{2}),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}(\lambda_{m}^{0}).

(5.65)

Proof.

Since boundary conditions are strictly regular, Lemma
5.14
implies existence of constants
\delta,C_{0}>0
such that (
5.62
) holds. Further, in accordance with Jacobi’s formula (
2.21
)

\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)(a_{kj}^{0})^{\prime}(\lambda).

(5.66)

Combining estimate (
5.28
) on
|(a_{kj}^{0})^{\prime}(\lambda)|
from above and estimate (
5.62
) on
|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)|
from below with identity (
5.66
) yields

C_{0}\leqslant|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)|\leqslant\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|\cdot|(a_{kj}^{0})^{\prime}(\lambda)|\leqslant M_{h}\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}|A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)|,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{\delta}(\lambda_{m}^{0}).

(5.67)

This implies (
5.64
) with
C_{1}=C_{0}/M_{h}
.
∎

Proposition 5.16
.

Let entries of the matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfy condition (
5.9
). Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be strictly regular and let
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequence of eigenvalues of the operator
L_{0,U}=L_{0}(U)
, counting multiplicity. Then for each
|m|>m_{0}
there exists
p=p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, such that the vector function
\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}(\cdot):=Y_{p_{m}}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})
given by (
5.19
) is a non-trivial eigenvector of the operator
L_{0,U}
corresponding to its simple eigenvalue
\lambda_{m}^{0}
. Moreover, the following uniform estimate holds,

C_{3}\leqslant\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{p_{m}}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant C_{4},\qquad|m|>m_{0},

(5.68)

where
C_{4}>C_{3}>0
do not depend on
m
.

Proof.

Lemma
5.9
implies that
\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, for some
h\geqslant 0
. Hence estimate (
5.29
) from Lemma
5.4
trivially implies desired estimate from above with any choice of
p=p_{m}
,

\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant C_{4}=M_{h},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},\quad p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.69)

By Lemma
5.15
, there exist indices
p=p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
q=q_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, and a constant
C_{2}>0
such that estimate (
5.65
) holds, i.e.
|A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\geqslant C_{2}
,
|m|>m_{0}
. Emphasize, that although
p
and
q
depend on
m
, the constant
C_{2}
in the above estimate does not.
This estimate, definition (
5.19
) of
Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)
and orthogonality in
\mathfrak{H}
of the vector function
\Phi_{q}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
to other
\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
imply that

\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\geqslant|A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\cdot\|\Phi_{q}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\geqslant C_{2}\sqrt{\int_{0}^{\ell}\left|{e^{i\lambda_{m}^{0}\rho_{q}(x)}}\right|^{2}|\beta_{q}(x)|\,dx},\qquad|m|>m_{0}.

(5.70)

Let us estimate the integral in (
5.70
). Recall that
\beta_{q}(\cdot)
does not change sign on
[0,\ell]
. Hence
|\beta_{q}(x)|=s_{q}\beta_{q}(x)
,
x\in[0,\ell]
, where
s_{q}=\sign(\beta_{q}(\cdot))
. Making a change of variable
t=\rho_{q}(x)
(and so
\beta_{q}(x)dx=dt
), we have

\int_{0}^{\ell}\left|{\exp\left(i\lambda\rho_{q}(x)\right)}\right|^{2}|\beta_{q}(x)|dx=\left|{\int_{0}^{b_{q}}\left|{\exp\left(i\lambda t\right)}\right|^{2}dt}\right|\geqslant\min\{1,e^{-2\Im\lambda\cdot b_{q}}\}\geqslant e^{-2|b_{q}|h},\qquad\lambda\in\Pi_{h}.

(5.71)

Inserting (
5.71
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
into (
5.70
) we arrive at the estimate from below in (
5.68
) with with some
C_{3}>0
that does not depend on
q
and
m
. Thus, vector function
\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}(\cdot)=Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})
is non-zero. Lemma
5.1
implies that it is a non-trivial eigenvector of the operator
L_{0,U}
corresponding to its simple eigenvalue
\lambda_{m}^{0}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

5.4
Examples of strictly regular boundary conditions

In the next remark we outline known cases of strictly regular boundary conditions for
n=2
established in
[
33
]
.

Remark 5.17
.

If
n=2
and
b_{1}<0<b_{2}
, canonical form (see (
5.57
)) of regular boundary conditions (
5.2
) is

\begin{cases}\widehat{U}_{1}(y)=y_{1}(0)+by_{2}(0)+ay_{1}(1)=0,\\
\widehat{U}_{2}(y)=dy_{2}(0)+cy_{1}(1)+y_{2}(1)=0,\end{cases}

(5.72)

with some
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
, such that
ad\neq bc
, while the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
takes the following form,

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=d+ae^{i(b_{1}+b_{2})\lambda}+(ad-bc)e^{ib_{1}\lambda}+e^{ib_{2}\lambda},\\

(5.73)

Let us list some types of
strictly regular
boundary conditions of the form (
5.72
). In all of these cases except 4b the set of zeros
of
\Delta_{0}
is a union of finite number of arithmetic progressions.

1.

Regular boundary conditions (
5.72
) for Dirac operator (
-b_{1}=b_{2}=1
) are
strictly regular if and only if
(a-d)^{2}\neq-4bc
.

2.

Separated boundary conditions (
a=d=0
,
bc\neq 0
) are always strictly regular.

3.

Let
b_{1}/b_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
, i.e.
b_{1}=-m_{1}b_{0}
,
b_{2}=m_{2}b_{0}
, where
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
b_{0}>0
and
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})=1
.
Since
ad\neq bc
,
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is a polynomial at
e^{ib_{0}\lambda}
of degree
m_{1}+m_{2}
. Hence, boundary conditions (
5.72
) are strictly regular if and only if this polynomial does not have multiple roots. Let us list some cases with explicit conditions.

(a)

[
33
, Lemma 5.3]
Let
ad\neq 0
and
bc=0
. Then
boundary conditions (
5.72
) are strictly regular if and only if

b_{1}\ln|d|+b_{2}\ln|a|\neq 0\quad\text{or}\quad m_{1}\arg(-d)-m_{2}\arg(-a)\notin 2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

(5.74)

(b)

In particular, antiperiodic boundary conditions (
a=d=1
,
b=c=0
) are strictly regular if
and only if
m_{1}-m_{2}
is odd. Note that these boundary conditions are not strictly regular in
the case of a Dirac system.

(c)

[
33
, Proposition 5.6]
Let
a=0
,
bc\neq 0
. Then
boundary conditions (
5.72
) are strictly regular if and only if

m_{1}^{m_{1}}m_{2}^{m_{2}}(-d)^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\neq(m_{1}+m_{2})^{m_{1}+m_{2}}(-bc)^{m_{2}}.

(5.75)

4.

Let
\alpha:=-b_{1}/b_{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}
. Then the problem of strict regularity of boundary conditions is
generally much more complicated. Let us list some known cases:

(a)

[
33
, Lemma 5.3]
Let
ad\neq 0
and
bc=0
. Then
boundary conditions (
5.72
) are strictly regular if and only if

b_{1}\ln|d|+b_{2}\ln|a|\neq 0.

(5.76)

(b)

[
33
, Proposition 5.6]
Let
a=0
and
bc,d\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Then boundary conditions (
5.72
) are strictly regular if and only if

d\neq-(\alpha+1)\left(|bc|\alpha^{-\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}.

(5.77)

Let us extend results listed in this remark to the case of arbitrary
n
. The next result establishes criterion of strict regularity of boundary conditions of periodic type
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. To this end, for any pair
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
of real numbers with
x/y\in\mathbb{Q}
denote by
\gcd(x,y)
their greatest common divisor, i.e. the largest number
b>0
such that
x/b
and
y/b
are integers.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be of the form

y_{k}(\ell)=c_{k}y_{k}(0),\qquad c_{k}\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(5.78)

i.e.
U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0
, where
C=\diag(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
is an invertible diagonal matrix and
D=-I_{n}
. Then boundary conditions (
5.78
) are regular.

(i)
Let
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the
sequence of zeros of the characteristic determinant

\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
and assume it is ordered in such a way that

\Re\lambda_{m}^{0}\leqslant\Re\lambda_{m+1}^{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then there
exists a sequence of integers
\{m_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, such that

m_{k}<m_{k+1}\leqslant m_{k}+n,\quad\Re\lambda_{m_{k}}^{0}-\Re\lambda_{m_{k}-1}^{0}\geqslant\varepsilon,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

(5.79)

where
\varepsilon:=\frac{2\pi}{b_{\max}n}>0
and
b_{\max}:=\max\{|b_{1}|,\ldots,|b_{n}|\}
.

(ii)
Let numbers
\left\{\frac{\ln|c_{k}|}{b_{k}}\right\}_{k=1}^{n}
be distinct, i.e.

b_{j}\ln|c_{k}|\neq b_{k}\ln|c_{j}|,\qquad j\neq k.

(5.80)

Then boundary conditions (
5.78
) are strictly regular.

(iii)
More precisely, boundary conditions (
5.78
) are strictly regular if and only if for all
j\neq k
the following condition holds,

\text{either}\quad b_{j}\ln|c_{k}|\neq b_{k}\ln|c_{j}|\quad\text{or}\quad\left(\frac{b_{j}}{b_{k}}\in\mathbb{Q}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{b_{j}\arg(c_{k})-b_{k}\arg(c_{j})}{2\pi\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})}\not\in\mathbb{Z}\right).

(5.81)

(iv)

Periodic
boundary conditions
(c_{1}=\ldots=c_{n}=1)
are always non-strictly regular.
Antiperiodic
boundary conditions
(c_{1}=\ldots=c_{n}=-1)
are strictly regular if and only if there exists
b_{0}>0
, odd integers
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
and distinct non-negative integers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
such that
b_{k}=2^{a_{k}}M_{k}b_{0}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. In other words, numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
can be ordered in such a way that the following representation holds

b_{k}=2^{a_{k}}(2u_{k}+1)b_{0},\qquad a_{k},u_{k}\in\mathbb{Z},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\qquad 0\leqslant a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<a_{n}.

(5.82)

In particular, if
b_{k}=2^{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, then antiperiodic boundary conditions are strictly regular.

Proof.

Since matrices
C
and
D
are invertible and diagonal, it follows that
T_{P}(C,D)
given by (
5.40
) is also invertible diagonal matrix for every
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. This implies regularity of boundary conditions. Further, it is clear that

C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag(c_{1}-e^{i\lambda b_{1}},\ldots,c_{n}-e^{i\lambda b_{n}}).

Hence the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
defined in (
5.13
) becomes

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\det(C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda))=(c_{1}-e^{i\lambda b_{1}})\times\ldots\times(c_{n}-e^{i\lambda b_{n}}).

(5.83)

Let
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}=\{\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, be the sequences of zeros of the
k
-th factor in this product. Clearly,

\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}=\frac{-i\ln c_{k}+2\pi m}{b_{k}}=\frac{\arg(c_{k})+2\pi m}{b_{k}}-i\frac{\ln|c_{k}|}{b_{k}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(5.84)

Thus, each sequence
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, is algebraically simple and constitutes an arithmetic progression that lies on the line parallel to the real axis.

(i)
Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and consider
n+1
consecutive eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}^{0},\lambda_{m+1}^{0},\ldots,\lambda_{m+n}^{0}
(ordered by their real parts). By pigeonhole principle, we can find two different indexes
p,q\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, such that eigenvalues
\lambda_{m+p}^{0}
and
\lambda_{m+q}^{0}
belong to the same arithmetic progression
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that they are consecutive elements of this progression,

\lambda_{m+p}^{0}=\lambda^{\per}_{k,u},\qquad\lambda_{m+q}^{0}=\lambda^{\per}_{k,u+s_{k}},

for some
0\leqslant p<q\leqslant n
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
u=u_{m+p,k}
. Here
s_{k}=\sign(b_{k})
, i.e.
u+s_{k}=u+1
if
b_{k}>0
and
u+s_{k}=u-1
if
b_{k}<0
. Then formula (
5.84
) implies that

\Re\lambda_{m+q}^{0}-\Re\lambda_{m+p}^{0}=\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,u+s_{k}}-\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,u}=\frac{\arg(c_{k})+2\pi(u+s_{k})}{b_{k}}-\frac{\arg(c_{k})+2\pi u}{b_{k}}=\frac{2\pi}{|b_{k}|}.

Hence there exists
r=r_{m}\in\{p,p+1,\ldots,q-1\}
such that

\Re\lambda_{m+r_{m}+1}^{0}-\Re\lambda_{m+r_{m}}^{0}\geqslant\frac{2\pi}{|b_{k}|\cdot(q-p)}\geqslant\frac{2\pi}{b_{\max}n}=\varepsilon.

Now we can choose the desired sequence
\{m_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
as a sequence one obtains from the set
{\{m+r_{m}+1\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}}
after ordering it and removing repetitions.

(ii)
It follows from (
5.84
) and (
5.80
) that

|\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}-\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}|\geqslant|\Im\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}-\Im\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}|=\left|\frac{\ln|c_{j}|}{b_{j}}-\frac{\ln|c_{k}|}{b_{k}}\right|=:\varepsilon_{j,k}>0,\qquad m,p\in\mathbb{Z}.

(5.85)

It follows from (
5.84
) and (
5.85
) that the sequence of zeros of
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is separated. Namely, separation parameter
\delta
can be chosen as any number less than
\frac{1}{2}\min\{\varepsilon_{j,k}:1\leqslant j<k\leqslant n\}>0
. Hence boundary conditions (
5.78
) are strictly regular.

(iii)
It is clear that boundary conditions (
5.78
) are strictly regular if and only if for each
j\neq k
arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
are asymptotically separated. Thus, we need to show that for each
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
such that
j\neq k
, arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
are asymptotically separated if and only if condition (
5.81
) is satisfied. To this end, let
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,
j\neq k
, be fixed.

First assume that
\alpha:=\alpha_{jk}:=b_{j}/b_{k}\not\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then condition (
5.81
) is equivalent to (
5.80
). Part (ii) of the lemma implies that if condition (
5.80
) is satisfied, then arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
are separated.
Now let condition (
5.80
) be violated. In this case

\Im\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}=\Im\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}=-\frac{\ln|c_{j}|}{b_{j}}=-\frac{\ln|c_{k}|}{b_{k}},\qquad p,m\in\mathbb{Z},

i.e. the progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
lie on the same line parallel to the real axis. Hence for each
p,m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have,

\displaystyle|\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}-\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}|=\left|{\frac{\arg(c_{j})}{b_{j}}-\frac{\arg(c_{k})}{b_{k}}+2\pi\left(\frac{p}{b_{j}}-\frac{m}{b_{k}}\right)}\right|=\frac{2\pi}{b_{j}}\cdot|r+p-\alpha m|,

(5.86)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad r:=r_{jk}:=\frac{\arg(c_{j})-\alpha\arg(c_{k})}{2\pi}\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.87)

Since
\alpha
is irrational, the Kronecker theorem ensures that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
M>0
there exist
p,m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|p|,|m|>M
and
|r+p-\alpha m|<\varepsilon
. This means that arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
are not asymptotically separated and finishes the proof when
b_{j}/b_{k}\notin\mathbb{Q}
.

Now let
\alpha=b_{j}/b_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
. As before, part (ii) of the lemma implies that if condition (
5.80
) is satisfied, then arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
are separated. Assuming that condition (
5.80
) is violated let us express a criterion for arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
to be asymptotically separated. As in the previous case, arithmetic progressions
\Lambda^{\per}_{j}
and
\Lambda^{\per}_{k}
lie on the same line parallel to the real axis and condition (
5.86
) holds. Since
\alpha
is rational, the union of these progressions is asymptotically separated if and only if they have no common entries. Due to (
5.86
) this is equivalent to the fact that Diophantine equation
p-\alpha m=-r
does not have integer solutions
p,m
. It is well-known that such equation has solutions if and only if
r/\gcd(\alpha,1)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
r=\frac{\arg(c_{j})-\alpha\arg(c_{k})}{2\pi}
and
\alpha=\frac{b_{j}}{b_{k}}
, condition
\frac{r}{\gcd(\alpha,1)}\in\mathbb{Z}
is equivalent to
\frac{b_{j}\arg(c_{k})-b_{k}\arg(c_{j})}{2\pi\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Comparing this with (
5.81
), we see that the proof is now complete.

(iv)
If
c_{1}=\ldots=c_{n}=1
, then
\ln|c_{k}|=0
and
\arg c_{k}=0
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Hence condition (
5.81
) is violated. Which implies that periodic boundary conditions are not strictly regular.

If
c_{1}=\ldots=c_{n}-1
, then
\ln|c_{k}|=0
and
\arg c_{k}=\pi
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Hence condition (
5.81
) turns into

\frac{b_{j}}{b_{k}}\in\mathbb{Q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{b_{j}-b_{k}}{\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})}\quad\text{is odd},\qquad j\neq k.

(5.88)

Let us simplify this condition. Assuming all ratios
b_{j}/b_{k}
to be rational, we can choose a “base”
b_{0}>0
, such that
b_{k}=N_{k}b_{0}
,
N_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Let
N_{k}=2^{a_{k}}M_{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, where
a_{k}\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
and
M_{k}
is odd. Clearly each non-zero integer has unique representation of this form. Let
j\neq k
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
a_{j}\leqslant a_{k}
. Then, with the above representation in mind, we have

b_{j}-b_{k}=2^{a_{j}}b_{0}(M_{j}-2^{a_{k}-a_{j}}M_{k}),\qquad\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})=2^{a_{j}}b_{0}\gcd(M_{j},M_{k}).

Since
M_{j}
and
M_{k}
are odd, it is clear, that the ratio
(b_{j}-b_{k})/\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})
is odd if and only if
a_{j}\neq a_{k}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Example 5.19
.

In some cases we can select the “blocks” in inequality (
5.79
) and the number
\varepsilon
more explicitly. For instance, if
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}>0
, and
b_{j}/b_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
,
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, we show that
\varepsilon
in (
5.79
) can be chosen as

\varepsilon:=\min\left\{\frac{2\pi\cdot\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})}{|b_{j}b_{k}|}:\ \ j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\ \ j\neq k\right\}.

Indeed, let
j\neq k
be fixed. In this case
\arg(c_{j})=\arg(c_{k})=0
and
b_{j}=N_{j}b_{jk}
,
b_{k}=N_{k}b_{jk}
, where
b_{jk}=\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})>0
,
N_{j},N_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, formula (
5.84
) implies that

\Re\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}=\frac{2\pi p}{b_{jk}N_{j}},\qquad\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}=\frac{2\pi m}{b_{jk}N_{k}},\qquad p,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

Hence for pairs of eigenvalues
\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}
,
\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}
, satisfying
\Re\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}\neq\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}
one derives

|\Re\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}-\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}|=\frac{2\pi\cdot|N_{k}p-N_{j}m|}{b_{jk}|N_{j}N_{k}|}\geqslant\frac{2\pi}{b_{jk}|N_{j}N_{k}|}=\frac{2\pi\cdot\gcd(b_{j},b_{k})}{|b_{j}b_{k}|}\geqslant\varepsilon>0.

(5.89)

Therefore putting the eigenvalues
\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}
,
\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}
with
\Re\lambda^{\per}_{j,p}=\Re\lambda^{\per}_{k,m}
in one block we conclude from (
5.89
) that estimate (
5.79
) holds with the desired
\varepsilon
.

Note also that if there are infinitely many pairs of eigenvalues
with equal real parts one cannot replace the second inequality
in (
5.79
) by
n_{k+1}\leqslant n_{k}+1
for

n_{k}
big enough. As a consequence of this fact, the system of
root vectors of the BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
),
forms a Riesz basis only with parentheses (cf. Corollary

9.21
).

The next result establishes similar criterion for a certain subclass of separated boundary conditions.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
n=2N
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}

satisfy the following condition,

b_{1}<0<b_{2},\quad b_{3}<0<b_{4},\quad\ldots,\quad b_{n-1}<0<b_{n}.

(5.90)

Further, let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be of the form

c_{2k-1}y_{2k-1}(0)+c_{2k}y_{2k}(0)=0,\qquad d_{2k-1}y_{2k-1}(1)+d_{2k}y_{2k}(1)=0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\},

(5.91)

where
c_{j},d_{j}\neq 0
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, i.e.
U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0
, where

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}c_{3}&c_{4}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\ldots,\begin{pmatrix}c_{n-1}&c_{n}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right),

(5.92)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
d_{1}&d_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
d_{3}&d_{4}\end{pmatrix},\ldots,\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
d_{n-1}&d_{n}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(5.93)

Then boundary conditions (
5.91
) are regular. Set

\sigma_{k}:=b_{2k}-b_{2k-1}>0,\qquad\tau_{k}:=\frac{c_{2k}d_{2k-1}}{c_{2k-1}d_{2k}}\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}.

(5.94)

(i)
Let
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the
sequence of zeros of the characteristic determinant

\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
and assume it is ordered in such a way that

\Re\lambda_{m}^{0}\leqslant\Re\lambda_{m+1}^{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then there
exists a sequence of integers
\{m_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, such that

m_{k}<m_{k+1}\leqslant m_{k}+n/2,\quad\Re\lambda_{m_{k}}^{0}-\Re\lambda_{m_{k}-1}^{0}\geqslant\varepsilon,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

(5.95)

where

\varepsilon:=\frac{4\pi}{\sigma_{\max}n}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{\max}:=\max\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N}\}=\max\{b_{2}-b_{1},b_{4}-b_{3},\ldots,b_{n}-b_{n-1}\}.

(ii)
Let numbers
\left\{\frac{\ln|\tau_{k}|}{\sigma_{k}}\right\}_{k=1}^{N}
be distinct, i.e.

\sigma_{j}\ln|\tau_{k}|\neq\sigma_{k}\ln|\tau_{j}|,\qquad j\neq k,\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}.

(5.96)

Then boundary conditions (
5.91
) are strictly regular. In particular, this is always the case if
n=2
.

(iii)
More precisely, boundary conditions (
5.91
) are strictly regular if and only if for all
j\neq k
the following condition holds,

\text{either}\quad\sigma_{j}\ln|\tau_{k}|\neq\sigma_{k}\ln|\tau_{j}|\quad\text{or}\quad\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sigma_{k}}\in\mathbb{Q}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\sigma_{j}\arg(\tau_{k})-\sigma_{k}\arg(\tau_{j})}{2\pi\gcd(\sigma_{j},\sigma_{k})}\not\in\mathbb{Z}\right).

(5.97)

Proof.

Condition (
5.90
) implies that

P_{+}=\diag(0,1,0,1,\ldots,0,1),\qquad P_{-}=\diag(1,0,1,0,\ldots,1,0).

where “projectors”
P_{\pm}
are defined in (
5.41
). Hence for determinant
J_{P_{-}}(C,D)
given by (
5.40
) we have,

J_{P_{-}}(C,D)=\det\left(\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&c_{2}\\
d_{1}&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&c_{4}\\
d_{3}&0\end{pmatrix},\ldots,\begin{pmatrix}0&c_{n}\\
d_{n-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\right)\neq 0,

since numbers
c_{j},d_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
are non-zero. Similarly
J_{P_{+}}(C,D)\neq 0
. This implies regularity of boundary conditions. Further, it is clear that

C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
d_{1}e^{i\lambda b_{1}}&d_{2}e^{i\lambda b_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}c_{3}&c_{4}\\
d_{3}e^{i\lambda b_{3}}&d_{4}e^{i\lambda b_{4}}\end{pmatrix},\ldots,\begin{pmatrix}c_{n-1}&c_{n}\\
d_{n-1}e^{i\lambda b_{n-1}}&d_{n}e^{i\lambda b_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\right).

Hence the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
defined in (
5.13
) becomes

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\det(C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda))=\prod_{k=1}^{N}\bigl(c_{2k-1}d_{2k}e^{i\lambda b_{2k}}-c_{2k}d_{2k-1}e^{i\lambda b_{2k-1}}\bigr).

(5.98)

Let
\Lambda^{\separ}_{k}=\{\lambda^{\separ}_{k,m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, be the sequences of zeros of the
k
-th factor
in this product. Clearly,

\lambda^{\separ}_{k,m}=\frac{-i\ln\tau_{k}+2\pi m}{\sigma_{k}}=\frac{\arg(\tau_{k})+2\pi m}{\sigma_{k}}-i\frac{\ln|\tau_{k}|}{\sigma_{k}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\},

(5.99)

where
\sigma_{k}
and
\tau_{k}
are given by (
5.94
).
Thus, each sequence
\Lambda_{k}^{\separ}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}
, is algebraically simple and constitutes an arithmetic progression that lies on the line parallel to the real axis. From here the proof if finished the same way as in Lemma
5.18
.
∎

6
Asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenvectors

6.1
The key identity for characteristic determinant

Here we present the key formula relating the characteristic determinants
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
and
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
) and let
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)
and
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
be the characteristic determinants of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) and BVP (
5.8
), (
5.2
), respectively, given by (
5.13
). Then there exists function
g\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
, where
b_{\pm}
are defined in (
5.38
), such that the following identity holds

\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=\Delta_{0}(\lambda)+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(6.1)

In addition, entries of adjugate matrices
A_{Q}^{a}(\lambda)
and
A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)
, given by (
5.14
)–(
5.15
), are connected via

A_{kp}(\lambda)=A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g_{kp}(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\quad k,p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(6.2)

for some
g_{kp}\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
,
k,p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
be fixed. Since
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=\det(C+D\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda))
, applying formula (
2.26
) one gets,

\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)=\det(C)+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}C[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot D[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}],

(6.3)

where notations
\mathfrak{P}_{m}
,
\mathcal{A}[\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}]
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
were introduced in Subsection
2.5
. To transform (
6.3
), we will apply formula (
4.56
) for
\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]
. To this end, observe that diagonal structure of the matrix function
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
,
{\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)=\diag(e^{i\lambda\rho_{1}(\cdot)},\ldots,e^{i\lambda\rho_{n}(\cdot)})}
, implies

\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]=\delta_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}\exp\left(i\lambda\rho_{\mathfrak{r}}(\ell)\right),\qquad\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(6.4)

where
\rho_{\mathfrak{r}}(\ell)=\rho_{r_{1}}(\ell)+\ldots+\rho_{r_{m}}(\ell)
,
\mathfrak{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{m})
, and was defined in (
4.52
). Thus, setting
x=\ell
in (
4.56
) and taking into account formula (
6.4
), we get

\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]=\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]+\int_{\tau_{m}^{-}(\ell)}^{\tau_{m}^{+}(\ell)}R_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(\ell,u)e^{i\lambda u}du,\qquad\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(6.5)

With account of notations (
4.53
)–(
4.54
) and notation
b_{k}=\rho_{k}(\ell)
, we get

\displaystyle\tau_{m}^{-}(\ell)=\min\{b_{1}+\ldots+b_{m},0\},\qquad\tau_{m}^{+}(\ell)=\max\{b_{n-m+1}+\ldots+b_{n},0\},\qquad m\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(6.6)

It is clear from the canonical ordering
b_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n_{-}}<0<b_{n_{-}+1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant b_{n}
, definition (
5.38
) of
b_{\pm}
and identities (
6.6
) that

[\tau_{m}^{-}(\ell),\tau_{m}^{+}(\ell)]\subset[b_{-},b_{+}].

(6.7)

Hence by setting

g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(u):=\begin{cases}R_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(\ell,u),&u\in[\tau_{m}^{-}(\ell),\tau_{m}^{+}(\ell)],\\
0,&u\in[b_{-},b_{+}]\setminus[\tau_{m}^{-}(\ell),\tau_{m}^{+}(\ell)],\end{cases}\quad\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(6.8)

we can further transform (
6.5
) into

\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]=\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(u)e^{i\lambda u}du,\qquad\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}.

(6.9)

Note also, that condition (
4.55
) implies inclusion
g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
,
\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
.

Inserting (
6.9
) into (
6.3
) and using (
6.3
) for
Q=0

we get

\displaystyle\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\det(C)+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}C[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]D[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot\left(\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)[\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}]+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(u)e^{i\lambda u}du\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}(\lambda)+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g(u)e^{i\lambda u}du,

(6.10)

where

g(u):=\sum_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma(\mathfrak{p})+\sigma(\mathfrak{q})}C[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot D[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{r}]\cdot g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}(u).

(6.11)

Since
g_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}}\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
,
\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{m}
, it is clear that
g\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
, which finishes the proof of formula (
6.1
).

Formula (
6.2
) can be obtained the same way as above by using Lemma
2.7
instead of Lemma
2.6
and taking into account notations (
5.16
).
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Note that if
n_{-}=0
, and so
b_{-}=0
and
b_{+}=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n}
, then integration limits in (
6.2
) can be reduced from
[0,b_{+}]
to
[0,b_{+}-b_{1}]
. Similar effect happens if
n_{-}=n
.

For completeness of exposition, let us obtain similar Fourier transform related representation for vector functions
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)
defined in (
5.18
). Study of these vector functions is motivated by Lemma
5.1
and their appearance as eigenvectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. To this end, let us set

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{-}(x)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{-}=\min\{\rho_{1}(x),0\}+b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n_{-}},\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(6.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{+}(x)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{+}=\min\{\rho_{n}(x),0\}+b_{n_{-}+1}+\ldots+b_{n},\qquad x\in[0,\ell].

(6.13)

where
\rho_{1}^{-}(x)
,
\rho_{n}^{+}(x)
are defined in (
4.42
) and
b_{\pm}
are defined in (
5.38
).

Proposition 6.3
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
) and let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then there exists a measurable vector kernel
G_{p}
defined on

\widehat{\Omega}:=\{(x,u):x\in[0,\ell],u\in[\widehat{\rho}_{-}(x),\widehat{\rho}_{+}(x)]\}

such that for each
x\in[0,\ell]
, a trace function
G_{p}(x,\cdot)
is well-defined, summable,

\sup_{x\in[0,\ell]}\int_{\widehat{\rho}_{-}(x)}^{\widehat{\rho}_{+}(x)}\left\|{G_{p}(x,u)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\,du<\infty,

(6.14)

and the following representation holds

Y_{p}(x,\lambda)=Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+\int_{\widehat{\rho}_{-}(x)}^{\widehat{\rho}_{+}(x)}G_{p}(x,u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(6.15)

where
Y_{p}(x,\lambda)
and
Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)
are defined in (
5.18
)–(
5.19
).

Proof.

Let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,
x\in[0,\ell]
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
be fixed for the entire proof. Inserting formula (
4.45
) for
\Phi_{p}(x,\lambda)
and formula (
6.2
) into formula (
5.18
) for
Y_{p}(x,\lambda)
and taking into account formula (
5.19
) for
Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)
we get

\displaystyle Y_{p}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)+\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}g_{kp}(u)\,du\right)\left(\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)+\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda u}\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du\right)

\displaystyle=Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda u}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}g_{kp}(u)\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)\,du

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}g_{kp}(u)\,du\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda u}\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du.

(6.16)

Let us analyze each sum in r.h.s. of (
6.16
).

1st sum.
Note, that
A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)
has a formula similar to (
5.43
),

A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\gamma_{kp}^{[P]}\cdot e^{i\lambda b_{P}},\qquad k,p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad

(6.17)

with some coefficients
\gamma_{kp}^{[P]}
that only depend on matrices
C
and
D
from boundary conditions. Recall that
b_{P}=p_{1}b_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}b_{n}
, where
P=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=P^{2}
.
Hence the first sum in r.h.s. of (
6.16
) turns into

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda u}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\gamma_{kp}^{[P]}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda(u+b_{P})}\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\gamma_{kp}^{[P]}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{P}}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{P}}e^{i\lambda v}\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,v-b_{P})\,dv

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{-}}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{+}}e^{i\lambda v}G_{1p}(x,v)\,dv,

(6.18)

with some
G_{1p}
that satisfy condition (
6.14
). Here we used the fact that
b_{-}=\min\{b_{P}:P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\}
and
b_{+}=\max\{b_{P}:P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\}
.

2nd sum.
Due to explicit formula

\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)=e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}\col(\delta_{1k},\ldots,\delta_{nk}),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

the second sum in r.h.s. of (
6.16
) turns into

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}g_{kp}(u)\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda(u+\rho_{k}(x)}g_{kp}(u)\,du\cdot\col(\delta_{1k},\ldots,\delta_{nk})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{k}(x)+b_{-}}^{\rho_{k}(x)+b_{+}}e^{i\lambda v}g_{kp}(v-\rho_{k}(x))\,dv\cdot\col(\delta_{1k},\ldots,\delta_{nk})

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{-}}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{+}}e^{i\lambda v}G_{2p}(x,v)\,dv,

(6.19)

with some
G_{2p}
that satisfy condition (
6.14
). Here, we used the fact that
\rho_{1}^{-}(x)\leqslant\rho_{k}(x)\leqslant\rho_{n}^{+}(x)
.

3rd sum.
Finally, for the third sum in r.h.s. of (
6.16
) we have after doing a change of variable
t=u+v
and changing order of integration

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}g_{kp}(u)\,du

\displaystyle\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}e^{i\lambda u}\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,u)\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}\left(\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda(u+v)}g_{kp}(u)\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,v)\,du\right)\,dv

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}\left(\int_{b_{-}+v}^{b_{+}+v}e^{i\lambda t}g_{kp}(t-v)\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,v)\,dt\right)\,dv

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{-}}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{+}}e^{i\lambda t}\left(\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)}g_{kp}(t-v)\widetilde{R}_{k}(x,v)\,dv\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{1}^{-}(x)+b_{-}}^{\rho_{n}^{+}(x)+b_{+}}e^{i\lambda t}G_{3p}(x,t)\,dt,

(6.20)

with some
G_{3p}
that satisfy condition (
6.14
).

Inserting formulas we obtained for each sum above into (
6.16
), we arrive at (
4.45
) with
G_{p}:=G_{1p}+G_{2p}+G_{3p}
.
∎

6.2
Asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues

To effectively estimate integral term in representations (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) we need the following simple generalization of Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma.

Lemma 6.4

(cf. Lemma 3.5 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
a_{-}\leqslant 0\leqslant a_{+}
and let
f\in L^{1}[a_{-},a_{+}]
. Then for any
\delta>0
there exists
R_{\delta}>0
such that the following estimate holds,

\left|{\int_{a_{-}}^{a_{+}}f(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du}\right|<\delta\cdot\left(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot a_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot a_{+}}\right),\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\delta},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(6.21)

The following result generalizes Lemma
5.9
and establishes certain important properties of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
as entire function of exponential type.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
), let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular and let
\Delta(\cdot)=\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
be the characteristic determinant of the problem (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) given by (
5.13
). Then the following statements hold:

(i)
The characteristic determinant
\Delta(\cdot)
is a
sine-type function with
h_{\Delta}(\pi/2)=-b_{-}
and

h_{\Delta}(-\pi/2)=b_{+}
. In particular,
\Delta(\cdot)
has
infinitely many zeros

\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

(6.22)

counting multiplicity and
\Lambda\subset\Pi_{h}
for some
h>0
.

(ii)
The sequence
\Lambda
is incompressible.

(iii)
For any
\varepsilon>0
the determinant

\Delta(\cdot)
admits the following estimate from below

|\Delta(\lambda)|>C_{\varepsilon}(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}})>C_{\varepsilon},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{m}),

(6.23)

with some
C_{\varepsilon}>0
.

(iv)
The sequence
\Lambda
can be ordered in such a
way that the following asymptotical formula holds

\lambda_{m}=\frac{2\pi m}{b_{+}-b_{-}}+o(m)\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty.

(6.24)

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
[
33
, Proposition 4.6]
with only a few minor differences. For reader’s convenience we show the full proof here.

(i)
Let
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
be the characteristic
determinant of the problem (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) with

Q=0
. It easily follows from (
5.53
) that

\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
admits a representation

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}d\sigma_{0}(u),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(6.25)

with a piecewise constant function
\sigma_{0}(\cdot)
having up to
2^{n}
jump-points
\left\{\sum_{k\in S}b_{k}:S\subset\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\right\}
. Regularity of boundary conditions and formula (
5.53
) imply that

\sigma_{0}(b_{-}+0)-\sigma_{0}(b_{-})=J_{P_{-}}(C,D)\neq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{0}(b_{+})-\sigma_{0}(b_{+}-0)=J_{P_{+}}(C,D)\neq 0.

(6.26)

Proposition
6.1
implies representation (
6.1
) with certain
g\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
.
Let us set

\sigma(u)=\sigma_{0}(u)+\int_{b_{-}}^{u}g(s)ds,\qquad u\in[b_{-},b_{+}].

(6.27)

Combining these notations with formulas (
6.1
)
and (
6.25
) we arrive at the following representation for
the characteristic determinant

\Delta(\lambda)=\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}e^{i\lambda u}d\sigma(u),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(6.28)

It follows from (
6.27
) and (
6.26
) that

\sigma(b_{-}+0)-\sigma(b_{-})=J_{P_{-}}(C,D)\neq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma(b_{+})-\sigma(b_{+}-0)=J_{P_{+}}(C,D)\neq 0.

(6.29)

Due to the property (
6.29
) representation (
6.28
)
ensures that
\Delta(\cdot)
is a sine-type function with

h_{\Delta_{0}}(\pi/2)=-b_{-}
and
h_{\Delta_{0}}(-\pi/2)=b_{+}

(see
[
28
]
). Moreover, statement
(i)
is also
implied by the representation (
6.28
) (see
[
26
, Chapter
1.4.3]
).

(ii)
and
(iii)
. These statements coincide
with the corresponding statements of
[
22
, Lemmas 3 and
4]
for sine-type functions (see also
[
28
, Lemma
22.1]
in connection with part
(iii)
).

(iv)
The proof is the same as in
[
33
, Proposition 4.6(iv)]
.
∎

Based on Lemma
5.9
the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
given by (
5.13
) has the same properties provided that boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular. Recall, that
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the sequence of its zeros counting multiplicity. Let us order the sequence
\Lambda_{0}
in a (possibly non-unique) way such that
\Re\lambda_{m}^{0}\leqslant\Re\lambda_{m+1}^{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. The following result establishes a key asymptotic formula for zeros of
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
(eigenvalues of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
).

Theorem 6.6
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
), in particular

Q_{jk}\equiv 0\quad\text{whenever}\quad\beta_{j}\equiv\beta_{k},\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(6.30)

Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular and let
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequence of eigenvalues (counting multiplicity) of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
(sequence of zeros of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
). Then operator
L_{U}(Q)
has a discrete spectrum and the sequence
\Lambda=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of its eigenvalues (counting multiplicity), which is the sequence of zeros the characteristic determinants
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)
of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) given by (
5.13
), can be ordered in such a way that the following asymptotic formula holds

\lambda_{m}=\lambda_{m}^{0}+o(1)\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.31)

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. By Proposition
6.5
(iii) there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the estimate (
6.23
) for
\Delta(\cdot)
holds, On the other hand, it follows from Lemma
6.4
with
\delta=C_{\varepsilon}
and Proposition
6.1
that

|\Delta(\lambda)-\Delta_{0}(\lambda)|=\left|{\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du}\right|<C_{\varepsilon}(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}}),\qquad|\lambda|\geqslant\ R_{\varepsilon},

(6.32)

with certain
R_{\varepsilon}>0
. Combining estimate (
6.23
) with (
6.32
) yields

\displaystyle|\Delta(\lambda)-\Delta_{0}(\lambda)|<|\Delta(\lambda)|,\qquad\lambda\not\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon},

(6.33)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{D}_{R_{\varepsilon}}(0)\cup\Omega_{\varepsilon},\qquad\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{m}).

(6.34)

The proof is finished the same way as the proof of
[
33
, Proposition 4.7]
by using
[
33
, Lemma 4.3]
(certain geometric property of incompressible sequences) and Rouché theorem.
∎

6.3
Asymptotic behavior of root vectors

Based on Lemma
5.1
, for a given
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, vector function
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)
given by (
5.18
) is the eigenvector of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda
, provided that this function is not zero. To obtain asymptotic behavior of such eigenvectors we first need the following asymptotic result.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
h\geqslant 0
and
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then the following uniform asymptotic formula holds:

Y_{p}(x,\lambda)=Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)+o(1),\quad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h}.

(6.35)

Proof.

Let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed for the entire proof. Recall, that by definition,

Y_{p}(x,\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda),\qquad Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(6.36)

Hence

Y_{p}(x,\lambda)-Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\lambda)(\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)-\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda))+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(A_{kp}(\lambda)-A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda))\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda),

(6.37)

for
x\in[0,\ell]
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

Let
\delta>0
. It follows from Lemma
6.4
and formula (
6.2
) (see Proposition
6.1
) that for given
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
we have

|A_{kp}(\lambda)-A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)|=\left|{\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g_{kp}(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du}\right|<\delta\cdot(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}}),\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\delta},

(6.38)

for some
R_{\delta}>0
. This implies that

|A_{kp}(\lambda)-A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)|<\delta\cdot(e^{-hb_{-}}+e^{hb_{+}})=:\varepsilon,\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\delta},\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(6.39)

It follows from asymptotic formula (
4.40
) for
\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)
that

\left\|{\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)-\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}<\varepsilon,\qquad x\in[0,\ell]\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(6.40)

for some
R_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\geqslant R_{\delta}
.
It follows from Lemma
5.4
that

|A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)|\leqslant M,\qquad\left\|{\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\leqslant M,\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(6.41)

for some
M>0
. Combining (
6.41
) and (
6.39
) we see that

|A_{kp}(\lambda)|\leqslant M+\varepsilon,\qquad|\lambda|>R_{\delta},\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(6.42)

Inserting all of the above estimates into (
6.37
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\left\|{Y_{p}(x,\lambda)-Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}|A_{kp}(\lambda)|\cdot\left\|{\Phi_{k}(x,\lambda)-\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda))}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}|A_{kp}(\lambda)-A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)|\cdot\left\|{\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda)}\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}((M+\varepsilon)\varepsilon+\varepsilon M)

\displaystyle=2n\varepsilon(2M+\varepsilon),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],\quad|\lambda|>R_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\quad\lambda\in\Pi_{h}.

(6.43)

Since
\delta>0
can be chosen arbitrary small and
\varepsilon=\delta\cdot(e^{-hb_{-}}+e^{hb_{+}})
, then estimate (
6.43
) implies desired uniform asymptotic relation (
6.35
).
∎

Remark 6.8
.

It might be tempting to use Proposition
6.3
to prove Lemma
6.7
, but, unfortunately, as explained in Remark
4.6
, the properties of the vector kernel
G_{p}
from representation (
6.15
) are not sufficient to prove relation (
6.35
)
uniformly
at
x\in[0,\ell]
.

Now we are ready to state asymptotic result for root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. Going forward we will call sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vectors in
\mathfrak{H}

normalized
if
\|\varphi_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
), let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular and let
\Lambda=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\Lambda_{0}=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequences of zeros of characteristic determinants
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
and
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
, respectively, satisfying asymptotic formula (
6.31
).

(i)
Let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Then the following asymptotic formula holds uniformly at
x\in[0,\ell]

Y_{p}(x,\lambda_{m})=Y_{p}^{0}(x,\lambda_{m}^{0})+o(1)\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z},

(6.44)

where vector functions
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)
and
Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
are given by (
5.18
)–(
5.19
). In particular,

\left\|{Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})-Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})}\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.45)

(ii)
Let in addition boundary conditions (
5.2
) be strictly regular, then one can choose normalized system of root vector
\{f_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
and normalized system of root vector
\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{U}(0)
such that the following relation holds

\displaystyle\|f_{m}-f_{m}^{0}\|_{\infty}:=\|f_{m}-f_{m}^{0}\|_{C([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.46)

Moreover, for sufficiently large
|m|
, eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}
and
\lambda_{m}^{0}
are simple, and the corresponding eigenvectors
f_{m}
and
f_{m}^{0}
admit the following representation,

f_{m}(\cdot)=\alpha_{m}Y_{p_{m}}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}),\qquad f_{m}^{0}(\cdot)=\alpha_{m}^{0}Y_{p_{m}}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0}),

(6.47)

for some
\alpha_{m},\alpha_{m}^{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and
p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
\delta>0
. By Proposition
6.5
,
\lambda_{m},\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, for some
h\geqslant 0
. It is also clear, that
\lambda_{m}\to\infty
and
\lambda_{m}^{0}\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
. Hence Lemma
6.7
implies that

\left\|{Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})-Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})}\right\|_{\infty}<\delta,\qquad|m|\geqslant\widetilde{m}_{\delta},

(6.48)

for some
\widetilde{m}_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N}
. Since boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular, then by Theorem
6.6
there exists
m_{\delta}\geqslant\widetilde{m}_{\delta}
such that

|\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{m}^{0}|<\delta,\qquad|m|>m_{\delta}.

(6.49)

It follows from (
5.27
) and (
6.49
) that

\displaystyle\left\|{Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})-Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sup_{\lambda\in\Pi_{h}}\left\|{\frac{d}{d\lambda}Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)}\right\|_{\infty}|\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{m}^{0}|

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{h}\cdot\delta,\qquad|m|>m_{\delta}.

(6.50)

Since
\delta>0
can be chosen arbitrarily small, estimates (
6.48
), (
6.50
) imply desired relation (
6.44
).

Finally, note that for any
f=\col(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\in C([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
we have,

\|f\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\ell}|f_{k}(x)|^{2}|\beta_{k}(x)|dx\leqslant\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot(|b_{1}|+\ldots+|b_{n}|)=\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot(b_{+}-b_{-}).

(6.51)

Relation (
6.45
) is now implied by (
6.44
) and (
6.51
).

(ii)
Strict regularity of boundary conditions (
5.2
) and asymptotic formula (
6.31
) imply that for some
m_{1}\geqslant m_{0}
, eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}
and
\lambda_{m}^{0}
,
|m|>m_{1}
, of operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}(0)
are algebraically and geometrically simple. By Lemma
5.15
, there exist indices
p=p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
q=q_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, and a constant
C_{2}>0
such that estimate (
5.65
) holds, i.e.
|A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\geqslant C_{2}
,
|m|>m_{0}
. Emphasize, that although
p
and
q
depend on
m
, the constant
C_{2}
in the above estimate does not. By the proof of Proposition
5.16
, this choice of
p=p_{m}
guarantees uniform estimates (
5.68
),

C_{3}\leqslant\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant C_{4},\qquad|m|>m_{0},

(6.52)

where
C_{4}>C_{3}>0
do not depend on
m
. Combining this estimate with relation (
6.45
) imply that for some
m_{2}\geqslant m_{1}
, we have

C_{3}/2\leqslant\|Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\geqslant 2C_{4},\qquad|m|>m_{2}.

(6.53)

Note also that trivial estimates (
5.27
)–(
5.28
) provide uniform estimate on
\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\|_{\infty}
,
\lambda\in\Pi_{h}
. Hence,

\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{5},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},

(6.54)

for some
C_{5}>0
that does not depend on
m
and
p=p_{m}
.

Since vector functions
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})
and
Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})
are non-zero for
|m|>m_{2}
, Lemma
5.1
implies that they are proper eigenvectors of the operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}(0)
corresponding to simple eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}
and
\lambda_{m}^{0}
, respectively. Let us normalize them, by setting

f_{m}(\cdot):=\frac{Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})}{\|Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}},\qquad f_{m}^{0}(\cdot):=\frac{Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})}{\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}},\qquad|m|>m_{2}.

(6.55)

For any vector functions
u,v\in C([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
we have

\left\|{\frac{u}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}-\frac{v}{\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{\|u-v\|_{\infty}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}+\frac{\|v\|_{\infty}\bigl|{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}-\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}\leqslant\frac{\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|u-v\|_{\infty}+\|v\|_{\infty}\|u-v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}.

(6.56)

Setting
u(\cdot)=Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})
and
v(\cdot)=Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})
in this inequality and combining it with relations (
6.44
)–(
6.45
), estimates (
6.52
)–(
6.53
) from below on
\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}
and
\|v\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}
and estimate (
6.54
) on
\|v\|_{\infty}=\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\infty}
from above, we arrive at the desired relation (
6.46
) for sequences
\{f_{m}\}_{|m|>m_{2}}
and
\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{2}}
.
Extending these sequences by arbitrary chosen normalized root vectors of the operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}(0)
corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}
and
\lambda_{m}^{0}
for
|m|\leqslant m_{2}
, we arrive at the normalized system of root vectors
\{f_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, satisfying desired relation (
6.46
). Formula (
6.55
) trivially implies (
6.47
).
∎

Remark 6.10
.

In a very recent paper
[
55
]
L. Rzepnicki obtained sharp asymptotic formulas for deviations
\lambda_{n}-\lambda_{n}^{0}=\delta_{n}+\rho_{n}
in the case of Dirichlet BVP for Dirac system, i.e. system (
5.1
) with
B(\cdot)\equiv\diag(-1,1)
, with
Q\in{L^{p}}({[0,\ell]};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
,
1\leqslant p<2
. Namely,
\delta_{n}
is explicitly expressed via Fourier coefficients and Fourier transforms of
Q_{12}
and
Q_{21}
, while
\{\rho_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{p^{\prime}/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Similar result was obtained for eigenvectors. For Sturm-Liouville operators with singular potentials, A. Gomilko and L. Rzepnicki obtained similar results in another recent paper
[
19
]
.

6.4
The case of matrix function
Q
with non-trivial block diagonal

Our main results on asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, Theorems
6.6
and
6.9
, assume that
Q
satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
). Let us formulate them without this condition by reducing
general case to “zero block diagonal” case using special gauge transform.

To this end, recall that the matrix function
B(\cdot)=B(\cdot)^{*}
has block-diagonal form (
4.9
),

B=\diag(\widetilde{\beta}_{1}I_{n_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{\beta}_{r}I_{n_{r}}),\qquad\widetilde{\beta}_{k}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},

(6.57)

where
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{r}=n
.
Matrix-function
Q(\cdot)
has related block-matrix decomposition,

Q=:(\mathcal{Q}_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{r},\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{jk}\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}\times n_{k}}),\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\}.

(6.58)

Let
Q_{\diag}(\cdot)
be the block diagonal of matrix function
Q(\cdot)
,

Q_{\diag}:=\diag(\mathcal{Q}_{11},\ldots,\mathcal{Q}_{rr}),

(6.59)

and let
W(\cdot)
be the
{n\times n}
-matrix solution of the Cauchy problem

W^{\prime}(x)+Q_{\diag}(x)W(x)=0,\quad x\in[0,\ell],\qquad W(0)=I_{n}.

(6.60)

Since
Q_{\diag}
is summable and has block-diagonal form (
6.59
), it is clear that

W(x)=\diag(W_{11}(x),\ldots,W_{rr}(x)),\qquad W_{kk}(x)\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}\times n_{k}},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{r}\},\quad x\in[0,\ell],

(6.61)

and

W,W^{-1}\in\AC([0,\ell],\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}),\qquad W(x)B(x)=B(x)W(x),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(6.62)

Let us also define operator
\mathcal{W}:\mathfrak{H}\to\mathfrak{H}
such that

(\mathcal{W}y)(x)=W(x)y(x),\qquad y\in\mathfrak{H}.

(6.63)

Inclusions
W,W^{-1}\in\AC([0,\ell],\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
imply that
\mathcal{W}
is bounded in
\mathfrak{H}
and have a bounded inverse.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
,
Q_{\diag}(\cdot)
and
W(\cdot)
be given by (
6.57
), (
6.59
), (
6.61
) and satisfy conditions above. Let also operator
\mathcal{W}
be given by (
6.63
). Then the following statements hold:

(i)
Operator
\mathcal{W}
(gauge transform) transforms operator
L_{U}(Q)
to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
with the same matrix function
B(\cdot)
,

L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})=\mathcal{W}^{-1}L_{U}(Q)\mathcal{W},

(6.64)

where

\widetilde{U}(y):=Cy(0)+DW(\ell)y(\ell)=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{Q}:=W^{-1}(Q-Q_{\diag})W.

(6.65)

(ii)
Matrix
\widetilde{Q}
has zero block diagonal with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
.

(iii)
Characteristic determinants corresponding to the operators
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
and
L_{U}(Q)
coincide.

(iv)
Boundary conditions
\widetilde{U}(y)=0
and
U(y)=0
are regular only simultaneously.

Proof.

(i)
Let
y\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
. It follows from (
6.60
) and (
6.62
) that

\mathcal{L}(Q)\cdot\mathcal{W}y=-iB^{-1}(Wy^{\prime}+W^{\prime}y+QWy)=-iB^{-1}(Wy^{\prime}-Q_{\diag}Wy+QWy)\\
=W\bigl(-iB^{-1}(y^{\prime}+W^{-1}(Q-Q_{\diag})Wy)\bigr)=\mathcal{W}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{Q})y.

(6.66)

Since
W(0)=I_{n}
, it is also clear that if
U(\mathcal{W}y)=\widetilde{U}(y)
. Hence (
6.66
) implies (
6.64
).

(ii)
It is clear that
Q-Q_{\diag}
has zero block diagonal with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
. Block-diagonal form (
6.61
) of
W
, implies that
\widetilde{Q}=W^{-1}(Q-Q_{\diag})W
also has zero block diagonal.

(iii)
It follows from (
6.66
) that
\widetilde{\Phi}=W^{-1}\Phi_{Q}
, where
\widetilde{\Phi}
is a fundamental solution of equation (
5.1
) with
\widetilde{Q}
in place of
Q
. With account of this, we have for the characteristic determinant
\widetilde{\Delta}(\cdot)
corresponding to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
,

\widetilde{\Delta}(\lambda):=\det(C+DW(\ell)\widetilde{\Phi}(\ell,\lambda))=\det(C+D\Phi(\ell,\lambda))=\Delta(\lambda),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(6.67)

which implies desired equality of characteristic determinants.

(iv)
Recall that regularity of boundary conditions
U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0
means condition (
5.42
), i.e.

J_{P_{\pm}}(C,D)=\det(CP_{\mp}+DP_{\pm})\neq 0,

(6.68)

where “projectors”
P_{\pm}
are defined in (
5.41
). Block-diagonal structure (
6.61
) of the matrix
W(x)
and definition (
5.41
) of “projectors”
P_{\pm}
imply that

P_{\pm}W(\ell)P_{\pm}=W(\ell)P_{\pm},\qquad P_{\mp}W(\ell)P_{\pm}=0.

It is also clear that
P_{\pm}P_{\pm}=P_{\pm}
and
P_{\pm}P_{\mp}=0
. Hence

J_{P_{\pm}}(C,DW(\ell))=\det(CP_{\mp}+DW(\ell)P_{\pm})\\
=\det(CP_{\mp}+DP_{\pm})\det(P_{\mp}+W(\ell)P_{\pm})=J_{P_{\pm}}(C,D)\prod_{b_{k}>0}\det W_{kk}(\ell).

(6.69)

It is clear that
\prod_{b_{k}>0}\det W_{kk}(\ell)\neq 0
. Hence
J_{P_{\pm}}(C,DW(\ell))\neq 0\Leftrightarrow J_{P_{\pm}}(C,D)
, and definition of regularity (
6.68
) implies that the new boundary conditions
\widetilde{U}(y)=Cy(0)+DW(\ell)y(\ell)
are regular if and only if original boundary conditions
U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)
are regular.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

Note, that similarity of the operators
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
and
L_{U}(Q)
implies that both operators have the same spectrum (counting multiplicity). Moreover,
y
is a root vector of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda
if and only if
\mathcal{W}y
is a root vector of the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda
. Since operator
\mathcal{W}
is bounded in
\mathfrak{H}
and has a bounded inverse, then systems of root vectors of the operators
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
and
L_{U}(Q)
have many spectral properties only simultaneously: completeness, minimality, uniform minimality, Riesz basis property (see corresponding definitions in future sections).

Note that gauge transform changes boundary conditions. Even though characteristic determinant
\Delta(\cdot)
and regularity of boundary conditions is preserved under this transform, the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
changes to
L_{\widetilde{U}}(0)
and they in general have different eigenvalues. This observation motivates the following definition.

Definition 6.13
.

Let
B,Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and
B(x)
is invertible for almost all
x
. Let matrix function
W(\cdot)
be constructed from block diagonal of
Q
using (
6.60
)–(
6.61
). BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) is called
strictly regular
if modified boundary conditions
\widetilde{U}(y)=Cy(0)+DW(\ell)y(\ell)=0
are strictly regular.

Remark 6.14
.

Note that strict regularity of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) is only expressed in terms of matrices
C
,
D
and
Q_{\diag}
and numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
. If
Q_{\diag}\equiv 0
then strict regularity of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) simply means strict regularity of original boundary conditions (
5.2
).

Lemma
6.11
(iv) implies that BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) is strictly regular if and only if boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular, and modified characteristic determinant

\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}(\cdot):=\det(C+DW(\ell)\Phi_{0}(\ell,\cdot))

(6.70)

has countable asymptotically separated sequence of zeros.

Note also, that if boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular, then Lemma
6.11
(iv) and Lemma
5.9
imply that
\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}(\cdot)
has countable sequence of zeros satisfying all the properties from Lemma
5.9
.

Now we are ready to formulate our main results on asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, Theorems
6.6
and
6.9
, for arbitrary summable
Q
.

Theorem 6.15
.

Let matrix function
B(\cdot)
given by (
4.3
) satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) and let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
.
Let matrix function
W(\cdot)
be constructed from the block diagonal
Q_{\diag}
of
Q
using (
6.60
)–(
6.61
). Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular and let
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{0}=\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequence of zeros (counting multiplicity) of the modified characteristic determinant
\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}(\cdot)
given by (
6.70
).

Then operator
L_{U}(Q)
has a countable sequence of eigenvalues
\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
counting multiplicity. The sequence
\Lambda
is incompressible (see Definition
5.7
) and lies in the strip
\Pi_{h}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\Im\lambda|\leqslant h\}
for some
h\geqslant 0
. In addition, the sequences
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{0}
and
\Lambda
can be ordered in such a way that the following asymptotical formulas hold

\lambda_{m}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{m}^{0}+o(1)=\frac{2\pi m}{b_{+}-b_{-}}+o(m)\quad\text{as}\quad m\to\infty,

(6.71)

where
b_{\pm}
are defined in (
5.38
). Moreover, if BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) is strictly regular according to Definition
6.13
(i.e. the sequence
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{0}
is asymptotically separated) then the sequence
\Lambda
is asymptotically separated.

Proof.

Applying gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
, we transform operator
L_{U}(Q)
to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
with the same matrix function
B(\cdot)
, and
\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{Q}
given by (
6.65
) with
\widetilde{Q}
satisfying “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
). Moreover, based on Lemma
6.11
(iii) this transform preserves the characteristic determinant and thus preserves the spectrum. Applying Proposition
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
we arrive at the desired relation (
6.71
) and all the desired properties of the sequence
\Lambda
.
∎

Reformulation of Theorem
6.9
on asymptotic behavior of eigenvectors in the case of general matrix function
Q(\cdot)
is cumbersome and is omitted.

7
Completeness property

Let us recall definition of completeness in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vectors in
\mathcal{H}
is called
complete in
\mathcal{H}
if closure of its span coincides with
\mathcal{H}
. Equivalently, a sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is complete in
\mathcal{H}
if and only if the following implication holds for every
f\in\mathcal{H}
,

(\varphi_{m},f)=0,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad f=0.

(7.1)

Completeness property in
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
of the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
with so called weakly regular boundary conditions in the case of constant (not necessarily self-adjoint) matrix
B(x)\equiv B=\const
and summable potential matrix
Q
was established in
[
43
]
using certain generalization of Birkhoff theorem on asymptotic behavior of solutions of system (
5.1
) in special “narrowed” sectors of
\mathbb{C}
. We need to extend this asymptotic result to the case of
non-constant
B(x)
. To this end let use introduce special “narrowed” sectors
S_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
,

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda:\varepsilon<\arg\lambda<\pi-\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad\varepsilon>0,

(7.2)

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon}^{-}

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda:-\pi+\varepsilon<\arg\lambda<-\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}_{-},\qquad\varepsilon>0,

(7.3)

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon,R}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}:|\lambda|>R\}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\pm},\qquad\varepsilon,R>0,

(7.4)

Proposition 7.2

(cf. Proposition 2.2 in
[
43
]
)
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small. Then for a sufficiently large
R
,
equation (
5.1
) has fundamental matrix solutions
Y^{\pm}(x,\lambda)
,

Y^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}^{\pm}&\ldots&Y_{n}^{\pm}\end{pmatrix},\quad Y_{k}^{\pm}=\col(y_{1k}^{\pm},\ldots,y_{nk}^{\pm}),\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(7.5)

which are analytic with respect to
\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon,R}^{\pm}
and have
the following asymptotic behavior uniformly in
x\in[0,\ell]
,

y_{jk}^{\pm}(x,\lambda)=(\delta_{jk}+o(1))e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)},\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\ \lambda\in S_{\varepsilon,R}^{\pm},\quad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(7.6)

where
\delta_{jk}
is a Kronecker symbol.

Proof.

It is clear that the matrix equation
\mathcal{L}(Q)Y=\lambda Y
,
Y=(y_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{n}
is equivalent to

Y^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=(i\lambda B(x)-Q(x))Y(x,\lambda)

and has the following scalar form

y_{jk}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=i\lambda\beta_{j}(x)y_{jk}(x,\lambda)-\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{js}(x)y_{sk}(x,\lambda).

(7.7)

This formula and formulas below are assumed to be valid for all
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
,
x\in[0,\ell]
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, unless stated otherwise. Relations (
7.7
) can be rewritten as follows,

\frac{d}{dx}\left(e^{-i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)}y_{jk}(x,\lambda)\right)=-e^{-i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)}\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{js}(x)y_{sk}(x,\lambda).

(7.8)

We will look for solution
Y^{\pm}(x,\lambda)
as the solution of (
7.8
) satisfying mixed initial conditions,

\displaystyle y_{jk}^{\pm}(a_{jk}^{\pm},\lambda)

\displaystyle=\delta_{jk},

(7.9)

where
a_{kk}^{\pm}=0
and
a_{jk}^{\pm}
,
j\neq k
, is either
0
or
\ell
and will be chosen later.

Integrating (
7.8
) with account of (
7.9
) we arrive at

y_{jk}^{\pm}(x,\lambda)=\delta_{jk}e^{i\lambda\rho_{j}(x)}-\int_{a_{jk}^{\pm}}^{x}e^{i\lambda(\rho_{j}(x)-\rho_{j}(t))}\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{js}(t)y_{sk}^{\pm}(t,\lambda)dt.

(7.10)

Setting
z_{jk}(x,\lambda):=e^{-i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}y_{jk}(x,\lambda)
, we can rewrite (
7.10
) as follows,

z_{jk}^{\pm}(x,\lambda)=\delta_{jk}-\int_{a_{jk}^{\pm}}^{x}e^{i\lambda\left((\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(x)-(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(t)\right)}\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q_{js}(t)z_{sk}^{\pm}(t,\lambda)dt.

(7.11)

For definiteness consider the case
\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon}^{+}
. It is clear from definition of
S_{\varepsilon}^{+}
that

\Im\lambda\geqslant\delta|\lambda|,\qquad\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon}^{+},

(7.12)

with some
\delta=\delta_{\varepsilon}
that does not depend on
\lambda
.

Recall that
\rho_{k}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\beta_{k}(t)dt
, where functions
\beta_{k}(\cdot)
satisfy uniform separation conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
).
Let
j>k
and assume that
\beta_{j}\not\equiv\beta_{k}
. Condition (
4.7
) implies that

\beta_{j}(u)-\beta_{k}(u)>\theta,\qquad u\in[0,\ell].

Hence

\left|{e^{i\lambda\left((\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(x)-(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(t)\right)}}\right|=\exp\left(-\Im\lambda\int_{t}^{x}(\beta_{j}(u)-\beta_{k}(u))du\right)\\
\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\delta|\lambda|\cdot\theta|x-t|\Bigr),\qquad\beta_{j}>\beta_{k},\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant x\leqslant\ell,\quad\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon}^{+}.

(7.13)

Similarly, if
j<k
and
\beta_{j}\not\equiv\beta_{k}
then the same estimate is valid for
0\leqslant x\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
,

\left|{e^{i\lambda\left((\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(x)-(\rho_{j}-\rho_{k})(t)\right)}}\right|\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\delta|\lambda|\cdot\theta|x-t|\Bigr),\qquad\beta_{j}<\beta_{k},\quad 0\leqslant x\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,\quad\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon}^{+}.

(7.14)

With estimates (
7.13
)–(
7.14
) in mind, we can now set

a_{jk}^{+}:=\begin{cases}0,\ \ \text{if}\ \ \ \beta_{j}(u)\geqslant\beta_{k}(u),\ \ u\in[0,\ell],\\
1,\ \ \text{if}\ \ \ \beta_{j}(u)<\beta_{k}(u),\ \ u\in[0,\ell].\end{cases}

(7.15)

In particular,
a_{jk}^{+}=0
if
\beta_{j}\equiv\beta_{k}
, which agrees with relation
a_{kk}^{+}=0
, we set earlier.

Now if
\beta_{j}\not\equiv\beta_{k}
, estimates (
7.13
)–(
7.14
) imply that for given
t\neq x
the exponential function in the integral (
7.11
) can be arbitrarily small for
\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon,R}^{+}
and sufficiently large
R
.
If
\beta_{j}\equiv\beta_{k}
, then
\rho_{k}\equiv\rho_{j}
and exponential function disappears. In this case we need to insert expressions for such
z_{jk}^{+}(x,\lambda)
terms into other equations in (
7.11
) to obtain a system only on functions
z_{jk}^{+}(x,\lambda)
with
\beta_{j}\not\equiv\beta_{k}
.
The proof is now finished the same way as in
[
43
, Proposition 2.2]
by using Banach fixed point theorem and following
[
52
, Lemma II.4.4.1]
.
∎

Following
[
30
]
we will first establish general completeness property provided that the trace of characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
on certain three rays has a certain asymptotic behavior, and then show that this is the case for regular boundary conditions (
5.2
).

Proposition 7.3
.

Let matrix function
B(\cdot)
given by (
4.3
) satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) and let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
.
Let
\Delta_{Q}(\lambda)
be the characteristic determinants of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) given by (
5.13
).
Assume that there exist
C,M>0
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}:=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
and
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\not\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)
the origin is the interior point of the triangle
\triangle_{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}}
;

(ii)
the following estimate holds

|\Delta(\lambda)|\geqslant\frac{C}{|\lambda|^{s}}\left(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}}\right),\qquad|\lambda|>M,\quad\arg\lambda=\arg z_{k},\quad k\in\{1,2,3\}.

(7.16)

Then operator
L_{U}(Q)
has discrete spectrum and the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
is complete in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Sketch of the proof.

(i)
The proof will be divided into multiple steps following the proofs of
[
43
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
30
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Step 1.
Applying gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
we transform operator
L_{U}(Q)
to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
with the same matrix function
B(\cdot)
, and
\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{Q}
given by (
6.65
) with
\widetilde{Q}=W^{-1}(Q-Q_{\diag})W
satisfying “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
). Moreover, based on Lemma
6.11
(iii) this transform preserves the characteristic determinant. Hence characteristic determinant of the new BVP still satisfies condition (
7.16
). Hence, without loss of generality we can assume that original
Q
satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition.

Step 2.

It is clear that
\Phi_{Q}(x,\cdot)
is an entire function of exponential type for each
x\in[0,\ell]
. Hence
\Delta(\cdot)=\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)=\det(C+D\Phi_{Q}(\ell,\lambda))
is an entire function of exponential type.
In turn, condition (
7.16
) and canonical factorization for entire functions of exponential type imply that

\Delta(\cdot)
has a countable set of zeros of finite multiplicities.
Lemma
5.1
now implies that the operator
L_{U}(Q)
has discrete spectrum. Let
\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the set of (distinct) eigenvalues of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
,
\mu_{k}\neq\mu_{j}
,
k\neq j
, and let
m_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
be the algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\mu_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We used notation
\mu_{k}
to avoid confusion with notation
\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
used in other sections.

Step 3.
Based on the proof of Lemma
5.1
the root subspace
\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{k}}(L_{U}(Q))
of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
is of the following form,

\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{k}}(L_{U}(Q))=\Span\left\{\left.\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}Y_{j}(x,\mu)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{k}}:\ \ p\in\{0,1,\ldots,m_{k}-1\},\ \ j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}\right\},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

(7.17)

where vector functions
Y_{j}(\cdot,\cdot)
are introduced in (
5.18
).

Let
f\in\mathfrak{H}
be a vector orthogonal to the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. Next, we will follow
[
30
, p. 87–88]
(see also step (iii) of the proof of
[
43
, Theorem 1.2]
).

Consider the entire functions

F_{j}(\lambda):=(Y_{j}(\cdot,\lambda),f(\cdot))_{\mathfrak{H}},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(7.18)

Since
f
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{R}_{\mu_{k}}(L_{U}(Q))
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from (
7.17
) that each
\mu_{k}(\in\sigma(L_{U}(Q)))
is a zero of
F_{j}(\cdot)
of multiplicity at least
m_{k}
, i.e.

F_{j}^{(p)}(\mu_{k})=0,\qquad p\in\{0,1,\dots,m_{k}-1\},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(7.19)

Lemma
5.1
implies the multiplicity of
\mu_{k}
as a root of the characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
equals to
m_{k}
. Thus, the ratio

G_{j}(\lambda):=\frac{F_{j}(\lambda)}{\Delta(\lambda)},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(7.20)

is an entire function. Moreover, since functions

Y_{j}(x,\cdot)
and
\Delta(\cdot)
are entire functions of
exponential type then so are
G_{1}(\cdot),\ldots,G_{n}(\cdot)
. Denote

\displaystyle G(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}G_{1}(\lambda)&\ldots&G_{n}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}

(7.21)

\displaystyle Y(\cdot,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}(\cdot,\lambda)&\ldots&Y_{n}(\cdot,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)A^{a}(\lambda),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(7.22)

where
A^{a}(\lambda)
is matrix adjugate to
A(\lambda)=C+D\Phi(\ell,\lambda)
and is defined in (
5.14
). It follows from (
7.18
) and (
7.20
)–(
7.22
)
that

\int_{0}^{1}f^{*}(x)\Phi(x,\lambda)A^{a}(\lambda)dx=\int_{0}^{1}f^{*}(x)Y(x,\lambda)dx=\Delta(\lambda)G(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(7.23)

where
f^{*}(x):=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{f_{1}(x)}&\ldots&\overline{f_{n}(x)}\end{pmatrix}=\overline{f(x)}^{T}
.

Multiplying (
7.23
) by the matrix
A(\lambda)
from the right we get in view of (
5.22
)

\Delta(\lambda)\int_{0}^{1}f^{*}(x)\Phi(x,\lambda)dx=\Delta(\lambda)G(\lambda)A(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(7.24)

or equivalently

\int_{0}^{1}f^{*}(x)\Phi(x,\lambda)dx=G(\lambda)A(\lambda),\quad\lambda\not\in\sigma(L_{U}(Q)).

(7.25)

Now the continuity of the integral in the last equality with
respect to
\lambda
, the discreteness of the set
\sigma(L_{U}(Q))

and definition of
A(\lambda)
yield the following relation

\int_{0}^{1}f^{*}(x)\Phi(x,\lambda)dx=G(\lambda)(C+D\Phi(1,\lambda)),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(7.26)

Step 4.
Let us prove that functions
G_{1}(\cdot),\ldots,G_{n}(\cdot)
are polynomials in
\lambda
by estimating their growth. To this end we consider solutions
Y^{\pm}(x,\lambda)
satisfying asymptotic behavior (
7.6
). Following the proof of
[
43
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
30
, Theorem 3.2]
we can derive that

\Delta_{Q}^{\pm}(\lambda)G_{j}(\lambda)=\left(U^{\pm}_{j}(\cdot,\lambda),f(\cdot)\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=:F_{j}^{\pm}(\lambda),\qquad\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon,R}^{\pm},

(7.27)

where

\displaystyle A_{Q}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=CY^{\pm}(0,\lambda)+DY^{\pm}(\ell,\lambda),

(7.28)

\displaystyle\Delta_{Q}^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\det(A_{Q}^{\pm}(\lambda)),

(7.29)

\displaystyle Y_{jk}^{a,\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=A_{Q}^{\pm}(\lambda)\{j,k\}\quad\text{are entries of the corresponding adjugate matrix},

(7.30)

\displaystyle U^{\pm}_{j}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y_{jk}^{a,\pm}(\lambda)Y_{k}^{\pm}(x,\lambda),\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(7.31)

Following the proof of
[
43
, Theorem 1.2]
we can show using asymptotic behavior (
7.6
) that

F_{j}^{\pm}(\lambda)=o\left(e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{-}}+e^{-\Im\lambda\cdot b_{+}}\right),\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\quad\lambda\in S_{\varepsilon,R}^{\pm}.

Inserting this estimate and the estimate (
7.16
) into (
7.27
) implies that

G_{j}(\lambda)=o(|\lambda|^{s}),\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma_{k},\quad k\in\{1,2,3\},

(7.32)

where
\Gamma_{k}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:\arg\lambda=\arg z_{k}\}
,
k\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Since zero is the interior point of the triangle
\triangle_{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}}
, the rays
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}
divide the
complex plane into three closed sectors
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}
of opening less than
\pi
. Fix
k\in\{1,2,3\}
and
apply the Phragmén-Lindelöf theorem
[
28
, Theorem
6.1]
to the function
G_{j}(\lambda)
considered in the
sector
\Omega_{k}
. Using (
7.32
) we get

\left|G_{j}(\lambda)\right|\leqslant C_{j}|\lambda|^{s},\quad\lambda\in\Omega_{k},

(7.33)

for some
C_{j}>0
, and hence

\left|G_{j}(\lambda)\right|\leqslant C_{j}|\lambda|^{s},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(7.34)

By the Liouville theorem
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
,

G_{j}(\lambda)
is a polynomial of degree not exceeding
s
.

Step 5.
Following
[
30
, p. 89-90]
we can prove that
G_{j}(\cdot)\equiv 0
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. The proof there works for non-constant matrix
B(\cdot)
without any changes. Now it follows from (
7.26
) that

\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\langle\Phi_{j}(x,\lambda),f(x)\bigr\rangle\,dx\equiv 0,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(7.35)

Following
[
43
, Theorem 1.2, step (vi)]
we can show that the vector function
f
satisfying (
7.35
) is zero. Again, the proof there works for non-constant matrix
B(\cdot)
without any changes. This means that the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
is complete, which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.4
.

Let invertible diagonal matrix function
B(\cdot)=B(\cdot)^{*}
satisfy relaxed condition (
5.9
