), i.e.
B\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and every its entry does not change sign on
[0,\ell]
. Let boundary conditions of the boundary value problem (
5.8
), (
5.2
) be regular.

(i)
Then the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(0)
is complete in
\mathfrak{H}
.

(ii)
Let in addition matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfy uniform separation conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) and let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
. Then (
7.16
) holds with
s=0
and the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
is complete in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

(i)
Since boundary conditions are regular and
Q=0
, then estimate (
7.16
) with
s=0
follows from Lemma
5.9
. Namely, we need to combine estimate (
5.48
) and the fact that zeros of
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
lie in the strip
\Pi_{h}
. Hence Proposition
7.3
finishes the proof.

Note that if
Q=0
then special solutions
Y^{\pm}(x,\lambda)
of system
\mathcal{L}(0)Y=\lambda Y
satisfying asymptotic formula (
7.6
) always exist whenever
B\in L^{1}([0,\ell])
. Namely,
Y^{\pm}(x,\lambda)=\Phi^{0}(x,\lambda)
. Hence all steps of the proof of Proposition
7.3
remain valid under relaxed condition (
5.9
) on
B(\cdot)
.

(ii)
Applying gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
we transform operator
L_{U}(Q)
to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
with the same matrix function
B(\cdot)
, and
\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{Q}
given by (
6.65
) with
\widetilde{Q}
satisfying “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
). Moreover, based on Lemma
6.11
(iii-iv) this transform preserves the characteristic determinant and regularity of boundary conditions.
Since new boundary conditions are regular and new characteristic determinant is the same, then estimate (
7.16
) with
s=0
follows from Proposition
6.5
(it is applicable, since
Q
now satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
)). Namely, we need to combine estimate (
6.23
) and the fact that zeros of
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
lie in the strip
\Pi_{h}
. Hence Proposition
7.3
finishes the proof.
∎

8
Adjoint operator

8.1
General properties of the adjoint operator

In our approach to Riesz basis property one needs to work with the biorthogonal system to the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. It is well-known that the properly chosen system of root vectors of the operator
(L_{U}(Q))^{*}
plays this role.
As a first step, we show that, as in the case of
B(x)=B=\const
(see
[
43
]
),
(L_{U}(Q))^{*}=L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})
, i.e. the adjoint operator is also associated to the problem (
5.1
)–(
5.2
), but with another pair of matrices
C,D
and another potential
Q_{*}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let diagonal matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfies relaxed conditions (
5.9
), let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
, and let
L_{U}(Q)
be the operator associated in
\mathfrak{H}
with BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
).

(i)
Let
S=\sign(B(\cdot))
be the signature matrix defined in (
1.9
). Set
Q_{*}:=-SQ^{*}S
. Then there exists a pair of matrices
\{C_{*},D_{*}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
with
\rank(C_{*}\ D_{*})=n
and such that the adjoint operator
(L_{U}(Q))^{*}

is associated in
\mathfrak{H}
with the boundary value problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(Q_{*})y=-iB(x)^{-1}(y^{\prime}+Q_{*}(x)y),\qquad y=\col(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\quad x\in[0,\ell],

(8.1)

\displaystyle U_{*}(y):=C_{*}y(0)+D_{*}y(\ell)=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rank(C_{*}\ D_{*})=n.

(8.2)

In other words,
L_{U}^{*}(Q):=(L_{U}(Q))^{*}=L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})
, where operator
L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})
is generated by the differential expression
\mathcal{L}(Q_{*})
on the domain

\dom((L_{U}(Q))^{*})=\{y\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}):\ \ \mathcal{L}(Q_{*})y\in\mathfrak{H},\ \ C_{*}y(0)+D_{*}y(\ell)=0\}.

(8.3)

(ii)
The boundary conditions
U(y)=Cy(0)+Dy(\ell)=0
and
U_{*}(y)=C_{*}y(0)+D_{*}y(\ell)=0
are regular only simultaneously.

Proof.

It follows from definition (
1.9
) of the signature matrix
S
that

S=S^{*}=S^{-1},\quad S^{2}=I_{n},\qquad|B(x)|=SB(x)=B(x)S.

(8.4)

First, we note that for any
f,g\in L^{\infty}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
we have

\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{|B(x)|\cdot(-iB(x)^{-1})Q(x)f(x),g(x)}\right\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{-iSQ(x)f(x),g(x)}\right\rangle dx\\
=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{f(x),iQ^{*}(x)Sg(x)}\right\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{|B(x)|f(x),-iB(x)^{-1}Q_{*}(x)g(x)}\right\rangle dx

(8.5)

Further, integrating by parts and taking (
8.4
) into account we obtain for
f,g\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
:

\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{|B(x)|\cdot(-iB(x)^{-1})f^{\prime}(x),g(x)}\right\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{-iSf^{\prime}(x),g(x)}\right\rangle dx\\
=-i\bigl(\left\langle{Sf(\ell),g(\ell)}\right\rangle-\left\langle{Sf(0),g(0)}\right\rangle\bigr)+\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\langle{|B(x)|f(x),-iB(x)^{-1}g^{\prime}(x)}\right\rangle dx.

(8.6)

Adding (
8.5
) and (
8.6
) we arrive at

(\mathcal{L}(Q)f,g)_{\mathfrak{H}}=-i\bigl(\left\langle{Sf(\ell),g(\ell)}\right\rangle-\left\langle{Sf(0),g(0)}\right\rangle\bigr)+(f,\mathcal{L}(Q_{*})g)_{\mathfrak{H}},\qquad f,g\in\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(8.7)

Since
\AC([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n})
is dense in
\dom(L_{U}(Q))
, this identity implies
L_{U}^{*}(Q)g=L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})g
and
g\in\dom(L_{U}^{*}(Q))
if and only if

\left\langle{Sf(\ell),g(\ell)}\right\rangle=\left\langle{Sf(0),g(0)}\right\rangle,\qquad f\in\dom(L_{U}(Q)).

(8.8)

This leads to existence of the desired matrices
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
. Namely, put
\mathcal{S}=\diag(S,-S)
and equip the space
\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the bilinear form

w(u,v)=\left\langle{\mathcal{S}u,v}\right\rangle=\left\langle{Su_{1},v_{1}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{Su_{2},v_{2}}\right\rangle,\qquad u=\col(x,t),\quad v=\col(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}).

Then condition (
8.8
) means that the subspace
\Ker(C_{*}\ D_{*})
is the right
w
-orthogonal complement to
\Ker(C\ D)
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

(ii) This statement was proved in
[
43
, Corollary 3.3]
in the case of constant matrix
B(x)=B=\const
. The proof remains the same in the case of non-constant matrix
B(x)
.
∎

Next, assuming boundary conditions (
5.2
) to be regular, we find an explicit form of the matrices
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
. The proof substantially relies on the canonical form (
5.57
) of regular boundary conditions.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let the pair of matrices
\{C,D\}
from regular boundary conditions
Uy=0
be of the canonical form (
5.57
).
Then the matrices
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
from boundary conditions (
8.2
) of the adjoint operator
L_{0,U}^{*}

admit the following triangular block-matrix representation:

C_{*}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}^{*}&\mathbb{O}\\
C_{12}^{*}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}\qquad D_{*}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&D_{21}^{*}\\
\mathbb{O}&C_{22}^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

(8.9)

Proof.

Recall that
S=\diag(-I_{n_{-}},I_{n_{+}})
is a canonical form of the signature matrix of
B(x)
, where
n_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
n_{+}=n-n_{-}
.

(i)
First assume that
n_{-}=0
, and so
n_{+}=n
. Then
D=S=I_{n}
and
f(\ell)=-Cf(0)
for
f\in\dom(L_{0,U})
. Hence (
8.8
) turns into

\left\langle{-Cf(0),g(\ell)}\right\rangle=\left\langle{f(0),g(0)}\right\rangle,\qquad f\in\dom(L_{0,U}),\quad g\in\dom(L_{0,U}^{*}),

or

\left\langle{u,g(0)+C^{*}g(\ell)}\right\rangle=0,\qquad u\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\quad g\in\dom(L_{0,U}^{*}).

Hence
g\in\dom{L_{0,U}^{*}}
if and only if
g(0)+C^{*}g(\ell)=0
,
which leads to the desired formulas for
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
. The case
n_{+}=0
is treated similarly.

(ii)
Now assume that
n_{-},n_{+}>0
in the representation (
5.57
).
For any vector function
f=f(x)
let
f=:\col(f_{-},f_{+})
, where
f_{\pm}(x)\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{\pm}}
,
x\in[0,\ell]
, be its decomposition with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}
. With account of this notation we have for
f\in\dom(L_{0,U})
:

\displaystyle 0=Cf(0)+Df(\ell)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&C_{12}\\
\mathbb{O}&C_{22}\end{pmatrix}\binom{f_{-}(0)}{f_{+}(0)}+\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}&\mathbb{O}\\
D_{21}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}\binom{f_{-}(\ell)}{f_{+}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\binom{f_{-}(0)+C_{12}f_{+}(0)+D_{11}f_{-}(\ell)}{C_{22}f_{+}(0)+D_{21}f_{-}(\ell)+f_{+}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\binom{f_{-}(0)}{f_{+}(\ell)}+\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}&C_{12}\\
D_{21}&C_{22}\end{pmatrix}\binom{f_{-}(\ell)}{f_{+}(0)}=f_{0,\ell}+Mf_{\ell,0},

(8.10)

where

f_{0,\ell}:=\binom{f_{-}(0)}{f_{+}(\ell)},\quad f_{\ell,0}:=\binom{f_{-}(\ell)}{f_{+}(0)},\quad f\in C([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n}),\qquad M:=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}&C_{12}\\
D_{21}&C_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

(8.11)

Recall that
S=\diag(-I_{n_{-}},I_{n_{+}})
,
f=\col(f_{-},f_{+})
and
g=\col(g_{-},g_{+})
are decompositions of the matrix
S
and the vector functions
f
and
g
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}
. Taking into account notation (
8.11
) and explicit formula (
8.10
) for the domain
\dom(L_{0,U})
, we have from (
8.8
):

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left\langle{Sf(0),g(0)}\right\rangle-\left\langle{Sf(\ell),g(\ell)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\binom{-f_{-}(0)}{f_{+}(0)},\binom{g_{-}(0)}{g_{+}(0)}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\binom{-f_{-}(\ell)}{f_{+}(\ell)},\binom{g_{-}(\ell)}{g_{+}(\ell)}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\binom{f_{-}(\ell)}{f_{+}(0)},\binom{g_{-}(\ell)}{g_{+}(0)}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\binom{f_{-}(0)}{f_{+}(\ell)},\binom{g_{-}(0)}{g_{+}(\ell)}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{f_{\ell,0},g_{\ell,0}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{f_{0,\ell},g_{0,\ell}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{f_{\ell,0},g_{\ell,0}}\right\rangle+\left\langle{Mf_{\ell,0},g_{0,\ell}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{f_{\ell,0},g_{\ell,0}+M^{*}g_{0,\ell}}\right\rangle,\qquad f\in\dom(L_{0,U}),\quad g\in\dom(L_{0,U}^{*}).

(8.12)

This implies that
g\in\dom(L_{0,U}^{*})
if and only if

\displaystyle 0=g_{\ell,0}+M^{*}g_{0,\ell}

\displaystyle=\binom{g_{-}(\ell)}{g_{+}(0)}+\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}^{*}&D_{21}^{*}\\
C_{12}^{*}&C_{22}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\binom{g_{-}(0)}{g_{+}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\binom{g_{-}(\ell)+D_{11}^{*}g_{-}(0)+D_{21}^{*}g_{+}(\ell)}{g_{+}(0)+C_{12}^{*}g_{-}(0)+C_{22}^{*}g_{+}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}^{*}&\mathbb{O}\\
C_{12}^{*}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}\binom{g_{-}(0)}{g_{+}(0)}+\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&D_{21}^{*}\\
\mathbb{O}&C_{22}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\binom{g_{-}(\ell)}{g_{+}(\ell)}

(8.13)

\displaystyle=C_{*}g(0)+D_{*}g(\ell),

(8.14)

where
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
are given by (
8.9
). This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

(i)
If either
n_{-}=0
or
n_{-}=n
, the canonical form (
8.9
) can be simplified. Indeed, if
n_{-}=n
, in which case
n_{+}=0
and
D=S=I_{n}
, then
C_{*}=I_{n}
and
D_{*}=C^{*}
. And if
n_{-}=0
, in which case
n_{+}=n
and
C=-S=I_{n}
, then
C_{*}=D^{*}
and
D_{*}=I_{n}
.

(ii)
Let
D_{11}
be invertible. It is clear that
C_{*}
is also invertible. Set
X:=C_{*}^{-1}D_{*}
. It is interesting to mention that the matrices
C_{*}^{-1}
and
D_{*}
serve the triangular factorization of the matrix
X
.

Next, we indicate an explicit form of eigenvectors of the operators
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
and
L_{U}^{*}(0)
corresponding to their simple eigenvalues. To this end let

\Phi_{*}(x,\mu):=\Phi_{Q_{*}}(x,\mu)=:\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{*1}(x,\mu)&\ldots&\Phi_{*n}(x,\mu)\end{pmatrix}=:\left(\varphi_{*jk}(x,\mu)\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}

be a fundamental matrix solution of the system
\mathcal{L}(Q_{*})Y=\mu Y
that corresponds to the adjoint operator
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
according to Lemma
8.1
. Recall, that
\Phi_{0}(x,\mu)
is a fundamental matrix solution of
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{*}Y=\mathcal{L}_{0}Y=\mu Y
. Set

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\det(A_{*}(\mu)),\qquad A_{*}(\mu):=C_{*}+D_{*}\Phi_{*}(\ell,\mu)=:(a_{*jk}(\mu))_{j,k=1}^{n},

(8.15)

\displaystyle\Delta_{0*}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\det(A_{0*}(\mu)),\qquad A_{0*}(\mu):=C_{*}+D_{*}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\mu)=:(a_{*jk}^{0}(\mu))_{j,k=1}^{n}.

(8.16)

Recall that
A_{*}^{a}(\mu)
(
A_{0*}^{a}(\mu)
) denotes the adjugate matrix of
A_{*}(\mu)
(resp.
A_{0*}(\mu)
). Set

A^{a}_{*}(\mu)=:(A_{*jk}(\mu))_{j,k=1}^{n},\qquad A^{a}_{0*}(\mu)=:(A_{*jk}^{0}(\mu))_{j,k=1}^{n}.

Lemma
5.1
implies that for any simple eigenvalue
\mu
of

L_{U}^{*}(Q)=L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})
(simple zero of
\Delta_{*}(\cdot)
) there exists
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
such that the vector function

y_{*}(x,\mu):=Y_{*q}(x,\mu):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{*kq}(\mu)\Phi_{*k}(x,\mu)

(8.17)

(see (
5.18
)) is a non-trivial eigenvector of
L_{U}^{*}(Q)=L_{U_{*}}(Q_{*})
corresponding to its simple eigenvalue
\mu
. Similarly,

y_{0*}(x,\mu_{0}):=Y_{*q}^{0}(x,\mu_{0}):=\col\left(A_{*1q}^{0}(\mu_{0})e^{i\mu_{0}\rho_{1}(x)},\ldots,A_{*nq}^{0}(\mu_{0})e^{i\mu_{0}\rho_{n}(x)}\right)

(8.18)

is a non-trivial eigenvector of
L_{0,U}^{*}(=L_{0,U_{*}})
corresponding to its simple eigenvalue
\mu_{0}
.

Let us formulate Lemma
5.3
for adjoint operator
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\lambda
be an algebraically simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. Then
\overline{\lambda}
is an algebraically simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
. Let
g
be any eigenvector of
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
in
\mathfrak{H}
corresponding to
\overline{\lambda}
. Then, there exists
q=q_{\overline{\lambda}}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
\gamma_{*q}\in\mathbb{C}
, such that

g(\cdot)=\gamma_{*q}Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})=\gamma_{*q}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{*kq}(\overline{\lambda})\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda}),\qquad|\gamma_{*q}|=\|g\|_{\mathfrak{H}}/\|Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(8.19)

Morever, this is valid for any
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, for which
Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\not\equiv 0
.

8.2
Key identity for scalar product of eigenvectors

The following result plays a crucial role in proving the Riesz basis property. To state it we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\det(\Phi_{0}(\ell,-\lambda))=\exp(-i(b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n})\lambda).

(8.20)

\displaystyle b_{k}^{-}

\displaystyle:=\min\{b_{k},0\}\leqslant 0,\qquad b_{k}^{+}:=\max\{b_{k},0\}\geqslant 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(8.21)

Proposition 8.5
.

Let diagonal matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfies relaxed conditions (
5.9
) and let regular boundary conditions (
5.2
) be of canonical form (
5.57
). Further, let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
be a simple zero of
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
and let
y_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
and
y_{0*}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})
be the corresponding eigenvectors
given by (
5.18
) and (
8.18
), respectively.
Then the following identity holds

\left(y_{0}(\cdot,\lambda),y_{0*}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=-i\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\exp\left(ib_{q}^{-}\lambda\right)\cdot A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda),

(8.22)

where
A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\neq 0
.

Proof.

First we note that combining formulas for the eigenvectors (
5.18
) and (
8.18
) of the operators
L_{0,U}
and
L_{0,U_{*}}
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\lambda
and
\overline{\lambda}
, respectively, yields

\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{0}^{\ell}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(x)}\overline{A_{*kq}^{0}(\overline{\lambda})e^{i\overline{\lambda}\rho_{k}(x)}}|\beta_{k}(x)|dx\\
=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\overline{A_{*kq}^{0}(\overline{\lambda})}\cdot|b_{k}|\cdot A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda),\qquad p,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(8.23)

Our purpose is to transform identity (
8.23
) into (
8.22
).
To this end we divide the proof in three steps.

(i)
First we consider the case
n_{-}=0
, i.e.
D=S=I_{n}
.
Let us start with some general facts valid without the assumption
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=0
. Since
D=I_{n}
, one has
A_{0}(\lambda)=C+\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)
and

A_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag\left(ib_{1}e^{i\lambda b_{1}},\ldots,ib_{n}e^{i\lambda b_{n}}\right),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(8.24)

Further, Lemma
8.2
implies that
C_{*}=I_{n}
and
D_{*}=C^{*}
. Since
[\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})]^{-1}=\Phi_{0}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})=[\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*}
then

A_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})=I_{n}+C^{*}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})=(\Phi_{0}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})+C^{*})\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\\
=[C+\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})=[A_{0}(\lambda)]^{*}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda}).

(8.25)

It follows from (
8.20
) that

\det(\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda}))=\exp(i(b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n})\overline{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}.

(8.26)

Combining this identity with (
8.25
) and taking into account properties (
2.18
) and (
2.20
) of adjugate matrices and identity
[\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})]^{-1}=\Phi_{0}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})
, imply

\Delta_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\cdot\Delta_{0}(\lambda)},\qquad A^{a}_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}\cdot\Phi_{0}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})\cdot[A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)]^{*}.

(8.27)

Taking adjoint in (
8.27
) we arrive at

[A^{a}_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})]^{*}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda),\quad\text{i.e.}\quad\overline{A_{*kj}^{0}(\overline{\lambda})}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{k}}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda),

(8.28)

for
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Note that since
n_{-}=0
then
b_{k}>0
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Taking this into account and inserting (
8.28
) into (
8.23
), we get

\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{qk}^{0}(\lambda)b_{k}e^{i\lambda b_{k}}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda),\qquad p,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(8.29)

Since
\rank(A_{0}^{a}(\lambda))=1
, one has

0=\det\begin{pmatrix}A_{qk}^{0}(\lambda)&A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda)\\
A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)&A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=A_{qk}^{0}(\lambda)A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)-A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda),\qquad k,p,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(8.30)

Inserting this identity into (
8.29
), we derive

\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{k}e^{i\lambda b_{k}}A_{qk}^{0}(\lambda)A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)\\
=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{k}e^{i\lambda b_{k}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda).

(8.31)

Inserting (
8.24
) in formula (
5.66
) implies

\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\tr\left(A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)A_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)\right)=i\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda)b_{k}e^{i\lambda b_{k}}=i\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{k}e^{i\lambda b_{k}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda).

(8.32)

Inserting (
8.32
) into (
8.31
) and taking into account that
b_{q}^{-}=0
when
n_{-}=0
, we arrive at (
8.22
), which finishes the proof in the case
n_{-}=0
.

(ii)
In this step we consider the general case
n_{-},n_{+}>0
. Our first goal is to obtain formula for
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)
similar to (
8.32
). Since
n_{-}>0
, the matrix
A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)
has more complicated structure. Applying Jacobi formula to it directly will lead to more complicated unusable formula for
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)
. Hence, we need to do some preparations first. With account of definition (
8.21
), let us decompose the diagonal matrix function
\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag\left(e^{i\lambda b_{1}},\ldots,e^{i\lambda b_{n}}\right)
with respect to the orthogonal decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{+}}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)

\displaystyle=:\diag(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda),\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)),\qquad\Phi^{0}_{\pm}(\ell,\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},

(8.33)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\diag(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda),I_{n_{+}})=\diag\bigl(e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{-}}\bigr)_{k=1}^{n},

(8.34)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\diag(I_{n_{-}},\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda))=\diag\bigl(e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}\bigr)_{k=1}^{n}.

(8.35)

Using formulas (
8.34
)–(
8.35
) and (canonical) triangular block-matrix representation (
5.57
) of matrices
C
and
D
from the boundary conditions
Uy=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+D\Phi_{0}(\ell,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&C_{12}\\
0&C_{22}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}&0\\
D_{21}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)&0\\
0&\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}+D_{11}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)&C_{12}\\
D_{21}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)&C_{22}+\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda)+D_{11}&C_{12}\\
D_{21}&C_{22}+\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)&0\\
0&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\widehat{A}_{0}(\lambda)\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda),\qquad

(8.36)

where

\widehat{A}_{0}(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda)+D_{11}&C_{12}\\
D_{21}&C_{22}+\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=A_{0}(\lambda)\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda).

(8.37)

It is clear that the derivative of the matrix
\widehat{A}_{0}(\lambda)
is diagonal, which makes it more suitable for applying Jacobi’s formula. To this end we note that formulas (
8.34
), (
8.35
) and (
8.21
) imply

\diag\left(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda),\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\right)=[\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)]^{-1}\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)=\diag\bigl(e^{-i\lambda b_{k}^{-}}\cdot e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}\bigr)_{k=1}^{n}=\diag\left(e^{i\lambda|b_{k}|}\right)_{k=1}^{n},

(8.38)

where we used trivial identity
|b_{k}|=-b_{k}^{-}+b_{k}^{+}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
. Let also

e_{-}(\lambda):=\det(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda))=e^{-i\lambda b_{-}},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\qquad b_{-}:=b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n_{-}}<0.

(8.39)

Definition (
8.37
), properties (
2.19
) and (
2.17
) of adjugate matrices, and relation (
8.38
) imply that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{0}(\lambda):=\det(\widehat{A}_{0}(\lambda))=\det(A_{0}(\lambda))\det(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda))=e_{-}(\lambda)\Delta_{0}(\lambda),

(8.40)

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{0}^{a}(\lambda)=[A_{0}(\lambda)\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda)]^{a}=e_{-}(\lambda)\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)=\bigl(e_{-}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{j}^{-}}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)\bigr)_{j,k=1}^{n},

(8.41)

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{d}{d\lambda}\diag\left(\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\lambda),\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)\right)=\diag\left(i|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda|b_{k}|}\right)_{k=1}^{n}.

(8.42)

Now Jacobi’s formula (
2.21
) applies to
\widehat{\Delta}_{0}(\cdot)=e_{-}(\cdot){\Delta}_{0}(\cdot)
and gives with account of (
8.41
) and (
8.42
)

e_{-}(\lambda)\Delta^{\prime}_{0}(\lambda)+e_{-}^{\prime}(\lambda)\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\widehat{\Delta}^{\prime}_{0}(\lambda)=\tr\bigl(\widehat{A}_{0}^{a}(\lambda)\widehat{A}_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)\bigr)\\
=\sum_{k=1}^{n}e_{-}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{-}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda)\cdot i|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda|b_{k}|}=ie_{-}(\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{n}|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda).

(8.43)

Given that
e_{-}^{\prime}(\lambda)=-ib_{-}e_{-}(\lambda)
, it follows from (
8.43
) that

\Delta^{\prime}_{0}(\lambda)=ib_{-}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(8.44)

Now let us find an explicit form of the adjugate matrix
A^{a}_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})
.
Since

[\Phi^{0}_{\pm}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})]^{-1}=\Phi^{0}_{\pm}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})=[\Phi^{0}_{\pm}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*},

(8.45)

then

\displaystyle A_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})

\displaystyle=C_{*}+D_{*}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}^{*}&0\\
C_{12}^{*}&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&D_{21}^{*}\\
0&C_{22}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})&0\\
0&\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}D_{11}^{*}+\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})&D_{21}^{*}\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\\
C_{12}^{*}&I_{n_{+}}+C_{22}^{*}\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})&0\\
0&I_{n_{+}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}[\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*}D_{11}^{*}+I_{n_{-}}&[\Phi^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*}D_{21}^{*}\\
C_{12}^{*}&[\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda)]^{*}+C_{22}^{*}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{-}}&0\\
0&\Phi^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\cdot[A_{0}(\lambda)]^{*}\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda}).

Further, it follows from (
8.34
), (
8.35
) and (
8.20
) that

\det\bigl(\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\bigr)=\det(\Phi_{0}(\ell,\overline{\lambda}))=\exp(i(b_{1}+\ldots+b_{n})\overline{\lambda})=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}.

(8.46)

Hence due to (
8.26
) and properties (
2.18
) and (
2.20
) of adjugate matrix, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\det(A_{0*}(\overline{\lambda}))=\det\left(\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\overline{\lambda})\right)\cdot\overline{\det(A_{0}(\lambda))}=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\cdot\Delta_{0}(\lambda)},

\displaystyle A^{a}_{0*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{E}(\lambda)}\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda})\cdot[A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)]^{*}\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,-\overline{\lambda}).

(8.47)

Taking adjoint we get

[A^{a}_{0*}(\overline{\lambda})]^{*}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{-}(\ell,\lambda)\cdot A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)\cdot\widehat{\Phi}^{0}_{+}(\ell,\lambda).

(8.48)

Inserting (
8.34
)–(
8.35
) into (
8.48
) we arrive at the key identity

\overline{A_{*kj}^{0}(\overline{\lambda})}=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{j}^{-}}e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda),\qquad j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(8.49)

Inserting (
8.49
) into (
8.23
) and taking into account formula (
8.44
) for
\Delta^{\prime}_{0}(\cdot)
, identity (
8.30
), one deduces

\displaystyle\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{q}^{-}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{qk}^{0}(\lambda)|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{q}^{-}}A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{n}|b_{k}|e^{i\lambda b_{k}^{+}}A_{kk}^{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-i\mathcal{E}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda b_{q}^{-}}A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\left(\Delta^{\prime}_{0}(\lambda)-ib_{-}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)\right),\quad p,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(8.50)

Since
\lambda
is a simple root of
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
then (
8.50
) implies (
8.22
).

The case
n_{+}=0
can be treated similarly.

(iii)
Assuming
\lambda
to be a simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{0,U}
, let us show that
A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\neq 0
whenever,
Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\not\equiv 0
and
Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\not\equiv 0
. According to Lemma
5.1
, we have

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=0,\qquad\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0,\qquad\rank(A_{0}^{a}(\lambda))=1.

(8.51)

Since
A_{0}^{a}(\lambda)
is non-zero matrix, there exist
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
such that
A_{kj}^{0}(\lambda)\neq 0
. Formula (
8.22
) implies that

\left(Y_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*k}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=-i\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\exp\left(ib_{k}^{-}\lambda\right)\cdot A_{kj}^{0}(\lambda)\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0,

(8.52)

since
A_{jk}^{0}(\lambda)\neq 0
and
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)\neq 0
.

Now let
p,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be such that
Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)\not\equiv 0
and
Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\not\equiv 0
. Since
\rank(A_{0}^{a}(\lambda))=1
, then
Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
is proportional to
Y_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
. Formulas (
8.28
) and (
8.47
) imply that
\rank(A_{*0}^{a}(\overline{\lambda}))=1
. Hence
Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})
is proportional to
Y_{*k}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})
. Therefore,

Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)=\alpha_{pj}Y_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),\qquad Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})=\alpha_{*qk}Y_{*k}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda}),\qquad\alpha_{pj},\alpha_{*qk}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}.

(8.53)

Combining formulas (
8.22
), (
8.52
) and (
8.53
) we arrive at

\displaystyle-i\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\exp\left(ib_{q}^{-}\lambda\right)\cdot A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\left(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}=\alpha_{pj}\overline{\alpha_{*qk}}\left(Y_{j}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda),Y_{*k}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}\neq 0,

(8.54)

which implies
A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda)\neq 0
and finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

The proof remains valid for non-regular boundary conditions provided that
J_{P}(C,D)\neq 0
for some
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Indeed, we can use alternative canonical form of boundary conditions (
5.2
) outlined in Remark
5.12
.

9
Uniform minimality and Riesz basis property

9.1
Uniform minimality

Here we apply results of the previous section to show an important property of the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
: uniform minimality.

Definition 9.1
.

A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
in a Banach space
X
is called minimal if

\varphi_{m}\notin\Span\{\varphi_{k}:k\neq m\}\qquad\text{for any}\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.1)

It is called uniformly minimal if

\inf_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\dist}\bigl(\|\varphi_{m}\|^{-1}\varphi_{m},{\Span}\{\varphi_{k}:\ k\neq m\}\bigr)>0.

(9.2)

The following statement is well known.

Lemma 9.2
.

(i) The sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset X
is minimal if and only if there exists a biorthogonal system
\{\varphi_{*m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset X^{*}
, i.e. a system satisfying
(f_{k},\varphi_{*m})=\delta_{km}
,
k,m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii) The sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset X
is uniformly minimal if and only if it admits a biorthogonal system
\{\varphi_{*m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset X^{*}
satisfying

\sup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\|\varphi_{m}\|\cdot\|\varphi_{*m}\|<\infty.

(9.3)

Now we are ready to prove uniform minimality of the strictly regular BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
). If
Q=0
, this can be done under relaxed conditions on the matrix function
B(\cdot)
.

Proposition 9.3
.

Let self-adjoint invertible diagonal matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfy relaxed conditions (
5.9
). Namely,
B\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and every its entry does not change sign on
[0,\ell]
. Let boundary conditions of the boundary value problem (
5.8
), (
5.2
) be strictly regular. Then any system of root vectors of the operator
L_{0,U}=L_{U}(0)
is uniformly minimal in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

Since boundary conditions are strictly regular then
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
has a countable asymptotically separated sequence of zeros
\Lambda_{0}:=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
(counting multiplicity), satisfying (
5.61
)
with certain
\delta,m_{0}>0
and lying in the strip
\Pi_{h}
. Clearly,
\Lambda_{0}
is a sequence of eigenvalues of
L_{0,U}
(counting multiplicity) and
\overline{\Lambda_{0}}:=\{\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of eigenvalues of
L_{0,U}^{*}
(counting multiplicity). Moreover, each eigenvalue of
L_{0,U}
has finite multiplicity.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be any system of root vectors of the operator
L_{0,U}
. Since operator
L_{0,U}
has discrete spectrum and each its eigenvalue has finite multiplicity, we can choose system of root vectors
\mathcal{F}_{*0}:=\{f_{*m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{0,U}^{*}
in such a way that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*0}
are biorthogonal systems. i.e.
(f_{j}^{0},f_{*k}^{0})=\delta_{jk}
,
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. This, implies minimality of the system
\mathcal{F}_{0}
. In accordance with Lemma
9.2
(ii), to prove uniform minimality it is sufficient to show that

\sup_{|m|>m_{0}}\|f_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\cdot\|f_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}<\infty,

(9.4)

where
m_{0}
is from Definition
5.13
(iii) of strict regularity.

Let
|m|>m_{0}
. Then
\lambda_{m}^{0}
and
\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}}
are algebraically simple eigenvalues of the operators
L_{0,U}
and
L_{0,U}^{*}
, respectively. Moreover, by Lemma
5.15
, there exist indices
p=p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
q=q_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, and a constant
C_{2}>0
such that estimate (
5.65
) holds, i.e.
|A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\geqslant C_{2}
,
|m|>m_{0}
. Emphasize, that although
p
and
q
depend on
m
, the constant
C_{2}
in the above estimate does not. Starting with this
A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})(\not=0)
we define the vector functions

\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}(\cdot):=Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}(\cdot):=Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}})

(9.5)

by formulas (
5.19
) and (
8.18
), respectively.

Since boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular then by Lemmas
5.11
and
8.2
we can assume boundary conditions of the operators
L_{0,U}
and
L_{0,U}^{*}=L_{0,U_{*}}
to be of canonical forms (
5.57
) and (
8.9
), respectively. Hence Proposition
8.5
can be used. Besides, in accordance with Lemma
5.14
,
|\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|\geqslant C_{0}
,
|m|>m_{0}
, where a constant
C_{0}>0
is independent on
m
. Combining Proposition
8.5
with this estimate and the above estimate on
|A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})|
yields

\displaystyle\bigl|{\bigl(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\bigr)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|{\bigl(Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0}),Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}})\bigr)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|{\mathcal{E}(\lambda_{m}^{0})\exp\left(ib_{q}^{-}\lambda_{m}^{0}\right)}\bigr|\cdot\bigl|{A_{qp}^{0}(\lambda_{m}^{0})\cdot\Delta_{0}^{\prime}(\lambda_{m}^{0})}\bigr|

\displaystyle\geqslant\bigl|{\mathcal{E}(\lambda_{m}^{0})\exp\left(ib_{q}^{-}\lambda_{m}^{0}\right)}\bigr|C_{2}C_{0}\geqslant C_{3},

(9.6)

with some
C_{3}>0
that does not depend on
m
. Inequality (
9.6
) in particular implies that both vector functions
\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}
and
\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}
are non-zero.

Applying Lemma
5.4
to the “adjoint” BVP (
8.1
)–(
8.2
), we arrive at the estimate

\left\|{Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda})}\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant M_{*h},\qquad\lambda\in\Pi_{h},

(9.7)

with some different constant
M_{*h}
. Inclusion
\lambda_{m}^{0}\in\Pi_{h}
and estimates (
5.29
) and (
9.7
) imply that

\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant M_{h},\qquad\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant M_{*h},\qquad|m|>m_{0}.

(9.8)

Since
\lambda_{m}^{0}
is algebraically simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{0,U}
, and
\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\neq 0
, Lemmas
5.3
and
8.4
ensure that

f_{m}^{0}(\cdot)=\gamma_{m}^{0}\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}(\cdot)=\gamma_{m}^{0}Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0}),\qquad f_{*m}^{0}(\cdot)=\gamma_{*m}^{0}\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}(\cdot)=\gamma_{*m}^{0}Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}}),\qquad|m|>m_{1},

(9.9)

with some
\gamma_{m}^{0},\gamma_{*m}^{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Since vector systems
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*0}
are biorthogonal, it follows that

1=(f_{m}^{0},f_{*m}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}=\gamma_{m}^{0}\overline{\gamma_{*m}^{0}}\cdot(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(9.10)

Combining estimates (
9.8
) with estimate (
9.6
), relations (
9.9
) and equality (
9.10
) yields

\|f_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\cdot\|f_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=|\gamma_{m}^{0}\gamma_{*m}^{0}|\cdot\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\cdot\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant\frac{M_{h}M_{*h}}{\bigl|{(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|}\leqslant\frac{M_{h}M_{*h}}{C_{3}}=:C_{4},\qquad|m|>m_{0}.

(9.11)

Since
C_{4}
does not depend on
m
, estimate (
9.11
) implies estimate (
9.4
), which completes the proof.
∎

In general case we need more strict conditions on the matrix function
B(\cdot)

Theorem 9.4
.

Let matrix function
B(\cdot)
given by (
4.3
) satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
), let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and let BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) be strictly regular according to Definition
6.13
. Then any system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
is uniformly minimal in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

As usual, applying gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
we can reduce general case to the case of
Q
satisfying “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
). Since operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
are similar this transform preserves uniform minimality as explained in Remark
6.12
. Hence, without loss of generality we can assume that original
Q
satisfies “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
).

Due to assumptions on matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
,
Proposition
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
imply that characteristic determinant
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
has a countable sequence of eigenvalues
\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
(counting multiplicity) with asymptotic behavior (
6.31
) and lying in the strip
\Pi_{h}
(we can assume that both
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Lambda
lie in the same strip
\Pi_{h}
by increasing
h
if needed). By Lemma
5.1
,
\Lambda
is a sequence of eigenvalues of
L_{U}(Q)
(counting multiplicity) and
\overline{\Lambda}:=\{\overline{\lambda_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of eigenvalues of
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
(counting multiplicity). Moreover, each eigenvalue of
L_{U}(Q)
has finite multiplicity.
Combining asymptotic formula (
6.31
) and separation condition (
5.61
) on
\Lambda_{0}
imply that for some
m_{1}\geqslant m_{0}
we have

|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}|>\delta,\qquad j\neq k,\quad|j|,|k|>m_{1}.

(9.12)

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\{f_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be any system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
. Since operator
L_{U}(Q)
has discrete spectrum and each its eigenvalue has finite multiplicity,
we can choose system of root vectors
\mathcal{F}_{*}:=\{f_{*m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
in such a way that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*}
are biorthogonal systems. i.e.
(f_{j},f_{*k})=\delta_{jk}
,
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. This, implies minimality of the system
\mathcal{F}
. In accordance with Lemma
9.2
(ii), to prove uniform minimality it is sufficient to show that

\sup_{|m|>m_{2}}\|f_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\cdot\|f_{*m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}<\infty,

(9.13)

for some
m_{2}\geqslant m_{1}
that we will choose later. Here
m_{1}\geqslant m_{0}
is from the separation condition (
9.12
) on
\lambda_{m}
, while
m_{0}
is from separation condition (
5.61
) on
\lambda_{m}^{0}
.

Let
|m|>m_{1}\geqslant m_{0}
. Then
\lambda_{m}
and
\overline{\lambda_{m}}
are algebraically simple eigenvalues of the operators
L_{U}(Q)
and
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
, respectively. Let
p=p_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
and
q=q_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be indices chosen in the part (i) of the proof for which estimate (
9.6
) holds. Following part (i) we define similar vector functions

\widetilde{f}_{m}(\cdot):=Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{f}_{*m}(\cdot):=Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}})

(9.14)

by formulas (
5.18
) and (
8.17
), respectively. Set

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m}(\cdot):=\widetilde{f}_{m}(\cdot)-\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}(\cdot)=Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m})-Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0}),\qquad|m|\geqslant m_{1},

(9.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{*m}(\cdot):=\widetilde{f}_{*m}(\cdot)-\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}(\cdot)=Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}})-Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0}}),\qquad|m|\geqslant m_{1}.

(9.16)

It follows from Schwarz inequality that

\bigl|{\bigl(\widetilde{f}_{m},\widetilde{f}_{*m}\bigr)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|\geqslant\bigl|{\bigl(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\bigr)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|-\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\widetilde{F}_{*m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}-\|\widetilde{F}_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}-\|\widetilde{F}_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\widetilde{F}_{*m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(9.17)

It follows from Theorem
6.9
, that
\|\widetilde{F}_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\to 0
and
\|\widetilde{F}_{*m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\to 0
as
|m|\to\infty
.
Combining this observation with estimates (
9.17
), (
9.6
), and estimates (
5.29
) and (
9.7
) on
\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}^{0})\|_{\mathfrak{H}}
and
\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=\|Y_{*q}^{0}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}^{0})}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\bigl|{\bigl(\widetilde{f}_{m},\widetilde{f}_{*m}\bigr)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|\geqslant C_{3}/2,\qquad|m|>m_{2},

(9.18)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant 2M_{h},\qquad\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant 2M_{h},\qquad|m|>m_{2},

(9.19)

for some
m_{2}\geqslant m_{1}
. Inequality (
9.18
) implies that both vector functions
\widetilde{f}_{m}
and
\widetilde{f}_{*m}
are non-zero.
Since
\lambda_{m}
is algebraically simple eigenvalue of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
, and
\widetilde{f}_{m}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{f}_{*m}\neq 0
, Lemmas
5.3
and
8.4
ensure that

f_{m}(\cdot)=\gamma_{m}\widetilde{f}_{m}(\cdot)=\gamma_{m}Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda_{m}),\qquad f_{*m}(\cdot)=\gamma_{*m}\widetilde{f}_{*m}(\cdot)=\gamma_{*m}Y_{*q}(\cdot,\overline{\lambda_{m}}),\qquad|m|>m_{2},

(9.20)

with some
\gamma_{m},\gamma_{*m}\neq 0
. The proof of estimate (
9.13
) is finished the same way as in part (i) by using estimates (
9.18
)–(
9.19
).
∎

9.2
Riesz basis property

First, let us recall some definitions.

Definition 9.5
.

(i)
A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vectors in
\mathfrak{H}
is called a
Riesz basis
if it admits a representation
\varphi_{m}=Te_{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\{e_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathfrak{H}
and
T:\mathfrak{H}\to\mathfrak{H}
is a bounded operator with a bounded inverse.

(ii)
A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vectors in
\mathfrak{H}
is called
Besselian
if

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|{(f,\varphi_{m})_{\mathfrak{H}}}\right|^{2}<\infty,\qquad f\in\mathfrak{H}.

(9.21)

Remark 9.6
.

In accordance with closed graph theorem, inequality (
9.21
) is equivalent to

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|{(f,\varphi_{m})_{\mathfrak{H}}}\right|^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}\|f\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},\qquad f\in\mathfrak{H},

(9.22)

where
\gamma>0
does not depend on
f
. Putting in (
9.22
)
f=\varphi_{m}
implies

\|\varphi_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant\gamma
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Our investigation of the Riesz basis property of the system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
heavily relies on the following well-known Bari criterion.

Theorem 9.7
.

[
18
, Theorem VI.2.1]

Let
\mathfrak{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. The vectors system
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathfrak{H}
forms a Riesz basis in
\mathfrak{H}
if and only if it is complete and Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
, and there exists a biorthogonal system
\{\varphi_{*m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
that is also complete and Besselian.

First, we establish a result that implies Besselian property for eigenvectors of the unperturbed operator
L_{U}(0)
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let entries of
B(\cdot)
satisfy condition (
4.5
). Let
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an incompressible sequence lying in the strip
\Pi_{h}
(see Definition
5.7
). Then the following statements hold:

(i)
For any
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
the sequence
\{\Phi^{0}_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
4.20
) is Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
.

(ii)
For any
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
the sequence
\{Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
5.19
) is Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed and let

f=\col(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\in\mathfrak{H},\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad f_{j}\in L^{2}_{|\beta_{j}|}[0,\ell],\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

It follows from definition (
4.20
) of
\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
that

(f,\Phi^{0}_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m}))_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{k}(x)\cdot\overline{e^{i\mu_{m}\rho_{k}(x)}}|\beta_{k}(x)|\,dx.

(9.23)

Condition (
4.5
) implies conditions (
4.18
) on
\rho_{k}
and the inverse function
\rho_{k}^{-1}
.
Let
x_{k}:=|\rho_{k}^{-1}|\in\Lip[0,\ell_{k}]
, where
\ell_{k}:=|b_{k}|
. Since
\beta_{k}
and
\rho_{k}
do not change sign on the segment
[0,\ell]
, then
x_{k}
is the inverse function of
|\rho_{k}|
.
Consider the function
f_{k}\circ x_{k}
defined on
[0,\ell_{k}]
. Since
1/\beta_{k}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell]
, it follows from definition of
L^{2}_{|\beta_{k}|}[0,\ell]
that
f_{k}\in L^{2}[0,\ell]
.
Since
x_{k}\in\Lip[0,\ell]
and strictly monotonous, and
f_{k}\in L^{2}[0,\ell]
it follows that
f_{k}\circ x_{k}\in L^{2}[0,\ell_{k}]\subset L^{1}[0,\ell_{k}]
.
Finally, recall that
s_{k}=\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))=\sign(\rho_{k}(\cdot))=\const
.

Taking observations of the previous paragraph into account and making a change of variable
x=x_{k}(u)
in (
9.23
) (and so
u=|\rho_{k}(x)|=s_{k}\rho_{k}(x)
and
du=|\beta_{k}(x)|dx
), we get

(f,\Phi^{0}_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m}))_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{0}^{\ell_{k}}f_{k}(x_{k}(u))\cdot\overline{e^{is_{k}\mu_{m}u}}\,du.

(9.24)

Since sequence
\{s_{k}\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is incompressible, then
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
implies that the sequence
\{e^{is_{k}\mu_{m}u}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is Besselian in
L^{2}[0,\ell_{k}]
(see also the proof of Lemma 6.4 in
[
33
]
). With account of this observation, it follows from (
9.24
) and inclusion
f_{k}\circ x_{k}\in L^{2}[0,\ell_{k}]
, that the sequence
\{\Phi^{0}_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
.

(ii)
Combining formula (
5.19
) with
Schwarz inequality and estimate (
5.28
) (applicable since
\mu_{m}\in\Pi_{h}
), we arrive at

\bigl|{\left(f,Y_{p}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|^{2}=\Bigl|\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}^{0}(\mu_{m})\cdot\left(f,\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}\Bigr|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}|A_{kp}^{0}(\mu_{m})|^{2}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigl|{\left(f,\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|^{2}\\
\leqslant nM_{h}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigl|{\left(f,\Phi_{k}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|^{2},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},\quad p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(9.25)

Estimate (
9.25
) and part (i) of Lemma now finish the proof.
∎

Now, using integral representation (
4.23
) we can extend the previous result to vector functions
\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\lambda)
and
Y_{p}(\cdot,\lambda)
, which will imply Besselian property for eigenvectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
.

Proposition 9.9
.

Let matrix functions
B(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
satisfy conditions (
4.3
)–(
4.8
). Let
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an incompressible sequence lying in the strip
\Pi_{h}
.
Then the following statements hold:

(i)
For any
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
the sequence
\{\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
4.19
) is Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
.

(ii)
For any
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
the sequence
\{Y_{p}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in (
5.18
) is Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed and let

f=\col(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\in\mathfrak{H},\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad f_{j}\in L^{2}_{|\beta_{j}|}[0,\ell],\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

By Proposition
4.2
, representation (
4.23
) holds with
p=k
, where vector kernel
R_{q}^{[k]}
is defined via (
4.31
)–(
4.32
) and satisfies inclusion (
4.22
).
Moreover, by definition of signature matrix
S
we have,

\beta_{q}(x)=s_{q}|\beta_{q}(x)|,\qquad q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

Taking these observations and formula (
9.24
) into account, we get by changing order of integration

\displaystyle(f,\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\lambda))_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=(f,\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda))_{\mathfrak{H}}+\sum_{j,q=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{j}(x)\left(\int_{0}^{x}\overline{R_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}}\beta_{q}(t)\,dt\right)\cdot|\beta_{j}(x)|\,dx

\displaystyle=(f,\Phi_{k}^{0}(x,\lambda))_{\mathfrak{H}}+\sum_{j,q=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\ell}\left(\int_{t}^{\ell}f_{j}(x)\overline{R_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)}|\beta_{j}(x)|\,dx\right)\overline{e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}}\beta_{q}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{k}(t)\cdot\overline{e^{i\lambda\rho_{k}(t)}}|\beta_{k}(t)|\,dx+\sum_{j,q=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\ell}f_{kjq}(t)\cdot\overline{e^{i\lambda\rho_{q}(t)}}|\beta_{q}(t)|\,dt,

(9.26)

where

f_{kjq}(t):=\int_{t}^{\ell}\overline{R_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)}\cdot s_{q}f_{j}(x)|\beta_{j}(x)|\,dx,\qquad t\in[0,\ell],\quad j,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(9.27)

Since
\beta_{j}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell]
and
f_{j}\in L^{2}_{|\beta_{j}|}[0,\ell]
, it follows that

f_{j},\ s_{q}f_{j}|\beta_{j}|\in L^{2}[0,\ell],\qquad j,q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(9.28)

Consider an operator
\mathcal{R}_{kjq}
generated by the kernel
R_{jq}^{[k]}
by formula (
2.13
) in
L^{2}[0,\ell]
. Lemma
2.3
and inclusion (
4.22
) imply that operator
\mathcal{R}_{kjq}
is bounded in
L^{2}[0,\ell]
(and even Volterra operator). It is clear that operator
\mathcal{R}_{kjq}
and its adjoint operator
\mathcal{R}_{kjq}^{*}
are of the form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{kjq}f)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}R_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)f(t)dt,\qquad f\in L^{2}[0,\ell],

(9.29)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{kjq}^{*}g)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\ell}\overline{R_{jq}^{[k]}(x,t)}g(x)dx,\qquad g\in L^{2}[0,\ell].

(9.30)

Formulas (
9.27
), (
9.30
), inclusion (
9.28
) and boundedness of the operator
\mathcal{R}_{kjq}^{*}
imply that
{f_{kjq}\in L^{2}[0,\ell]}
. Recall, that
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
is fixed. Let us set

F_{q}:=F_{kq}\col(\delta_{1q},\ldots,\delta_{nq}),\qquad F_{kq}:=\delta_{kq}f_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{kjq}\in L^{2}[0,\ell],\qquad q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(9.31)

Since
\beta_{q}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell]
,
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, it is clear that
F_{q}\in\mathfrak{H}
. With account of notation (
9.31
) and formula (
9.23
), we get by setting
\lambda=\mu_{m}
in (
9.26
),

(f,\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m}))_{\mathfrak{H}}=\sum_{q=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\ell}F_{kq}(t)\cdot\overline{e^{i\mu_{m}\rho_{q}(t)}}|\beta_{q}(t)|\,dt=\sum_{q=1}^{n}(F_{q},\Phi_{q}(\cdot,\mu_{m}))_{\mathfrak{H}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.32)

Besselian property of the sequence
\{\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is now implied by Besselian property of the sequences
\{\Phi_{q}^{0}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, established in Lemma
9.8
(i), and inclusion
F_{q}\in\mathfrak{H}
,
q\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, which finishes the proof.

(ii)
Let
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
be fixed. It follows from representation (
6.2
) for
A_{kp}(\lambda)
, inclusion
g_{kp}\in L^{1}[b_{-},b_{+}]
and estimate (
5.28
) on
A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)
that for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
the following estimate holds,

|A_{kp}(\lambda)|\leqslant|A_{kp}^{0}(\lambda)|+\left|{\int_{b_{-}}^{b_{+}}g_{kp}(u)e^{i\lambda u}\,du}\right|\leqslant M_{h}+\|g_{kp}\|_{1}(e^{-b_{-}h}+e^{b_{+}h}):=M_{h,kp},\qquad|\lambda|\leqslant\Pi_{h}.

(9.33)

As in the proof of Lemma
9.8
(ii), combining formula (
5.18
) with
Schwarz inequality and estimate (
9.33
) (applicable since
\mu_{m}\in\Pi_{h}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
), we arrive at

\bigl|{\left(f,Y_{p}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|^{2}=\Bigl|\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kp}(\mu_{m})\cdot\left(f,\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}\Bigr|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{h,kp}^{2}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigl|{\left(f,\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\mu_{m})\right)_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|^{2},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.34)

Estimate (
9.34
) and part (i) of Proposition finish the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.10
.

Let entries of matrix function
B(\cdot)
satisfy condition (
4.5
), i.e.

\beta_{k},\ 1/\beta_{k}\ \in L^{\infty}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}),\qquad\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))\equiv\const,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(9.35)

and let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be strictly regular. Then any normalized system of root vectors of the operator
L_{0,U}=L_{U}(0)
forms a Riesz basis in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
9.3
, operator
L_{0,U}
has countable asymptotically separated sequence of eigenvalues
\Lambda_{0}:=\{\lambda_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be some normalized system of root vectors of the operator
L_{0,U}
, where
f_{m}^{0}
is a root vector corresponding to
\lambda_{m}^{0}
,
\|f_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. As in the proof of Proposition
9.3
, we can choose system of root vectors
\mathcal{F}_{*0}:=\{f_{*m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{0,U}^{*}
in such a way that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*0}
are biorthogonal systems.

Since boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular then by Lemma
8.1
boundary conditions (
8.2
) of
L_{0,U}^{*}
are also regular. Hence Theorem
7.4
(i) ensures the completeness property of both systems
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*0}
.

Let
|m|>m_{0}
, where
m_{0}
is from Definition
5.13
(iii) of strict regularity. Based on the proof of Proposition
9.3
, all of relations (
9.5
)–(
9.10
) hold. Since
\|f_{m}^{0}\|=1
, it is clear from (
9.9
)–(
9.10
) that

|\gamma_{m}^{0}|=\frac{1}{\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}},\qquad|\gamma_{*m}^{0}|=\frac{\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\bigl|{(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}}\bigr|}.

(9.36)

Combining estimates (
9.6
) and (
9.8
) with the Schwartz inequality yields

C_{3}\leqslant|(\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0},\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0})|\leqslant\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|\cdot\|\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\|\leqslant M_{h}\|\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\|\leqslant M_{h}^{2}.

(9.37)

Inserting this estimate into (
9.36
) implies

|\gamma_{m}^{0}|\leqslant C_{5},\qquad|\gamma_{*m}^{0}|\leqslant C_{5},\qquad C_{5}:=M_{h}/C_{3}.

(9.38)

With account of definitions (
9.5
) and assumption (
9.35
), Lemma
9.8
(ii) implies that the sequences
\{\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}
and
\{\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}
are Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
. Since
f_{m}^{0}=\gamma_{m}^{0}\widetilde{f}_{m}^{0}
,
f_{*m}^{0}=\gamma_{*m}^{0}\widetilde{f}_{*m}^{0}
,
|m|>m_{0}
, inequality (
9.38
) implies that the sequences
\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}
and
\{f_{*m}^{0}\}_{|m|>m_{0}}
are also Besselian. And hence so are
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{f_{m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*0}=\{f_{*m}^{0}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Theorem
9.7
now finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 9.11
.

Let matrix function
B(\cdot)=\diag(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
), i.e. for some
n_{-}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{k},1/\beta_{k}\in L^{\infty}[0,\ell],\qquad s_{k}:=\sign(\beta_{k}(\cdot))\equiv\const\neq 0,\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\},

(9.39)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n_{-}}(x)<0<\beta_{n_{-}+1}(x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\beta_{n}(x),\qquad x\in[0,\ell],

(9.40)

and there exists
\theta>0
such that for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n-1}\}

\text{either}\quad\beta_{k}\equiv\beta_{k+1}\quad\text{or}\quad\beta_{k}(x)+\theta<\beta_{k+1}(x),\quad x\in[0,\ell].

(9.41)

Further, let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
and let BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) be strictly regular according to Definition
6.13
. Then any normalized system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
forms a Riesz basis in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
9.4
, applying gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
, we can assumed that
Q
satisfy “zero block diagonality” condition (
4.8
).
Hence, as in the proof of Theorem
9.4
, operator
L_{U}(Q)
has countable asymptotically separated sequence of eigenvalues
\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\{f_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be some normalized system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
, where
f_{m}
is a root vector corresponding to
\lambda_{m}
,
\|f_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. As in the proof of Theorem
9.4
, we can choose system of root vectors
\mathcal{F}_{*}:=\{f_{*m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the operator
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
in such a way that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*}
are biorthogonal systems.

Since boundary conditions (
5.2
) are regular then by Lemma
8.1
boundary conditions (
8.2
) of
L_{U}^{*}(Q)
are also regular. Hence assumptions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
), (
4.8
) and Theorem
7.4
(ii) ensures the completeness property of both systems
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{*}
.

Let
|m|>m_{2}
, where
m_{2}
was chosen in the proof of Theorem
9.4
to satisfy relations (
9.18
)–(
9.19
).
Based on the proof of Theorem
9.4
, all of relations (
9.14
)–(
9.20
) hold. With account of definitions (
9.14
), Proposition
9.9
(ii) implies that the sequences
\{\widetilde{f}_{m}\}_{|m|>m_{2}}
and
\{\widetilde{f}_{*m}\}_{|m|>m_{2}}
are Besselian in
\mathfrak{H}
. From this point the proof is finished the same way as in part (i) by using key estimates (
9.18
)–(
9.19
).
∎

Remark 9.12
.

Let us provide brief history of results on Riesz basis property for BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) with constant
2\times 2
matrix
B(\cdot)\equiv B=\diag(b_{1},b_{2})=B^{*}
.

(i)
The Riesz basis property for
2\times 2
Dirac type operators

L_{U}(Q)
and separated boundary conditions was established earlier than for the operators with general regular boundary conditions. Namely, this property was proved firstly in
[
68
,
69
]
by I. Trooshin and M. Yamamoto for
B=\diag(-1,1)
and
Q\in C^{1}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
. Later, P. Djakov and B. Mityagin in
[
8
]
, and A.G. Baskakov, A.V. Derbushev and A.O. Shcherbakov in
[
6
]
relaxed smoothness assumption on potential to
Q\in L^{2}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
. Independently, in
[
20
]
S. Hassi and L. Oridoroga established this property for Dirac type operator with
B=\diag(b_{1},b_{2})=B^{*}
and
Q\in C^{1}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
.

(ii)
The Bari-Markus property of the Riesz projectors of unperturbed and perturbed
2\times 2
Dirac operators with separated, periodic and antiperiodic boundary conditions was established in
[
50
,
8
]
and reproved by another method in
[
6
]
. In
[
11
]
similar results have been obtained for general regular boundary conditions.

(iii)
The most complete result on the Riesz basis property for
2\times 2
Dirac and Dirac-type systems with
Q\in L^{1}
and strictly regular boundary conditions was obtained independently by different methods and at the same time by A.M. Savchuk and A.A. Shkalikov
[
60
]
and by the authors
[
31
,
33
]
. The case of regular boundary conditions and
Q\in L^{1}
is treated in
[
60
]
for the first time. Other proofs were obtained later in
[
57
]
,
[
58
]
(see also their recent survey
[
59
]
and references therein).

(iv)
Periodic and antiperiodic (necessarily non-strictly regular) BVP for
2\times 2
Dirac equation have attracted certain attention during the last decade. In
[
10
, Theorem 13]
,
[
9
, Theorem 19]
and
[
13
]
, P. Djakov and B. Mityagin established a
criterion
for the system of root vectors to contain a Riesz basis for periodic (resp., antiperiodic)
2\times 2
Dirac operator in terms of the Fourier coefficients of
Q
as well as in terms of periodic (resp., antiperiodic) and Dirichlet spectra. A. Makin
[
35
,
36
]
established Riesz basis property for periodic
2\times 2
Dirac operator under certain explicit algebraic assumptions on a potential matrix. See also recent survey
[
14
]
by P. Djakov and B. Mityagin, survey
[
38
]
by A.S. Makin, and the references therein.

Remark 9.13
.

In this remark we go over known results on Riesz basis property for BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) with constant
n\times n
matrix
B(\cdot)\equiv B=B^{*}
, when
n>2
.

(i)
In
[
51
]
the results of
[
8
]
regarding the Bari-Markus property in
L^{2}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2})
were extended to the case of the Dirichlet BVP for
2m\times 2m
Dirac equation with
Q\in L^{2}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{2m\times 2m})
.

(ii)
To the best of our knowledge the first result on the Riesz basis property for BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) generated by general
n\times n
system (
5.1
) with
B(x)=B=\diag(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\neq B^{*}
and bounded
Q\in L^{\infty}([0,1];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})

was obtained by the authors in
[
32
]
. Treated boundary conditions form rather broad class that covers, in particular, periodic, antiperiodic, and regular separated (not necessarily self-adjoint) boundary conditions.

(iii)
In
[
24
,
25
]
, Bessel and Riesz basis properties on abstract level were established, i.e. the operator
L_{U}(Q)
was studied without explicit boundary conditions.

9.3
Riesz basis property with parentheses

First, let us recall a corresponding definition.

Definition 9.14
.

(i) A sequence of subspaces
\{\mathcal{H}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
is called a
Riesz basis of subspaces
in a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
if there exists a complete sequence of mutually orthogonal subspaces
\{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
and a bounded operator
T
in
\mathcal{H}
with a bounded inverse such that
\mathcal{H}_{m}=T\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii) A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
of vectors in
\mathcal{H}
is called a
Riesz basis with parentheses
if each its finite subsequence is linearly independent, and there exists an increasing sequence
\{m_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{N}
such that
m_{0}=1
and the sequence
\mathcal{H}_{k}:=\Span\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j=m_{k-1}}^{m_{k}-1}
, forms a Riesz basis of subspaces in
\mathcal{H}
. Subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{k}
are called blocks.

In
[
61
]
, A.A. Shkalikov established Riesz basis property with parentheses for BVP for ODE of
n
-th order with coefficients
q_{2},\ldots,q_{n}\in L^{1}
and regular boundary conditions
V(y)=0
. Denote corresponding operator as
\mathcal{L}(V,q)
. The idea was to present the operator
\mathcal{L}(V,q)
as a bounded perturbation of the operator similar to
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{q})
, where boundary conditions
\widetilde{V}(y)=0
are already strictly regular and the operator is known to have a Riesz basis property (without parentheses). Then abstract result of Katsnel’son-Markus-Matsaev (see
[
21
, Theorem 3.1]
and also
[
1
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
47
, Theorem 6.12]
) implies desired Riesz basis property with parentheses for the original operator
\mathcal{L}(V,q)
.

This idea later was used in
[
60
]
to establish Riesz basis property with parentheses for
2\times 2
Dirac operator with regular boundary conditions, i.e. operator
L_{U}(Q)
with
B(\cdot)\equiv\diag(-1,1)
. Later we also used this idea in
[
33
]
, to establish this result for
2\times 2
Dirac type operator, i.e. when
B(\cdot)\equiv\diag(b_{1},b_{2})
,
b_{1}<0<b_{2}
.

In this subsection we use the same idea, and, following
[
33
, Section 7]
, show that the system of root vectors of BVP (
5.1
)–(
5.2
) with regular boundary conditions forms a Riesz basis with parentheses under assumptions (
4.3
)–(
4.7
).

As in
[
33
, Section 7]
we start with certain properties of zeros of exponential polynomials. The
n\times n
case is more difficult and requires more advanced properties. We start with the following simple lemma.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
f\not\equiv 0
and
g\not\equiv 0
be entire functions.

(i)
Let
g
have simple zeros (possibly empty set), i.e.
|g(z)|+|g^{\prime}(z)|>0
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
f+wg
has simple zeros for all but countable number of values of
w\in\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)
Let
f
,
f^{\prime}
,
g
,
g^{\prime}
be sine-type functions with separated (possible empty) sets of zeros. Then for all
w
outside of a certain strip
\Pi_{h}
,
h=h_{f,g}
, function
f+wg
is a sine-type function with separated (possible empty) set of zeros.

Proof.

(i)
If
f
and
g
are proportional, i.e.
f\equiv\alpha g
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, it is clear that
f+wg
has simple zeros for all
w\neq-\alpha
.
Let
f
and
g
be non-proportional. Since
f
and
g
are non-zero entire functions, it follows that each of them has at most countable set of zeros. Hence, ratio
f/g
is a non-constant analytic function with at most countable set of poles. This implies that
(f/g)^{\prime}
is non-zero analytic function with at most countable set of poles, which implies that
fg^{\prime}-f^{\prime}g
is non-zero entire function and has at most countable set of zeros
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m=1}^{N}
,
0\leqslant N\leqslant\infty
(it can be empty as well).

Let
w\in\mathbb{C}
be such that some
z=z_{w}
is a multiple zero of
f+wg
. Then

f(z)+wg(z)=f^{\prime}(z)+wg^{\prime}(z)=0.

This implies that
f(z)g^{\prime}(z)-f^{\prime}(z)g(z)=0
, i.e.
z
is a zero of the entire function
fg^{\prime}-f^{\prime}g
. Hence
z=z_{w}=\mu_{m}
for some
m=m_{w}\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}
. Since function
g
has simple zeros it follows that
|g(z)|+|g^{\prime}(z)|>0
. Hence

\text{either}\quad w=-f(z)/g(z)=-f(\mu_{m})/g(\mu_{m})\quad\text{or}\quad w=-f^{\prime}(z)/g^{\prime}(z)=-f^{\prime}(\mu_{m})/g^{\prime}(\mu_{m}).

This implies that the set of values
w
for which function
f+wg
has multiple zeros is at most countable and finishes the proof.

(ii)
Since
f
is a sine-type function it follows from estimate on
f
from below outside of zeros and estimate on
f^{\prime}
near zeros that
|f(z)|+|f^{\prime}(z)|>\varepsilon
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, for some
\varepsilon>0
and the same is valid for
g
. It also follows that zeros of
f
and
f^{\prime}
combined are separated. I.e. zeros of
ff^{\prime}
and
gg^{\prime}
are separated. These properties are sufficient to show the desired property of function
f+wg
for sufficiently large
|\Im w|
.
As an example, let
f(z)=e^{ibz}g(z)
for some
b>0
. Then

f(z)+wg(z)=(e^{ibz}+w)g(z)
. So we need to find
w
for each arithmetic progression

\left\{\frac{-i\ln w+2\pi m}{b}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

is separated from zeros of
g(\cdot)
. It is clear that this is true for
w
with sufficiently large
|\Im w|
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Let
S
be the set of entire functions with simple zeros, i.e.
f\in S
iff
|f(z)|+|f^{\prime}(z)|>0
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Denote
\mathcal{S}:=S\cup\{0\}
. The following property is a trivial consequence of Lemma
9.15
.

Corollary 9.16
.

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
f+wg\in\mathcal{S}
for all but countable number of values of
w\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

If either
f
or
g
is zero the statement is trivial and
f+wg\in\mathcal{S}
for all
w\in\mathbb{C}
. Otherwise the statement follows from Lemma
9.15
.
∎

Lemma 9.17
.

Let
(f_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}
, be a sequence of
2^{n}
functions from the class
\mathcal{S}
indexed with diagonal idempotent matrices of size
n
(see (
5.39
) for the definition of the class
\mathcal{P}_{n}
). Then there exists a diagonal matrix
W=\diag(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
with non-zero entries such that

\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\det(PW)\cdot f_{P}\in\mathcal{S}.

(9.42)

For example, for
n=2
, this function looks like
f_{\diag(0,0)}+w_{1}f_{\diag(1,0)}+w_{2}f_{\diag(0,1)}+w_{1}w_{2}f_{\diag(1,1)}
.

Proof.

Let us prove this via induction by
n
. For
n=1
the statement trivially follows from Corollary
9.16
. Assume the statement is valid for
n=m-1\in\mathbb{N}
and consider
n=m
, i.e. we have a sequence
(f_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{m}}
of
2^{m}
functions from the class
\mathcal{S}
. Set
\widetilde{W}:=\diag(w_{1},\ldots,w_{m-1})
and for each
\widetilde{P}=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m-1})\in\mathcal{P}_{m-1}
denote,

\widetilde{P}_{0}:=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m-1},0)\in\mathcal{P}_{m},\qquad\widetilde{P}_{1}:=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m-1},1)\in\mathcal{P}_{m}.

We can transform the sum in (
9.42
) the following way,

\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{m}}\det(PW)\cdot f_{P}=\sum_{\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{m-1}}\det(\widetilde{P}\cdot\widetilde{W})\cdot\left(f_{\widetilde{P}_{0}}+w_{m}f_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}\right)

(9.43)

By Corollary
9.16
, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}_{m-1}
the function
f_{\widetilde{P}_{0}}+w_{m}f_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}\in\mathcal{S}
for all but countable number of values of
w_{m}\in\mathbb{C}
. Hence we can choose a single
w_{m}\neq 0
that “serves” all
P
, i.e.
f_{\widetilde{P}_{0}}+w_{m}f_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}\in\mathcal{S}
for all
P\in\mathcal{P}_{m-1}
. Now induction hypothesis applied to the r.h.s. of (
9.43
) implies existence of the desired non-zero
w_{1},\ldots,w_{m-1}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.18
.

Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
given by (
4.17
) are non-zero and let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular. Then there exists
W_{\ell}=\diag(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
with non-zero entries such that the sequence of zeros of entire function

\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot):=\det(C+DW_{\ell}\Phi_{0}(\ell,\cdot))

is separated, where
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda)
is given by (
4.20
).

Proof.

Throughout the proof we will heavily use notations and formulas from Subsection
5.2
:
b_{\pm}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
b_{P}
,
P_{\pm}
,
J_{P}(C,D)
, etc.
First note that

J_{P}(C,DW_{\ell})=\det(PW_{\ell})\cdot J_{P}(C,D),\qquad P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}.

(9.44)

The proof is very similar to the proof of (
6.69
). It follows from (
5.43
) and (
9.44
) that

\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\lambda)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}J_{P}(C,DW_{\ell})e^{i\lambda b_{P}}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\det(PW_{\ell})f_{P}(\lambda),\quad f_{P}(\lambda):=J_{P}(C,D)e^{i\lambda b_{P}},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(9.45)

(i)
Let us consider the case when
b_{k}=m_{k}b_{0}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, for some
b_{0}>0
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Similar to notations
b_{\pm}
and
b_{P}
we denote

m_{\pm}:=b_{\pm}/b_{0},\quad\text{e.g.}\quad m_{-}:=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n_{-}},

and

m_{P}:=b_{P}/b_{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}m_{k}\quad\text{for}\quad P=\diag(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}.

Let
W_{\ell}
be any invertible diagonal matrix. It follows from (
5.43
) and (
9.44
) that

\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\lambda)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\det(PW_{\ell})J_{P}(C,D)(e^{i\lambda b_{0}})^{m_{P}}=(e^{i\lambda b_{0}})^{m_{-}}F_{W_{\ell}}(e^{i\lambda b_{0}}),

(9.46)

where
F_{W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
is some polynomial (since
m_{P}\geqslant m_{-}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, are integers). Since boundary conditions are regular it follows that
J_{P_{\pm}}(C,D)\neq 0
. Hence
F_{W_{\ell}}(0)=J_{P_{-}}(C,D)\neq 0
and
\deg F_{W_{\ell}}=m_{+}-m_{-}=:N
. Therefore
F_{W_{\ell}}
has exactly
N
non-zero roots
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}
(that depend on
W_{\ell}
,
z_{k}=z_{k,W_{\ell}}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}
). It is clear that the sequence of zeros of
\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
is of the form

\left\{\frac{-i\ln z_{k}+2\pi m}{b_{0}}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z},k\in\{1,\ldots,{N}\}}.

Hence
\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
has separated zeros if and only if roots are distinct
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}
. This also means that if
\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
has simple zeros then they are separated.

Going back to (
9.45
) we see that
f_{P}\in\mathcal{S}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Indeed, if
J_{P}(C,D)=0
, then
f_{P}\equiv 0\in\mathcal{S}
. Otherwise
f_{P}
has no zeros (and thus has simple zeros by definition). Thus, Lemma
9.17
implies existence of invertible
W=W_{\ell}=\diag(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
, for which (
9.42
) holds. In view of (
9.45
) this implies that
\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
has simple zeros (since it is non-zero function, due to observations above about polynomial
F_{W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
). Another observation above, implies that
\Delta_{0,W_{\ell}}(\cdot)
has separated zeros and finishes the proof.

(ii)
The proof is similar by using Lemma
9.15
. Namely, this lemma implies variation of Lemma (
9.17
) for sine-type functions with separated zeros. Using this result we can finish the proof by following steps above.
∎

To state the next result we recall that
m_{a}(\mu_{0})
and

m_{g}(\mu_{0})
denote the algebraic and geometric multiplicities of

\mu_{0}
, respectively. Moreover, if
\mu_{0}
is an isolated
eigenvalue, then
m_{a}(\mu_{0})
equals to the dimension of the
Riesz projection.
We need the following known abstract result (see
e.g.
[
60
,
33
]
) that follows from Katsnel’son-Markus-Matsaev
theorem with
p=1
(see
[
21
, Theorem 3.1]
and
also
[
1
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
47
, Theorem
6.12]
).

Proposition 9.19

(Proposition 7.3 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
L
be an operator with compact resolvent in a separable
Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and let
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the
sequence of its distinct eigenvalues. Assume that
m_{a}(\mu_{m})<\infty
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and that
A
has finitely many
associative vectors, i.e. there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m_{a}(\mu_{m})=m_{g}(\mu_{m})
for
|m|>n_{0}.
Further, assume that

|\mu_{m}|\geqslant C|m|,\quad|\Im\mu_{m}|\leqslant\tau,\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z},

(9.47)

for some
C,\tau>0
. Finally, let the system of root vectors
of the operator
L
forms a Riesz basis in
\mathfrak{H}
. Then for any
bounded operator
T
in
\mathfrak{H}
the system of root vectors of the
perturbed operator
A=L+T
forms a Riesz basis with parentheses
in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Finally, we are ready to prove the main result of this subsection concerning Riesz basis property with parentheses.

Theorem 9.20
.

Let matrix function
B(\cdot)
given by (
4.3
) satisfy conditions (
4.5
)–(
4.7
) and let
Q\in L^{1}([0,\ell];\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})
. Let boundary conditions (
5.2
) be regular
Then any normalized system of root vectors of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
forms a Riesz basis with parentheses in
\mathfrak{H}
. Moreover, block sizes are uniformly bounded by
2^{n}-1
and each block consists of root vectors corresponding to the eigenvalues with pairwise close real parts.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.11
(iv), the regularity of boundary conditions is preserved under the gauge transformation used in Lemma
6.11
. Therefore one can assume that
Q
is off-diagonal. Now let us consider a perturbation of the operator
L(Q)
by a constant diagonal potential matrix
Q_{0}=\diag(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Applying Lemma
6.11
again, but this time to the operator
L_{U}(Q+Q_{0})
, and noting that
(Q+Q_{0})_{\diag}=Q_{0}
, we see that the operator
L_{U}(Q+Q_{0})
is similar to the operator
L_{\widetilde{U}}(\widetilde{Q})
with off-diagonal
\widetilde{Q}
and with boundary conditions

\widetilde{U}(y)=Cy(0)+\widetilde{D}y(\ell)=0,\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{D}=D\cdot W_{\ell},

(9.48)

and

W_{\ell}=\diag(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}),\qquad w_{k}=e^{-ib_{k}q_{k}},\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}.

(9.49)

By Proposition
9.18
, we can choose
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that the boundary conditions (
9.48
) are strictly regular. In turn, setting,
q_{k}=ib_{k}^{-1}\ln w_{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}
, provides us with the desired perturbation
Q_{0}
. Namely, solution
W(\cdot)
of the equation (
6.60
) with
Q_{0}
in place of
Q_{\diag}
, satisfies condition
W(\ell)=W_{\ell}
, where
W_{\ell}
is given by (
9.49
).

Therefore, by Theorem
6.6
and Definition
5.13
(iii), the eigenvalues of
L(Q+Q_{0})
are of finite multiplicity, asymptotically simple and separated. In particular, the operator
L(Q+Q_{0})
has only finitely many associated vectors. Moreover, according to Theorem
9.11
the root vectors system of the operator
L(Q+Q_{0})
forms a Riesz basis in
\mathfrak{H}
.

To verify that the operator
L(Q+Q_{0})
satisfies conditions of Proposition
9.19
it suffices to note that inequalities (
9.47
) are implied by Proposition
6.5
(iv). Thus, the operator
L(Q+Q_{0})
meets the conditions of Proposition
9.19
, and hence the root vectors system of the original operator
L(Q)=L(Q+Q_{0})-Q_{0}
forms a Riesz basis with parentheses, since operator
T:y\to Q_{0}y
is clearly bounded in
\mathfrak{H}
.

Further, note that since
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
is exponential polynomial with at most
2^{n}
terms, we can find a number
w>0
such that any rectangle
[x,x+w]\times[-h,h]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, has no more than
2^{n}-1
zeros of
\Delta_{0}(\cdot)
(see
[
7
, pp. 95–97]
where this property of exponential polynomial was proved for
n=2
). The same is valid for
\Delta_{Q}(\cdot)
due to asymptotic formula
\lambda_{m}=\lambda_{m}^{0}+o(1)
as
m\to\infty
. Recall that this formula is valid in the case of any regular boundary conditions. Hence we can separate sequence
\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
(ordered in increasing order of real parts) into blocks

\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m=m_{k}}^{m_{k+1}-1},\quad\text{where}\quad m_{k}<m_{k+1}<m_{k}+2^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\Re\lambda_{m_{k}-1}+\varepsilon\leqslant\Re\lambda_{m_{k}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

with
\varepsilon:=\frac{w}{\max\{2^{n}-2,1\}}
. From the proof of Katsnel’son-Markus-Matsaev theorem with
p=1
(see
[
21
, Theorem 3.1]
and also
[
1
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
47
, Theorem 6.12]
), it follows that each block consists of root vectors corresponding to the eigenvalues with pairwise close real parts, which finishes the proof.
∎

Under certain additional assumptions on boundary conditions we can clarify the sizes of blocks in Theorem
9.20
even more. We restrict ourselves to a special type of boundary conditions including periodic and antiperiodic conditions.

Corollary 9.21
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
9.20
and let
b_{1}<\ldots<b_{n}
. Assume also that boundary conditions (
5.2
) are of the form
Cy(0)-y(\ell)=0
, where
C=\diag(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
is invertible. Then the blocks of Riesz basis decomposition with parentheses stated in
Theorem
9.20
have sizes at most
n
and correspond to eigenvalues of the operator
L_{U}(Q)
with mutually close real parts. In particular, this is true for periodic and antiperiodic boundary conditions.

Proof.

Applying the gauge transform from Lemma
6.11
we can assume that
Q
is off-diagonal matrix,
Q_{11}\equiv\ldots\equiv Q_{nn}\equiv 0
. Since
b_{1}<\ldots<b_{n}
it is clear that the new matrix
D
is of the same diagonal form as
C
. Hence multiplying boundary conditions by
-D^{-1}
from the left we can assume that again
D=-I_{n}
. Let
\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the sequence of eigenvalues of
A:=L_{U}(Q)
counting multiplicity, ordered in such a way that
\Re\lambda_{n}\leqslant\Re\lambda_{n+1}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
Q
is off-diagonal, one derives by combining Theorem
6.6
with Lemma
5.18
(i) that there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a sequence of integers
\{m_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that

m_{k}<m_{k+1}\leqslant m_{k}+n,\quad\Re\lambda_{m_{k}}-\Re\lambda_{m_{k}-1}\geqslant\varepsilon,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.50)

Let
N(t)
be the number of eigenvalues (counting multiplicity)
of the operator
A
belonging to the vertical strip
\{\lambda:|\Re\lambda|\leqslant t\}
. Setting
t_{k}:=2^{-1}\Re(\lambda_{m_{k}-1}+\lambda_{m_{k}})
we obtain from (
9.50
) that there
exists
C>0
such that

\sup_{t>0,t\neq t_{k}}\frac{N(t)-N(t_{k})}{t-t_{k}}<C,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.51)

Let
f_{n}
be the root vector of
A
corresponding to
\lambda_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then it follows from the proof of Theorem 3.1
in
[
21
]
(see also Theorem 1.3 in
[
21
]
) that
under the condition (
9.51
) the subspaces
\mathfrak{H}_{k}:=\Span\{f_{j}\}_{j=m_{k}}^{m_{k+1}-1}
constitute a Riesz basis
of subspaces in
\mathfrak{H}
. To complete the proof it
remains to note that due to (
9.50
) the sizes

\dim\mathfrak{H}_{k}
of blocks
\mathfrak{H}_{k}
do not exceed
n
. Indeed,
\dim\mathfrak{H}_{k}=m_{k+1}-m_{k}\leqslant n
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Remark 9.22
.

(i)
Corollary
9.21
is valid under the following slightly more general assumption,

\forall j,k\in\{1,\ldots,{n}\}:\quad j\neq k\quad\text{and}\quad b_{j}=b_{k}\quad\Rightarrow\quad Q_{jk}\equiv 0.

(9.52)

This is evident from the fact that for such
Q
its block diagonal
Q_{\diag}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}
is actually a regular diagonal matrix,
Q_{\diag}=\diag(Q_{11},\ldots,Q_{nn})
. Hence, the solution
W(\cdot)
of the Cauchy problem (
6.60
) is a diagonal matrix function. This in turn imply, that the new matrix
D=-W(\ell)
after applying gauge transform is of the same diagonal form as
C
, which allows to finish the proof the same way.

(ii)
Result similar to Corollary
9.21
is also valid for boundary conditions of the form (
5.91
) if numbers
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
satisfy condition (
5.90
). We just need to apply Lemma
5.20
(i) instead of Lemma
5.18
(i). In fact, the blocks of Riesz basis decomposition with parentheses stated in Theorem
9.20
have sizes at most
n/2
in this case.

(iii)
More generally, if the sequence
\Lambda_{0}
is a union of
N
arithmetic progressions that lie on the lines parallel to the real axis, then the blocks of Riesz basis decomposition with parentheses stated in Theorem
9.20
have sizes at most
N
.

10
Application to the Timoshenko beam model

10.1
Problem statement and reduction to Dirac type operator
